# Daily Summary: 2025-06-16

### Generalization or Hallucination? Understanding Out-of-Context Reasoning in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10887v1)
- **Authors**: Yixiao Huang, Hanlin Zhu, Tianyu Guo, Jiantao Jiao, Somayeh Sojoudi, Michael I. Jordan, Stuart Russell, Song Mei
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) can acquire new knowledge through fine-tuning, but this process exhibits a puzzling duality: models can generalize remarkably from new facts, yet are also prone to hallucinating incorrect information. However, the reasons for this phenomenon remain poorly understood. In this work, we argue that both behaviors stem from a single mechanism known as out-of-context reasoning (OCR): the ability to deduce implications by associating concepts, even those without a causal link. Our experiments across five prominent LLMs confirm that OCR indeed drives both generalization and hallucination, depending on whether the associated concepts are causally related. To build a rigorous theoretical understanding of this phenomenon, we then formalize OCR as a synthetic factual recall task. We empirically show that a one-layer single-head attention-only transformer with factorized output and value matrices can learn to solve this task, while a model with combined weights cannot, highlighting the crucial role of matrix factorization. Our theoretical analysis shows that the OCR capability can be attributed to the implicit bias of gradient descent, which favors solutions that minimize the nuclear norm of the combined output-value matrix. This mathematical structure explains why the model learns to associate facts and implications with high sample efficiency, regardless of whether the correlation is causal or merely spurious. Ultimately, our work provides a theoretical foundation for understanding the OCR phenomenon, offering a new lens for analyzing and mitigating undesirable behaviors from knowledge injection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates why Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit both impressive generalization and problematic hallucination after fine-tuning on new factual knowledge. The authors propose that both behaviors stem from a single underlying mechanism: out-of-context reasoning (OCR), where the model deduces implications by associating concepts, even without a causal link. They empirically confirm this across five LLMs, showing OCR drives both generalization (when concepts are causally related) and hallucination (when concepts are unrelated). They formalize OCR as a symbolic factual recall task and demonstrate that a one-layer transformer with factorized output and value matrices can solve this task, while a model with combined weights cannot.  A theoretical analysis reveals that gradient descent implicitly favors solutions that minimize the nuclear norm of the combined output-value matrix, explaining how models efficiently learn associations regardless of causality.  The paper concludes by providing a theoretical framework for understanding and potentially mitigating undesirable behaviors during knowledge injection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a compelling, unified explanation for generalization and hallucination in LLMs based on OCR. The connection between these seemingly disparate behaviors is insightful and well-supported by experiments.  Formalizing OCR as a synthetic factual recall task is a valuable contribution, allowing for more rigorous analysis.  The theoretical analysis, particularly the emphasis on matrix factorization and the nuclear norm, provides a novel perspective on the training dynamics and implicit biases of transformers. However, other works have noticed the effect of reparameterization such as [Tarzanagh et al., 2023a, Sheen et al., 2024] and this papers builds on them.

*   **Significance:** Understanding generalization and hallucination is crucial for building reliable and trustworthy LLMs. This work provides a theoretical basis for analyzing and potentially mitigating undesirable behaviors during knowledge injection. The OCR framework offers a new lens for studying LLMs and designing interventions to control their reasoning processes.  The findings on matrix factorization and the implicit bias of gradient descent could inform architectural choices and training strategies for future models. It has impact on both theory and practice of understanding neural networks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement.
    *   Strong empirical support across multiple LLMs.
    *   Rigorous theoretical analysis with formal proofs.
    *   Unified framework connecting generalization and hallucination.
    *   Insightful connection to matrix factorization and implicit bias.
    *   Good exposition and organization.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The theoretical analysis is limited to a simplified one-layer transformer. While it provides valuable insights, its applicability to deeper, more complex architectures is not fully established.
    *   The synthetic dataset, while controlled and useful for analysis, may not fully capture the nuances of real-world knowledge and reasoning.
    *   The proposed mitigation strategies for hallucination remain speculative and require further investigation.
    * The significance of the OCR effect to real world LLMs in the context of more complex reasoning tasks is not validated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:

    *   Theoretical understanding of transformers and their training dynamics.
    *   Development of more robust and reliable LLMs.
    *   Design of knowledge injection strategies that minimize hallucination.
    *   Analysis and mitigation of biases in LLMs.
    * New line of analysis for model editability techniques.
    * New line of analysis for factual knowledge retrival.

* **Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-executed study with a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM understanding. The unified framework of OCR, supported by empirical evidence and theoretical analysis, is a compelling explanation for both generalization and hallucination. Although the theoretical analysis is limited in scope, the insights it provides are valuable and could inform future research directions. However, its limitation with complex reasoning tasks impacts its impact. Overall the theoretical contribution, experiments, and analysis leads the following:

### The Diffusion Duality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10892v1)
- **Authors**: Subham Sekhar Sahoo, Justin Deschenaux, Aaron Gokaslan, Guanghan Wang, Justin Chiu, Volodymyr Kuleshov
- **Abstract**: Uniform-state discrete diffusion models hold the promise of fast text generation due to their inherent ability to self-correct. However, they are typically outperformed by autoregressive models and masked diffusion models. In this work, we narrow this performance gap by leveraging a key insight: Uniform-state diffusion processes naturally emerge from an underlying Gaussian diffusion. Our method, Duo, transfers powerful techniques from Gaussian diffusion to improve both training and sampling. First, we introduce a curriculum learning strategy guided by the Gaussian process, doubling training speed by reducing variance. Models trained with curriculum learning surpass autoregressive models in zero-shot perplexity on 3 of 7 benchmarks. Second, we present Discrete Consistency Distillation, which adapts consistency distillation from the continuous to the discrete setting. This algorithm unlocks few-step generation in diffusion language models by accelerating sampling by two orders of magnitude. We provide the code and model checkpoints on the project page: http://s-sahoo.github.io/duo
- **Summary**: Here's a summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Duo," a framework that leverages the connection between Uniform-state Discrete Diffusion Models (USDMs) and underlying Gaussian diffusion processes. The core idea is that USDMs can be viewed as emerging from a continuous Gaussian diffusion. This "Diffusion Duality" allows the transfer of techniques from Gaussian diffusion to improve USDMs, which have traditionally lagged behind autoregressive models and masked diffusion models in text generation. Duo incorporates two main techniques: (1) a curriculum learning strategy guided by the Gaussian process, accelerating training by reducing variance, and (2) Discrete Consistency Distillation, adapting consistency distillation from the continuous domain to the discrete setting for faster sampling and few-step generation. Experiments demonstrate improved performance in zero-shot perplexity (surpassing autoregressive models on some benchmarks) and accelerated sampling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of connecting discrete and continuous diffusion models and exploiting this duality is novel. While connections between continuous and discrete domains are often explored, directly applying this to diffusion *language* models and extracting tangible benefits for training and sampling seems to be a fresh take. The specific techniques (curriculum learning guided by Gaussian process, discrete consistency distillation) are adaptations of existing methods, but their application in this context appears innovative.
*   **Significance:**
    *   **Improved Performance:** The paper reports improvements in zero-shot perplexity and sampling speed, which addresses a key performance gap for USDMs. Surpassing autoregressive models on some benchmarks is a significant achievement. The ability to perform few-step generation is particularly promising, making USDMs more practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Theoretical Contribution:** Establishing the "Diffusion Duality" offers a potentially valuable framework for future research in discrete diffusion models. It opens up a design space for USDMs by enabling the transfer of techniques from the well-studied Gaussian diffusion domain.
    *   **Practical Tools:** The authors provide code and model checkpoints, facilitating adoption and further research by the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The "Diffusion Duality" is grounded in mathematical analysis.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results support the claims of improved performance and faster sampling.
    *   **Well-Written and Structured:** The paper is generally easy to follow and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus is primarily on USDMs. While this allows for in-depth analysis, it might not be directly applicable to other discrete diffusion models.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** While the results are significant, they are still incremental compared to the state-of-the-art in general language modeling. The paper narrows the gap but doesn't completely close it.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Techniques:** The techniques used (curriculum learning, consistency distillation) are adaptations rather than entirely new inventions. The novelty lies in the way they are adapted and applied.
    *   **Limited Ablation studies:** While the ablation studies presented in the paper address key components of Duo, further analyses to understand the impact of individual hyperparameter settings would strengthen the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in several ways:
    *   **Inspiration for New Discrete Diffusion Models:** The "Diffusion Duality" concept could inspire new designs for discrete diffusion models that leverage connections to continuous processes.
    *   **Development of More Efficient Training Techniques:** The curriculum learning strategy could lead to more efficient training methods for USDMs and related models.
    *   **Faster Sampling Algorithms:** The Discrete Consistency Distillation algorithm could pave the way for faster sampling techniques in diffusion language models.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper presents a novel and well-supported framework for improving USDMs. The "Diffusion Duality" offers a valuable theoretical perspective, and the empirical results demonstrate tangible benefits in terms of performance and speed. However, the improvements are somewhat incremental, and the techniques are based on adaptations of existing methods. Therefore, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

### GenPlanX. Generation of Plans and Execution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10897v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Borrajo, Giuseppe Canonaco, Tomás de la Rosa, Alfredo Garrachón, Sriram Gopalakrishnan, Simerjot Kaur, Marianela Morales, Sunandita Patra, Alberto Pozanco, Keshav Ramani, Charese Smiley, Pietro Totis, Manuela Veloso
- **Abstract**: Classical AI Planning techniques generate sequences of actions for complex tasks. However, they lack the ability to understand planning tasks when provided using natural language. The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has introduced novel capabilities in human-computer interaction. In the context of planning tasks, LLMs have shown to be particularly good in interpreting human intents among other uses. This paper introduces GenPlanX that integrates LLMs for natural language-based description of planning tasks, with a classical AI planning engine, alongside an execution and monitoring framework. We demonstrate the efficacy of GenPlanX in assisting users with office-related tasks, highlighting its potential to streamline workflows and enhance productivity through seamless human-AI collaboration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GENPLANX, a system that integrates Large Language Models (LLMs) with classical AI planning techniques for automating office-related tasks.  GENPLANX receives natural language requests, uses an LLM to interpret them and generate a structured problem description (objects, initial state, and goals) in JSON format. This JSON is then compiled into PDDL, a standard AI planning language, which is used by a classical AI planner to generate a plan (sequence of actions). The system then executes the plan, monitors its progress, and replans if necessary.  The paper details the architecture of GENPLANX, focusing on each component, including entity extraction, prompt generation, the LLM interaction, compilation to PDDL, planning, and execution.  Finally, the paper presents several examples demonstrating GENPLANX's ability to solve tasks such as data manipulation, presentation generation, and integrating with external data sources. The authors also compare their approach to directly prompting LLMs for plan generation, showing the advantages of integrating LLMs with classical planning for ensuring valid and potentially optimal plans.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of LLMs with classical planning is not entirely novel, as the paper itself acknowledges several related works. However, GENPLANX presents a specific architecture and implementation tailored to office-related tasks, with a particular focus on seamlessly integrating existing AI tools and allowing the integration of new tools by adding actions in the domain model. The specific combination of components, the emphasis on entity extraction for organizational context, and the end-to-end execution and monitoring framework provide a certain degree of originality. What is new is the implementation itself, specifically in the office setting.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating a practical application of LLMs and AI planning to automate common office tasks. The architecture is designed to be extensible and adaptable, suggesting that GENPLANX could potentially streamline workflows and enhance productivity in real-world settings. The demonstration of advantages over purely LLM-based planning adds to the significance by highlighting robustness.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **End-to-end system:** The paper presents a complete system, from natural language input to plan execution and monitoring.
    *   **Practical application:** The focus on office-related tasks makes the system directly relevant to real-world needs.
    *   **Extensibility:**  The modular architecture and the ability to integrate new tools through PDDL definitions are a strength.
    *   **Clear explanations:** The paper provides detailed explanations of each component and the overall architecture.
    *   **Demonstrations:** The examples illustrate the capabilities of GENPLANX and demonstrate its effectiveness in solving different types of tasks.
    * **Rigorous domain models and explainable AI:** The explicit formal models used in classical AI make the whole process inspectable and explainable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited evaluation:**  The evaluation primarily consists of illustrative examples. A more comprehensive evaluation, including benchmarks, user studies, and comparisons to other existing systems (e.g., commercial RPA tools), would strengthen the paper. There could be more detail put into the analysis of the performance of GENPLANX in terms of time.
    *   **Scalability:** The complexity and cost of maintaining a comprehensive domain model with potentially hundreds of actions in the future needs to be considered.
    *   **LLM limitations:** While the paper emphasizes the advantages over purely LLM-based planning, it doesn't fully address the inherent limitations of LLMs, such as potential biases or hallucinations, which could impact the initial problem formulation. In the future, the authors may need to consider tools that validate such problem formalization.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    The paper could influence the development of similar systems for automating office tasks and other domains. The approach of combining LLMs with classical planning offers a promising direction for creating more robust and reliable AI solutions. The system will open a new avenue for research with integrated planning with LLMs with high explainability.

**Justification for Score:**

GENPLANX presents a solid and potentially valuable contribution by combining LLMs and classical planning for a practical purpose. While the core idea isn't entirely novel, the specific architecture, the focus on office tasks, the design for extensibility, and the demonstration of advantages over directly prompting LLMs justify a decent rating. However, the limited evaluation and potential LLM limitations prevent it from scoring higher.

### Beyond Gold Standards: Epistemic Ensemble of LLM Judges for Formal Mathematical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10903v1)
- **Authors**: Lan Zhang, Marco Valentino, Andre Freitas
- **Abstract**: Autoformalization plays a crucial role in formal mathematical reasoning by enabling the automatic translation of natural language statements into formal languages. While recent advances using large language models (LLMs) have shown promising results, methods for automatically evaluating autoformalization remain underexplored. As one moves to more complex domains (e.g., advanced mathematics), human evaluation requires significant time and domain expertise, especially as the complexity of the underlying statements and background knowledge increases. LLM-as-a-judge presents a promising approach for automating such evaluation. However, existing methods typically employ coarse-grained and generic evaluation criteria, which limit their effectiveness for advanced formal mathematical reasoning, where quality hinges on nuanced, multi-granular dimensions. In this work, we take a step toward addressing this gap by introducing a systematic, automatic method to evaluate autoformalization tasks. The proposed method is based on an epistemically and formally grounded ensemble (EFG) of LLM judges, defined on criteria encompassing logical preservation (LP), mathematical consistency (MC), formal validity (FV), and formal quality (FQ), resulting in a transparent assessment that accounts for different contributing factors. We validate the proposed framework to serve as a proxy for autoformalization assessment within the domain of formal mathematics. Overall, our experiments demonstrate that the EFG ensemble of LLM judges is a suitable emerging proxy for evaluation, more strongly correlating with human assessments than a coarse-grained model, especially when assessing formal qualities. These findings suggest that LLM-as-judges, especially when guided by a well-defined set of atomic properties, could offer a scalable, interpretable, and reliable support for evaluating formal mathematical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Gold Standards: Epistemic Ensemble of LLM Judges for Formal Mathematical Reasoning":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenge of automatically evaluating autoformalization, the task of translating natural language mathematical statements into formal languages. Recognizing the limitations of existing coarse-grained evaluation methods and the scalability issues of human evaluation (especially in complex mathematical domains), the authors propose a systematic method based on an epistemically and formally grounded ensemble (EFG) of LLM judges.  The EFG is defined on criteria encompassing logical preservation (LP), mathematical consistency (MC), formal validity (FV), and formal quality (FQ).  The method breaks down autoformalization into atomic properties and assesses them with specialized LLM judges, combining the results into a comprehensive evaluation score. The paper validates this framework by comparing it to human evaluations on Isabelle/HOL and Lean4 datasets, demonstrating that the EFG ensemble correlates better with human assessments than a simple, coarse-grained LLM judge, particularly in assessing formal qualities. Experiments indicate that smaller LLMs with EFG grounding can outperform larger LLMs employing direct judgement, particularly on logical preservation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the systematic and epistemically grounded approach to decompose and automate autoformalization evaluation. Prior work often relies on simple reference-based metrics (BLEU, etc.) or direct human assessment, lacking the nuance and interpretability provided by the EFG.  The breakdown into logical preservation, mathematical consistency, formal validity, and formal quality is a reasonable and useful taxonomy. However, the idea of using LLMs as judges, while a growing trend, isn't completely novel, but is well applied to a narrow application with a clear theoretical footing.

* **Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential to scale and improve the development of autoformalization systems. By providing a more automated, granular, and interpretable evaluation framework, the paper paves the way for more efficient and effective training and benchmarking of these systems. The finding that smaller, EFG-guided LLMs can outperform larger ones has practical implications for resource efficiency.  It also hints to the potential of specialized LLMs over one-size-fits-all LLMs.
The work addresses a long-standing bottleneck in formal reasoning: evaluation. A major contribution is not merely automation of evaluation, but decomposing it into interpretable components.

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the need for better autoformalization evaluation.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The EFG framework is well-defined and grounded in epistemic principles. The atomic properties, though not necessarily exhaustive, provide a good starting point.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides solid experimental evidence, comparing the EFG-based evaluation with human judgments and reference-based metrics. Datasets used are relevant and experiments well-designed.
    *   **Insightful Results:** The results highlight the benefits of fine-grained evaluation and the potential for smaller LLMs to excel in this specific task.
    *   **Interpretability:** The framework's decomposition into atomic properties makes the evaluation process more transparent and interpretable, providing insights into the strengths and weaknesses of autoformalization models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the EFG provides a valuable framework, it's still limited to the chosen properties and the specific LLMs used. A more thorough exploration of different LLMs and OAPs could strengthen the findings. The prompt engineering process is briefly mentioned, but could be explored at length.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance hinges on the quality and reliability of LLM judgments.  Bias in LLMs is a known issue, and while the authors address it to some extent, further investigation into potential biases and mitigation strategies is warranted.  Are biases towards specific formalisms?
    *   **Linear Evaluation Model:** Using a linear combination to aggregate atomic property scores is a simplification.  More complex aggregation methods (e.g., non-linear models, hierarchical structures) could potentially improve the overall evaluation accuracy. The weighting scheme is not well-justified, and relies heavily on hyperparameter tuning on human assessments, which might not generalize.
    *   **Limited Domain:** Focus on Isabelle/HOL and Lean4, while suitable for initial work, limits external validity. More diverse formal languages could strengthen the argument.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in autoformalization and LLM-based reasoning. It provides a blueprint for creating more sophisticated and interpretable evaluation frameworks. Other downstream task will take inspiration from the work for the LLM-as-a-judge methodology. The analysis on strengths and weaknesses of different LLMs can spur greater progress in the field.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of autoformalization evaluation. The EFG framework offers a valuable improvement over existing methods, addressing the limitations of both human evaluation and simple reference-based metrics. The empirical validation supports the effectiveness of the approach, and the results provide insights into the use of LLMs for this specific task. While there are limitations in scope and reliance on LLMs, the paper's systematic approach and promising results justify a score of 7, indicating a solid and impactful contribution that is likely to stimulate further research in the area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Probably Approximately Correct Labels
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10908v1)
- **Authors**: Emmanuel J. Candès, Andrew Ilyas, Tijana Zrnic
- **Abstract**: Obtaining high-quality labeled datasets is often costly, requiring either extensive human annotation or expensive experiments. We propose a method that supplements such "expert" labels with AI predictions from pre-trained models to construct labeled datasets more cost-effectively. Our approach results in probably approximately correct labels: with high probability, the overall labeling error is small. This solution enables rigorous yet efficient dataset curation using modern AI models. We demonstrate the benefits of the methodology through text annotation with large language models, image labeling with pre-trained vision models, and protein folding analysis with AlphaFold.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Probably Approximately Correct Labels":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method called "Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) labeling" to reduce the cost of creating high-quality labeled datasets.  The core idea is to combine inexpensive, potentially noisy, AI-predicted labels with a smaller set of expensive, expert-provided labels. The method leverages uncertainty scores associated with AI predictions to strategically select data points for expert labeling. The aim is to create a dataset where the overall error rate is provably small (within a specified tolerance), with a high probability. The authors present algorithms for both a single AI model and multiple AI model scenarios, including ways to calibrate uncertainty scores and route data points to the most reliable AI source.  They demonstrate the method's effectiveness across various tasks: text annotation, image labeling, and protein folding analysis, showing significant savings in labeling cost while maintaining accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual components of the paper (active learning, uncertainty estimation, leveraging AI models for annotation) are not entirely novel, the *combination* of these ideas into a *rigorous and provable* framework for dataset curation *is* innovative. The focus on PAC guarantees in a dataset creation context is a fresh perspective. The approach of providing guarantees on the labeling *itself* rather than the performance of a downstream model is a worthwhile differentiator compared to more traditional active learning settings. The uncertainty calibration methods are standard, but their specific application in the context of PAC labeling is valuable. The introduction of the multi-model labeling (router) with simultaneous uncertainty calibration makes use of modern AI models and improves performance.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the growing importance of high-quality data for training reliable AI systems, alongside the increasing cost of human annotation. A method that demonstrably reduces annotation costs *while guaranteeing data quality* has broad implications. The work addresses a practical bottleneck in machine learning pipelines and offers a rigorous way to integrate AI into data curation. The real-world experiments, demonstrating cost savings across diverse data modalities, strengthens the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Provable Guarantees:** The PAC framework provides a level of confidence in the resulting dataset's quality, which is crucial for high-stakes applications.
    *   **Practical Applicability:**  The method is readily applicable using existing pre-trained AI models and standard uncertainty estimation techniques.
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness:** Demonstrated savings in labeling costs across different tasks.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** Evaluation across a variety of data types and models strengthens claims.
    *   **Addresses a Crucial Problem:** The work targets a key bottleneck in ML deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Assumptions about Uncertainty:**  The method heavily relies on the quality of uncertainty scores. If AI models produce poorly calibrated uncertainties, the cost savings could be less significant or guarantees less reliable. Although, the authors do touch on methods for recalibrating uncertainty.

    *   **Complexity:** Algorithm 1 requires valid individual confidence bounds, which may be difficult to estimate.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** Since the data is fixed, any inherent biases in the pre-trained models could propagate into the PAC-labeled dataset, and this isn't explicitly addressed.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The experimental setting does not test the cost sensitive routing in practice, just estimates. The experimental setting also assumes access to gold standard labels for evaluation and algorithm tuning, which may not be possible in many real-world scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence how datasets are curated in the future, particularly in areas where high data quality and cost-effectiveness are paramount. The PAC framework could become a standard approach for incorporating AI into the labeling process. The router can be thought of as a framework for "meta-labeling," where the labels are ultimately provided by humans, but guided by an AI system. This idea could be applied to other labeling tasks where it's difficult to directly label the data, but easier to label at a meta-level.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a well-motivated and technically sound approach to a critical problem in machine learning. The novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques and the focus on PAC guarantees. While there are weaknesses related to uncertainty score quality and the assumption of data being fixed, the overall contribution is significant and could have a considerable impact on the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### NoLoCo: No-all-reduce Low Communication Training Method for Large Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10911v1)
- **Authors**: Jari Kolehmainen, Nikolay Blagoev, John Donaghy, Oğuzhan Ersoy, Christopher Nies
- **Abstract**: Training large language models is generally done via optimization methods on clusters containing tens of thousands of accelerators, communicating over a high-bandwidth interconnect. Scaling up these clusters is expensive and can become impractical, imposing limits on the size of models that can be trained. Several recent studies have proposed training methods that are less communication intensive, avoiding the need for a highly connected compute cluster. These state-of-the-art low communication training methods still employ a synchronization step for model parameters, which, when performed over all model replicas, can become costly on a low-bandwidth network. In this work, we propose a novel optimization method, NoLoCo, that does not explicitly synchronize all model parameters during training and, as a result, does not require any collective communication. NoLoCo implicitly synchronizes model weights via a novel variant of the Nesterov momentum optimizer by partially averaging model weights with a randomly selected other one. We provide both a theoretical convergence analysis for our proposed optimizer as well as empirical results from language model training. We benchmark NoLoCo on a wide range of accelerator counts and model sizes, between 125M to 6.8B parameters. Our method requires significantly less communication overhead than fully sharded data parallel training or even widely used low communication training method, DiLoCo. The synchronization step itself is estimated to be one magnitude faster than the all-reduce used in DiLoCo for few hundred accelerators training over the internet. We also do not have any global blocking communication that reduces accelerator idling time. Compared to DiLoCo, we also observe up to $4\%$ faster convergence rate with wide range of model sizes and accelerator counts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "NoLoCo: No-all-reduce Low Communication Training Method for Large Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NoLoCo, a novel optimization method designed for training large language models on clusters with low-bandwidth interconnects.  Unlike existing low-communication methods like DiLoCo, NoLoCo eliminates the explicit synchronization of model parameters, removing the need for any collective communication. This is achieved through a variant of the Nesterov momentum optimizer that partially averages model weights with a randomly selected peer. The authors provide both theoretical convergence analysis and empirical results from language model training, benchmarking NoLoCo against fully sharded data parallel training and DiLoCo across varying accelerator counts and model sizes. Results suggest that NoLoCo requires significantly less communication overhead than existing approaches and exhibits a comparable or slightly faster convergence rate. They also experiment with dynamic pipeline routing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of completely eliminating all-reduce operations by relying on a pairwise averaging mechanism within a modified Nesterov framework represents a clear, if somewhat incremental, advance. While related work explored reduced communication, NoLoCo's "no communication" promise is distinctly novel. The dynamic pipeline routing is less novel, as variations of this exist.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of NoLoCo could be significant, especially if it enables large model training on more accessible (e.g., cloud-based) or geographically distributed infrastructure with limited inter-node bandwidth.  Reducing communication bottlenecks is a crucial challenge in scaling up deep learning. The empirical results, while showing modest gains over DiLoCo, suggest that the theoretical advantages translate into practical improvements. The theoretical contribution of the proof regarding convergence with momentum is significant, adding to the theoretical foundations of distributed training methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement.
    *   A relatively simple and elegant solution that avoids all-reduce.
    *   Solid theoretical analysis of convergence.
    *   Extensive empirical evaluation across various model sizes and accelerator counts.
    *   Comparison against relevant baseline methods (FSDP, DiLoCo).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The empirical gains over DiLoCo are somewhat modest (up to 4% faster convergence). It's not revolutionary.
    *   The dependence on specific hyperparameters and sensitivity to them can limits general applicability. While they use hyperparameters from other works to allow for repeatability, it would be helpful to note if new hyperparameters were found for this model.
    *   The experiments are confined to language modeling. Wider validation across diverse tasks would strengthen the claims.
    *   The assumption of identical distributions of slow weights for the convergence analysis is questionable. While justified based on random assignment, it might not perfectly hold in practice and affects the theoretical guarantees.
    *   The analysis of dynamic pipeline routing is not particularly deep, and could have been explored more thoroughly.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   NoLoCo could spur further research into communication-free or communication-minimized distributed training methods.
    *   The pairwise averaging approach might be adapted or combined with other techniques to further improve scalability.
    *   The theoretical convergence analysis could be extended to analyze other momentum-based optimization methods in distributed settings.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

NoLoCo's novelty lies in its complete elimination of collective communication, a distinct departure from prior low-communication strategies. The theoretical analysis, specifically convergence proofs, bolster its claims. It is important to emphasize that the impact is incremental and somewhat dependent on hyperparameter settings. It has a potential benefit in broadening the accessibility of large-scale training and has a reasonable impact.

### Breaking Bad Molecules: Are MLLMs Ready for Structure-Level Molecular Detoxification?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10912v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Lin, Ziyang Gong, Cong Wang, Yonglin Tian, Tengchao Zhang, Xue Yang, Gen Luo, Fei-Yue Wang
- **Abstract**: Toxicity remains a leading cause of early-stage drug development failure. Despite advances in molecular design and property prediction, the task of molecular toxicity repair - generating structurally valid molecular alternatives with reduced toxicity - has not yet been systematically defined or benchmarked. To fill this gap, we introduce ToxiMol, the first benchmark task for general-purpose Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) focused on molecular toxicity repair. We construct a standardized dataset covering 11 primary tasks and 560 representative toxic molecules spanning diverse mechanisms and granularities. We design a prompt annotation pipeline with mechanism-aware and task-adaptive capabilities, informed by expert toxicological knowledge. In parallel, we propose an automated evaluation framework, ToxiEval, which integrates toxicity endpoint prediction, synthetic accessibility, drug-likeness, and structural similarity into a high-throughput evaluation chain for repair success. We systematically assess nearly 30 mainstream general-purpose MLLMs and design multiple ablation studies to analyze key factors such as evaluation criteria, candidate diversity, and failure attribution. Experimental results show that although current MLLMs still face significant challenges on this task, they begin to demonstrate promising capabilities in toxicity understanding, semantic constraint adherence, and structure-aware molecule editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ToxiMol, a new benchmark task for evaluating the ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to perform molecular toxicity repair. This task involves generating structurally valid, less toxic molecular alternatives given a toxic molecule's SMILES string, 2D image, and a natural language description of the repair objective. The authors construct a dataset of 560 toxic molecules covering 11 primary tasks, design a mechanism-aware prompt annotation pipeline, and propose an automated evaluation framework called ToxiEval.  ToxiEval integrates toxicity prediction, synthetic accessibility, drug-likeness, and structural similarity to assess repair success. They evaluate nearly 30 MLLMs and conduct ablation studies to analyze key factors affecting performance. The results show that while MLLMs face challenges, they demonstrate promising capabilities in toxicity understanding, semantic constraint adherence, and structure-aware molecule editing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in defining and benchmarking the task of molecular toxicity repair for MLLMs. While previous work explored MLLMs for molecule grounding, toxicity prediction, and structure editing, systematically defining and evaluating *repair* is a valuable contribution. The mechanism-aware prompt engineering and the ToxiEval evaluation framework also represent novel components.

*   **Significance:** Toxicity remains a major bottleneck in drug development, making the ability to computationally repair toxic molecules highly significant. This benchmark provides a standardized way to assess and compare MLLMs' abilities in this crucial area. The dataset is well-constructed and covers a broad range of toxicity mechanisms, and the prompt engineering and evaluation components add considerable value to the field. It could significantly impact the application of AI in early-stage drug development. The paper's exploration of current MLLMs' limitations sets a direction for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and task formalization.
    *   Well-curated dataset with diverse toxicity mechanisms.
    *   Expert-informed prompt annotation pipeline.
    *   Comprehensive, multi-dimensional evaluation framework (ToxiEval).
    *   Extensive evaluation of a wide range of MLLMs.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to analyze key factors.
    *   The open-source release of the dataset and evaluation code promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The overall success rates of current MLLMs are relatively low, indicating substantial room for improvement and thus limiting the *current* practical utility. However, identifying limitations early is part of the goal of benchmarks like these.
    *   The study primarily focuses on small molecule drugs. Extending the benchmark to macromolecular systems would increase its applicability.
    *   While comprehensive, the evaluation metrics are based on *predicted* toxicity endpoints, not experimentally validated data. It would be beneficial to correlate the results with experimental findings in future work, increasing the predictive validity of ToxiEval.
    *   The prompt engineering approach, while novel, could be further enhanced by integrating active learning or reinforcement learning to optimize prompts adaptively.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:
    *   Providing a standard benchmark for molecular toxicity repair, driving further research and development in this area.
    *   Guiding the development of more effective MLLMs for drug discovery.
    *   Facilitating the adoption of AI in early-stage drug development.
    *   Setting a precedent for rigorous evaluation of AI models in scientific domains.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:** While the current performance of MLLMs on the ToxiMol benchmark is limited, the paper's contribution lies in its formulation of the *task itself*, the construction of a high-quality dataset, and the creation of a systematic evaluation framework. These aspects of the work are strong and set a valuable foundation for the field. The paper is not groundbreaking in its methodological innovations for MLLM but applies existing techniques in a novel problem area.

### Foundation Models for Causal Inference via Prior-Data Fitted Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10914v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Ma, Dennis Frauen, Emil Javurek, Stefan Feuerriegel
- **Abstract**: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) have recently been proposed as a promising way to train tabular foundation models. PFNs are transformers that are pre-trained on synthetic data generated from a prespecified prior distribution and that enable Bayesian inference through in-context learning. In this paper, we introduce CausalFM, a comprehensive framework for training PFN-based foundation models in various causal inference settings. First, we formalize the construction of Bayesian priors for causal inference based on structural causal models (SCMs) in a principled way and derive necessary criteria for the validity of such priors. Building on this, we propose a novel family of prior distributions using causality-inspired Bayesian neural networks that enable CausalFM to perform Bayesian causal inference in various settings, including back-door, front-door, and instrumental variable adjustment. Finally, we instantiate CausalFM and explicitly train a foundation model for estimating conditional average treatment effects (CATEs) using back-door adjustment. We show that CausalFM performs competitively for CATE estimation using various synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks. In sum, our framework can be used as a general recipe to train foundation models for various causal inference settings. In contrast to the current state-of-the-art in causal inference, CausalFM offers a novel paradigm with the potential to fundamentally change how practitioners perform causal inference in medicine, economics, and other disciplines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CausalFM, a novel framework for causal inference using prior-data fitted networks (PFNs). CausalFM aims to create foundation models for causal inference that can perform flexible, test-time inference without retraining. The framework formalizes the construction of Bayesian priors for causal inference based on structural causal models (SCMs), providing criteria for the validity of such priors.  The paper proposes a family of prior distributions using causality-inspired Bayesian neural networks that enable CausalFM to perform Bayesian causal inference in various settings, including back-door, front-door, and instrumental variable adjustment. The authors instantiate CausalFM and train a foundation model for estimating conditional average treatment effects (CATEs) using back-door adjustment. The results show CausalFM performs competitively on synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in bringing the foundation model paradigm, specifically prior-data fitted networks (PFNs), to the realm of causal inference in a general and principled way. The use of SCMs to generate priors and to control the distribution of causal queries is a key innovation.  The formalization of SCM-based priors for Bayesian causal inference and the derived criteria for their validity is a strong theoretical contribution. The ability to perform inference in various causal settings (back-door, front-door, IV) within a single framework is a significant advantage.

The paper distinguishes itself from concurrent works [5, 37]. Unlike [5], it does not restrict itself to back-door adjustment.  More importantly, it addresses the identifiability concerns with [37] by advocating for training separate PFNs for different identifiable causal settings. This aligns with established principles in causal inference.

**Significance:**

The potential significance of this work is high. Causal inference is a crucial component in many domains, and the current methods often require task-specific model development and retraining.  A truly flexible, generalizable causal inference foundation model could significantly impact these fields. The Bayesian nature of CausalFM, allowing for uncertainty quantification, is another important aspect.  The potential of automating the selection of appropriate adjustment strategies (back-door, front-door, IV) is appealing, although this claim requires more extensive experimental validation. If the method works in practice, it has the potential to fundamentally change how causal inference is performed.

**Strengths:**

*   **Principled approach:** Formalizing prior construction using SCMs and deriving validity criteria is a strong theoretical contribution.
*   **Generality:** The framework extends beyond back-door adjustment, encompassing front-door and IV settings.
*   **Addressing identifiability:** Tackling the identifiability problem by training separate PFNs for different settings demonstrates a deep understanding of causal inference.
*   **Competitive performance:** Initial results on CATE estimation are promising.
*   **Bayesian Approach:** Provides measures of uncertainty in addition to point estimates.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:** Training PFNs can be computationally intensive. The practical implications of this need to be addressed through scaling.
*   **Scalability to high-dimensional data:** The experiments demonstrate performance on a relatively small number of datasets. In high dimensional settings, CausalFM may suffer from the curse of dimensionality.
*   **Limited experimental validation:**  While the CATE estimation results are competitive, there is a lack of empirical validation in the front-door and IV settings. Claims around automaticaly selecting among strategies need more backing.
*   **Reliance on strong assumptions:** The well-specified prior relies on Assumptions A.1. These require a deep understanding of the underlying causal model, limiting the automation of the selection of adjustment strategies.
*   **Novelty:** Concurrent works [5, 37] are working on similar ideas.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper presents a promising direction for future research in causal inference. It could spur further work on developing more generalizable causal inference methods based on foundation models.  The theoretical framework for SCM-based priors could also be valuable in other areas of Bayesian causal inference. If the challenges of scalability and robustness can be addressed, CausalFM (or its successors) could become a widely used tool for causal analysis.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel approach with significant potential to impact the field of causal inference, justifying a score above the midpoint. The core idea of using foundation models for causal tasks and formalizing prior construction is compelling. The focus on identifiability and the theoretical results strengthen the paper. However, the limited experimental validation beyond CATE estimation and concurrent works temper expectations. The computational intensity of PFN training and the need for strong assumptions need to be addressed to realize its full potential, therefore a score of 7 is appropriate at this stage. Further work is necessary to validate its effectiveness in various causal inference settings, demonstrate scalability, and address the reliance on strong assumptions.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### M4V: Multi-Modal Mamba for Text-to-Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10915v1)
- **Authors**: Jiancheng Huang, Gengwei Zhang, Zequn Jie, Siyu Jiao, Yinlong Qian, Ling Chen, Yunchao Wei, Lin Ma
- **Abstract**: Text-to-video generation has significantly enriched content creation and holds the potential to evolve into powerful world simulators. However, modeling the vast spatiotemporal space remains computationally demanding, particularly when employing Transformers, which incur quadratic complexity in sequence processing and thus limit practical applications. Recent advancements in linear-time sequence modeling, particularly the Mamba architecture, offer a more efficient alternative. Nevertheless, its plain design limits its direct applicability to multi-modal and spatiotemporal video generation tasks. To address these challenges, we introduce M4V, a Multi-Modal Mamba framework for text-to-video generation. Specifically, we propose a multi-modal diffusion Mamba (MM-DiM) block that enables seamless integration of multi-modal information and spatiotemporal modeling through a multi-modal token re-composition design. As a result, the Mamba blocks in M4V reduce FLOPs by 45% compared to the attention-based alternative when generating videos at 768$\times$1280 resolution. Additionally, to mitigate the visual quality degradation in long-context autoregressive generation processes, we introduce a reward learning strategy that further enhances per-frame visual realism. Extensive experiments on text-to-video benchmarks demonstrate M4V's ability to produce high-quality videos while significantly lowering computational costs. Code and models will be publicly available at https://huangjch526.github.io/M4V_project.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces M4V, a Multi-Modal Mamba framework for text-to-video generation. M4V addresses the limitations of applying the Mamba architecture directly to video generation by proposing a multi-modal diffusion Mamba (MM-DiM) block. This block enables seamless integration of multi-modal information and spatiotemporal modeling through a multi-modal token re-composition design. The M4V framework also includes a reward learning strategy to mitigate visual quality degradation in long-context autoregressive generation. The authors demonstrate that M4V reduces computational costs compared to attention-based alternatives while producing high-quality videos. They conduct experiments on text-to-video benchmarks to validate their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in adapting the Mamba architecture, known for its linear-time complexity in sequence modeling, to the challenging domain of text-to-video generation. The MM-DiM block is a novel design element that addresses the limitations of Mamba for multi-modal and spatiotemporal data. The reward learning strategy is a standard technique, but its application to improve long-context video generation within the Mamba framework is a relevant contribution. However, the overall framework architecture borrows heavily from PyramidFlow, weakening the novelty claim.
*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it addresses the high computational cost of Transformer-based models in text-to-video generation, which is a crucial bottleneck for wider adoption and scalability. Mamba's inherent efficiency offers a potential pathway to more resource-friendly video generation. The reported reduction in FLOPs and computational time is a tangible benefit. The focus on long-range temporal coherence, addressed through the reward learning strategy and MM-DIM block, is also important for producing realistic and visually pleasing videos.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The core idea of applying Mamba to text-to-video generation is compelling.
    *   The MM-DiM block is a well-designed component that addresses the limitations of Mamba for multi-modal and spatiotemporal data.
    *   The paper provides quantitative results demonstrating a reduction in computational cost and qualitative results showcasing the generated video quality.
    *   The ablation studies offer insights into the contribution of each component.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on PyramidFlow as the foundational architecture weakens the overall novelty of M4V. While the Mamba component is new, it's built within an existing framework.
    *   The "Dynamic Degree" score reported on VBench is slightly lower than attention-based models, suggesting some limitations in motion diversity. The paper acknowledges this, but it represents a potential drawback of the current approach.
    *   While the computational cost is reduced, a truly fair comparison with existing approaches is difficult due to varying video resolutions, training data, and experimental setups.
    *   The generated videos, while qualitatively appealing, are not groundbreaking in terms of realism and coherence compared to the latest closed-source systems.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in text-to-video generation by demonstrating the viability of Mamba-based architectures as an alternative to Transformers. The MM-DiM block design could serve as inspiration for other researchers working on adapting sequence models to multi-modal and spatiotemporal data. The identified limitations related to dynamic degree can help shape future research directions. The opensource nature of the project (code and models) will likely facilitate adoption.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by adapting the Mamba architecture to text-to-video generation. The MM-DiM block is a novel and effective design element, and the reported reductions in computational cost are promising. However, the heavy reliance on PyramidFlow, the lower "Dynamic Degree" score, and the incremental advancements relative to state-of-the-art video generation models limit the overall impact.
