### Sequential-Parallel Duality in Prefix Scannable Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10918v1)
- **Authors**: Morris Yau, Sharut Gupta, Valerie Engelmayer, Kazuki Irie, Stefanie Jegelka, Jacob Andreas
- **Abstract**: Modern neural sequence models are designed to meet the dual mandate of parallelizable training and fast sequential inference. Recent developments have given rise to various models, such as Gated Linear Attention (GLA) and Mamba, that achieve such ``sequential-parallel duality.'' This raises a natural question: can we characterize the full class of neural sequence models that support near-constant-time parallel evaluation and linear-time, constant-space sequential inference? We begin by describing a broad class of such models -- state space models -- as those whose state updates can be computed using the classic parallel prefix scan algorithm with a custom associative aggregation operator. We then define a more general class, Prefix-Scannable Models (PSMs), by relaxing the state aggregation operator to allow arbitrary (potentially non-associative) functions such as softmax attention. This generalization unifies many existing architectures, including element-wise RNNs (e.g., Mamba) and linear transformers (e.g., GLA, Mamba2, mLSTM), while also introducing new models with softmax-like operators that achieve O(1) amortized compute per token and log(N) memory for sequence length N. We empirically evaluate such models on illustrative small-scale language modeling and canonical synthetic tasks, including state tracking and associative recall. Empirically, we find that PSMs retain the expressivity of transformer-based architectures while matching the inference efficiency of state space models -- in some cases exhibiting better length generalization than either.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces and formalizes the concept of Sequential-Parallel Duality (SPD) in neural sequence models, focusing on architectures that allow parallelizable training and efficient sequential inference. It defines Prefix-Scannable Models (PSMs) as a class of models that generalizes state space models (SSMs) and linear transformers, enabling the use of arbitrary (potentially non-associative) aggregation functions during state updates. The authors demonstrate that many existing architectures, including Mamba and GLA, fit within this framework and introduce new models with softmax-like operators that offer a trade-off between computation and memory. Empirical evaluations on language modeling and algorithmic tasks suggest that PSMs can retain the functional effectiveness of transformers while achieving the inference efficiency of SSMs, sometimes with better length generalization.

**Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution by unifying various recent efficient sequence models under the conceptual umbrella of Prefix-Scannable Models (PSMs) and Sequential-Parallel Duality (SPD). Formalizing SPD and characterizing models in terms of their ability to perform prefix scans provides a useful framework for understanding and designing new architectures. The generalization of SSMs to allow non-associative aggregation rules is a novel extension, potentially enabling the design of models that leverage the strengths of both transformers and SSMs.

However, the paper also exhibits several weaknesses. While the PSM framework is elegant, its practical advantages over existing methods are not entirely clear from the experiments presented. The empirical results are promising, but the tasks are relatively small-scale, and the benefits of PSMs in larger, more realistic scenarios need further validation. Furthermore, while the paper introduces the potential for non-associative aggregation, it does not delve deeply into the implications or best practices for utilizing this flexibility.  The framework's primary innovation is conceptual (a unified view and a slight generalization).  It relies heavily on the Blelloch scan, a well-known algorithm.  While the insights around non-associative aggregation are interesting, their empirical benefits remain somewhat limited in the presented experiments. The claim of better length generalization for the T-PSM in S5 task needs more analysis, including a comparison to other models trained with similar parameters, which are absent in the current paper. It's also unclear if the "uniform query sampling" in MQAR constitutes a realistic problem or is merely an artificial scenario in which the performance is altered. Finally, while the experiments show the model's capacity to recall and retain state, there is a lack of extensive analyses to measure each property individually.

The paper's significance lies in its conceptual framework. The rigorous formulation of SPD and PSMs provides a valuable lens for analyzing and comparing different sequence models. Furthermore, the exploration of non-associative aggregation operators opens a new avenue for research in this area. However, the paper's limited empirical validation and lack of deeper insight into the practical implications of PSMs diminish its immediate impact.  Its impact on the community is likely to be more as a unifying perspective than a foundation for significant practical improvements, at least without further work to bolster empirical advantages on a larger scale.

Score: 6

**Rationale:**
The score of 6 reflects a balance between the paper's conceptual contribution and its practical limitations. The unification and generalization provided by the PSM framework are valuable and novel aspects of the work. However, the limited experimental evidence and the lack of in-depth exploration of non-associative aggregation weaken the paper's overall impact. The paper lays the groundwork for further research and development in this area, but its direct influence on practical applications remains uncertain.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Decomposing MLP Activations into Interpretable Features via Semi-Nonnegative Matrix Factorization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10920v1)
- **Authors**: Or Shafran, Atticus Geiger, Mor Geva
- **Abstract**: A central goal for mechanistic interpretability has been to identify the right units of analysis in large language models (LLMs) that causally explain their outputs. While early work focused on individual neurons, evidence that neurons often encode multiple concepts has motivated a shift toward analyzing directions in activation space. A key question is how to find directions that capture interpretable features in an unsupervised manner. Current methods rely on dictionary learning with sparse autoencoders (SAEs), commonly trained over residual stream activations to learn directions from scratch. However, SAEs often struggle in causal evaluations and lack intrinsic interpretability, as their learning is not explicitly tied to the computations of the model. Here, we tackle these limitations by directly decomposing MLP activations with semi-nonnegative matrix factorization (SNMF), such that the learned features are (a) sparse linear combinations of co-activated neurons, and (b) mapped to their activating inputs, making them directly interpretable. Experiments on Llama 3.1, Gemma 2 and GPT-2 show that SNMF derived features outperform SAEs and a strong supervised baseline (difference-in-means) on causal steering, while aligning with human-interpretable concepts. Further analysis reveals that specific neuron combinations are reused across semantically-related features, exposing a hierarchical structure in the MLP's activation space. Together, these results position SNMF as a simple and effective tool for identifying interpretable features and dissecting concept representations in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize and critically evaluate the paper "Decomposing MLP Activations into Interpretable Features via Semi-Nonnegative Matrix Factorization."

**Summary:**

This paper presents a novel approach to mechanistic interpretability in large language models (LLMs) by decomposing Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) activations using semi-nonnegative matrix factorization (SNMF).  Unlike prevalent methods relying on sparse autoencoders (SAEs) which learn feature directions from scratch, the proposed method represents features as sparse linear combinations of existing directions in MLP weight matrices, defined by groups of neurons. This makes the resulting features intrinsically interpretable and directly tied to the model's computations. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of SNMF-derived features on Llama 3.1, Gemma 2, and GPT-2, showing improved causal steering compared to SAEs and a supervised difference-in-means baseline, while maintaining strong performance in concept detection. They further reveal that the MLP's activation space exhibits a hierarchical structure through reused neuron combinations across semantically related features.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in applying SNMF directly to MLP activations for feature extraction, rather than training SAEs on residual stream activations. Representing features as combinations of existing neurons is a distinct approach from dictionary learning that learns directions *de novo*. The concept of using SNMF to reveal the hierarchical organization of concepts in MLP activations also presents a new perspective.

*   **Significance:** This work directly addresses a key challenge in mechanistic interpretability: finding meaningful units of analysis within LLMs. The demonstrated improvements in causal steering are promising, suggesting that SNMF-derived features can be more effectively manipulated to control model behavior. Uncovering the hierarchical structure in MLP activation space provides valuable insights into how LLMs represent and process information. This hierarchical structure, while hinted at previously, has been made considerably clearer by applying SNMF recursively. Moreover, linking high-level concepts to additive combinations of neurons offers a new lens for understanding model computations and extends findings such as those by Geva et al.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Intrinsically interpretable features:** A major strength is the direct connection between learned features and the model's internal mechanisms (MLP weights and neurons). This contrasts with SAEs, whose learned directions are less directly tied to the model's computations.
    *   **Improved causal steering:** The experimental results showing that SNMF outperforms both unsupervised SAEs and supervised baselines on causal steering tasks strengthens the method's effectiveness.
    *   **Hierarchical organization discovery:** Revealing the hierarchical organization of concepts and the reuse of neuron combinations provides valuable insights into LLM representation learning.
    *   **Clear exposition and experimental design:** The paper is well-written with clearly defined methods and experimental setups.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale:** While the authors conducted experiments on reasonably sized LLMs, it will be important to see how well this approach scales to the largest LLMs available today. The claim of general applicability would be strengthened by scaling the analysis.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The choice of *k* (number of features) is a critical hyperparameter, and the discussion of its influence, while present, could be more in-depth. Exploring methods for automatically selecting an optimal *k* would enhance the practical applicability. The sparsity parameter is similarly a key hyperparameter. The sensitivity of SNMF to initialisation has not been carefully explored.
    *   **Concept Labeling Reliance on GPT-4o:** While the reliance on GPT-4o to generate concept descriptions is a standard procedure, this approach is inherently subjective to bias in the language models and may not perfectly capture the underlying feature. A more rigorous analysis involving human annotation may be useful to strengthen the claims.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** The reliance on Neuronpedia for dataset construction and comparison datasets, may also introduce biases into the evaluations. Using a more varied array of datasets and concepts would be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a solid foundation for future research in mechanistic interpretability. The method could be extended to analyze other components of LLMs, such as attention heads. The discovery of hierarchical organization and neuron reuse could inform novel model architectures. The code release will further facilitate adoption by the wider research community.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the methodology offers an advancement compared to SAE methods, the inherent reliance on language models for concept identification and the limited discussion on hyperparameter sensitivity limit the overall score.

**Score: 7**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution by applying SNMF to MLP activations, uncovering important insights into LLM representation learning, but also contains weaknesses. The improvement in causal steering is a strong result. The paper is well-written and sets the stage for further investigation into neuron compositionality and more effective methods for manipulating LLM behavior.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Robustly Improving LLM Fairness in Realistic Settings via Interpretability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10922v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Karvonen, Samuel Marks
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes hiring applications, making decisions that directly impact people's careers and livelihoods. While prior studies suggest simple anti-bias prompts can eliminate demographic biases in controlled evaluations, we find these mitigations fail when realistic contextual details are introduced. We address these failures through internal bias mitigation: by identifying and neutralizing sensitive attribute directions within model activations, we achieve robust bias reduction across all tested scenarios. Across leading commercial (GPT-4o, Claude 4 Sonnet, Gemini 2.5 Flash) and open-source models (Gemma-2 27B, Gemma-3, Mistral-24B), we find that adding realistic context such as company names, culture descriptions from public careers pages, and selective hiring constraints (e.g.,``only accept candidates in the top 10\%") induces significant racial and gender biases (up to 12\% differences in interview rates). When these biases emerge, they consistently favor Black over White candidates and female over male candidates across all tested models and scenarios. Moreover, models can infer demographics and become biased from subtle cues like college affiliations, with these biases remaining invisible even when inspecting the model's chain-of-thought reasoning. To address these limitations, our internal bias mitigation identifies race and gender-correlated directions and applies affine concept editing at inference time. Despite using directions from a simple synthetic dataset, the intervention generalizes robustly, consistently reducing bias to very low levels (typically under 1\%, always below 2.5\%) while largely maintaining model performance. Our findings suggest that practitioners deploying LLMs for hiring should adopt more realistic evaluation methodologies and consider internal mitigation strategies for equitable outcomes.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Robustly Improving LLM Fairness in Realistic Settings via Interpretability":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of prompt-based bias mitigation techniques in large language models (LLMs) when applied in more realistic hiring scenarios. The authors find that simple anti-bias prompts, which are effective in controlled evaluations, often fail when contextual details like company names, culture descriptions, and selective hiring constraints are introduced. They address these failures by using an internal bias mitigation method: identifying and neutralizing sensitive attribute directions within model activations using affine concept editing. The authors test their approach on commercial and open-source models, finding it reduces racial and gender bias robustly across various scenarios, even when bias is inferred from subtle cues like college affiliations, while largely maintaining model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates the fragility of prompt-based bias mitigation in LLMs when realistic contextual information is introduced, which is a valuable finding. The authors also propose a method for internally mitigating bias in LLMs in complex scenarios using interpretability techniques. While the technique of internal representation manipulation for bias reduction has been explored before, the application and demonstration within realistic hiring scenarios are novel and relevant. However, the specific method employed (affine concept editing) is not groundbreaking and relies on existing techniques.

*   **Significance:** This work is highly significant. The finding that prompt-based methods fail in realistic settings raises serious concerns about the deployment of LLMs in high-stakes areas like hiring. The ability to robustly reduce bias using internal interventions is crucial for ensuring fairness and equity. The focus on implicit bias that arises from subtle cues further emphasizes the practical importance of this mitigation strategy. The paper's findings push for a move towards more rigorous bias evaluation methodologies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Realistic Evaluation:** The study addresses a crucial gap by evaluating LLM bias in more realistic scenarios, going beyond simplified controlled experiments.
    *   **Robust Mitigation:** The proposed internal mitigation method shows promising results across various models and scenarios, consistently reducing bias.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a detailed and reproducible methodology for evaluating and mitigating bias.
    *   **Addresses Implicit Bias:** The study acknowledges and mitigates biases arising from subtle cues, which is often overlooked in other research.
    *   **Performance Impact:** The paper evaluates and shows limited impact on general model capabilities with the mitigation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Bias Definition:** The sensitive attribute directions are based on a synthetic dataset of name-based bias, which may not fully capture real-world biases embedded within training data. It is crucial that a method that involves fine-tuning/training be used to identify the attribute directions.
    *   **Limited Scope of Protected Attributes:** The study primarily focuses on race and gender. It should include all aspects of it.
    *   **Overclaims:** While the paper demonstrates robustness, it is hard to definitively claim "robustly improving" as there could be a wide variety of other situations and data that could elicit unexpected results.
    *   **Data and Code:** Given that the paper is from 2025, a 2024 codebase might not be directly compatible. This needs to be updated.
    *   **Generalizability to Other Models and Domains**: It is unclear how the proposed method will apply to larger and different architectures.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence researchers and practitioners deploying LLMs in hiring to:
    *   Adopt more realistic and comprehensive evaluation methodologies.
    *   Consider internal mitigation strategies to address bias, especially in complex scenarios.
    *   Be aware of implicit biases arising from subtle cues and develop methods to detect and mitigate them.

* **Rigour:** The paper's approach to addressing bias is comprehensive, and the methodology is well-documented. However, further investigation into the potential for introducing unintended biases with internal interventions is warranted. The authors acknowledge this concern, but additional analysis of specific instances where bias might be inadvertently amplified would strengthen the study.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the field by demonstrating the fragility of existing bias mitigation techniques and proposing a more robust alternative. The experimental results are compelling, and the analysis is well-structured. The work highlights important practical considerations for deploying LLMs in real-world applications. However, the reliance on a synthetic dataset for defining bias directions and the limited scope of protected attributes somewhat limit the scope of its impact. In addition, the work's claims are slightly overstated, which should be kept in check. Lastly, the work's findings are restricted only to code written in 2024, therefore not fully generalizable to the present times. Thus, a score of 7 is appropriate, reflecting the paper's valuable contribution and clear avenues for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Role of Generative AI in Facilitating Social Interactions: A Scoping Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10927v1)
- **Authors**: T. T. J. E. Arets, G. Perugia, M. Houben, W. A. IJsselsteijn
- **Abstract**: Reduced social connectedness increasingly poses a threat to mental health, life expectancy, and general well-being. Generative AI (GAI) technologies, such as large language models (LLMs) and image generation tools, are increasingly integrated into applications aimed at enhancing human social experiences. Despite their growing presence, little is known about how these technologies influence social interactions. This scoping review investigates how GAI-based applications are currently designed to facilitate social interaction, what forms of social engagement they target, and which design and evaluation methodologies designers use to create and evaluate them. Through an analysis of 30 studies published since 2020, we identify key trends in application domains including storytelling, socio-emotional skills training, reminiscence, collaborative learning, music making, and general conversation. We highlight the role of participatory and co-design approaches in fostering both effective technology use and social engagement, while also examining socio-ethical concerns such as cultural bias and accessibility. This review underscores the potential of GAI to support dynamic and personalized interactions, but calls for greater attention to equitable design practices and inclusive evaluation strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a scoping review investigating the role of Generative AI (GAI) technologies in facilitating social interactions.  It analyzes 30 studies published since 2020, examining how GAI-based applications are designed, the forms of social engagement they target, and the design and evaluation methodologies employed.  The review identifies trends in application domains such as storytelling, socio-emotional skills training, reminiscence, collaborative learning, and general conversation. It emphasizes the importance of participatory and co-design approaches while addressing socio-ethical concerns like cultural bias and accessibility.  The authors conclude that GAI has the potential to support dynamic and personalized interactions but requires greater attention to equitable design practices and inclusive evaluation strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and rapidly evolving area.  The integration of GAI into social applications is a significant trend, and understanding its impact is crucial.
    *   **Clear Scope and Methodology:**  The scoping review methodology is appropriate for the exploratory nature of the topic. The authors clearly define their research questions and inclusion/exclusion criteria. The use of PRISMA-ScR guidelines adds rigor.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The analysis of 30 studies provides a good overview of the current landscape. The identification of trends in application domains, design approaches, and evaluation metrics is valuable.
    *   **Emphasis on Socio-Ethical Considerations:** The inclusion of socio-ethical perspectives (bias, accessibility, equity) is crucial and often overlooked in technology development.
    *   **Well-Defined Evaluation:** It carefully examines the level of user involvement in design/evaluation.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The categorization of GAI's role (tool, medium, agent) adds a layer of nuance to the analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth of Analysis:**  As a scoping review, the paper aims for breadth rather than depth. While trends are identified, a more in-depth qualitative analysis of the included studies could have revealed richer insights.
    *   **Potential for Bias in Study Selection:** While the inclusion/exclusion criteria are defined, the rapid pace of GAI development makes it challenging to capture the entire landscape comprehensively.  There is a reliance on database and conference searches, and newer platforms that might not be indexed are missing.
    *   **General Conclusions:**  The conclusions are somewhat general and reiterate existing concerns and benefits. More specific recommendations for design practices and evaluation strategies would enhance the paper's practical impact.
    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** It lacks empirical validation that the conclusions are helpful for designers
    *   **Overreliance on GPT**: There is an overreliance on text-based GPT Models in existing work, it might not represent the entire GAI possibilities

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper fills a gap in the literature by systematically examining the design and evaluation of GAI-based social applications.
    *   It provides a valuable framework for researchers and designers to consider the ethical and social implications of GAI in this context.
    *   While the individual findings are not entirely novel (e.g., the importance of co-design, the risks of bias), the comprehensive synthesis and landscape-level view are significant contributions.
    *   It highlights the ethical and social considerations in the social interaction as a proxy for social connectedness.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a timely and well-executed scoping review that makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive overview of the design and evaluation of GAI-based applications for social interaction. While the individual findings are not groundbreaking, the synthesis and identification of trends are significant. However, a more detailed qualitative analysis, specific recommendations, and discussions of limitations would enhance the paper's impact. The ethical consideration can be enhanced to focus more on technical details for real GAI production. The reliance on GPT-based models is also considered. The scope of the social interaction also can be broadened. It is a solid, systematic effort, but its limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dynamic Epistemic Friction in Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10934v1)
- **Authors**: Timothy Obiso, Kenneth Lai, Abhijnan Nath, Nikhil Krishnaswamy, James Pustejovsky
- **Abstract**: Recent developments in aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences have significantly enhanced their utility in human-AI collaborative scenarios. However, such approaches often neglect the critical role of "epistemic friction," or the inherent resistance encountered when updating beliefs in response to new, conflicting, or ambiguous information. In this paper, we define dynamic epistemic friction as the resistance to epistemic integration, characterized by the misalignment between an agent's current belief state and new propositions supported by external evidence. We position this within the framework of Dynamic Epistemic Logic (Van Benthem and Pacuit, 2011), where friction emerges as nontrivial belief-revision during the interaction. We then present analyses from a situated collaborative task that demonstrate how this model of epistemic friction can effectively predict belief updates in dialogues, and we subsequently discuss how the model of belief alignment as a measure of epistemic resistance or friction can naturally be made more sophisticated to accommodate the complexities of real-world dialogue scenarios.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper, critically evaluate its novelty and significance, assign a score, and provide a detailed justification.

**Summary:**

This paper introduces the concept of "dynamic epistemic friction" (DEF) in dialogue, defining it as the resistance encountered when updating beliefs in response to new, conflicting, or ambiguous information. The authors frame DEF within Dynamic Epistemic Logic (DEL) and propose a vector-based model using Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR) to represent belief states and propositions. This approach allows for quantifying epistemic friction based on the geometric relationship (alignment/orthogonality) between belief vectors and new information vectors. The paper presents analyses of a situated collaborative task (the Weights Task Dataset) to demonstrate how the DEF model can predict belief updates and discusses how the model can be further sophisticated to handle real-world dialogue complexities.  The paper argues that modeling friction allows for a better understanding of cognitive and communicative processes in conversations, including deceptive behavior, strategic misalignment, and the interplay of inference, pragmatics, and cognitive representations.  The experimental evaluation involves predicting the final belief state of participants in the Weights Task based on interlocutor utterances and comparing this prediction with a ground truth. The results show that the DEF model can effectively predict belief updates and that properly modulating the amount of friction through the tuning of friction coefficients is important for dialogue interpretation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of "epistemic friction" is reasonably novel, although related concepts like "resistance to persuasion" or "cognitive dissonance" exist in other fields. Framing this as a force within a DEL framework is a potentially valuable contribution. The vector-based implementation with HRR provides a concrete way to operationalize the concept. However, the novelty is somewhat diminished by the fact that similar techniques have been applied in other NLP tasks like tracking common ground using vector representations, and the experimental setting focuses on a domain that is arguably more collaborative that contains many instances of conflict or misalignment.

*   **Significance:** If the DEF model proves to be robust and generalizable, it could significantly impact how we model dialogue. Specifically, DEF has the potential to provide insights into:

    *   **Detecting deceptive behavior:** High friction might indicate deception or strategic intent.
    *   **Improving human-AI interaction:** By recognizing and mitigating friction, AI agents could engage in more effective and natural conversations.
    *   **Modeling belief revision:** The DEF framework provides a structured way to represent and update beliefs in the face of conflicting evidence.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Formal Framework:** Grounding the concept in DEL provides a solid theoretical foundation.
    *   **Operationalization:** The vector-based implementation makes the model testable and applicable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on the Weights Task Dataset provide initial evidence for the model's effectiveness.
    *   **Clarity of Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and explains the key concepts clearly.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplified Task:** The Weights Task is a relatively simple collaborative setting. It's unclear how well the model would generalize to more complex, adversarial, or ambiguous dialogues.  The reliance on a cooperative task limits the ability to examine real-world scenarios that might contain more explicit disagreement, misunderstandings, or attempts at deception.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation is focused on one dataset and a relatively small number of groups. A more extensive evaluation across diverse dialogue datasets is needed.
    *   **Vectorization Simplification:** The simplification of belief states into 5-dimensional vectors may lose important nuances of the belief structure of agents. The chosen encoding function may be overly simplistic for expressing nuanced states.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The model's performance is sensitive to the choice of friction coefficients (α and β), requiring careful tuning. The sensitivity to hyperparameter settings also raises the question of how generalizable the model is across different domains without extensive re-tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in dialogue modeling, human-AI interaction, and belief revision. It could inspire new approaches for detecting deception, improving the naturalness of AI agents, and understanding the dynamics of belief change in conversation.

*   **Missing components**: There's no consideration for confidence in these models. Confidence in a statement or a belief is not captured in the provided model, yet this would be a critical signal to include in the epistemic friction measurement.

**Justification for Score:**

While the concept of dynamic epistemic friction is interesting and the formalization is well-executed, several weaknesses temper its potential impact. The limited evaluation on a simplified task and the sensitivity to hyperparameters suggest that the model may not be as robust or generalizable as needed for real-world applications. In addition, there is insufficient discussion of related literature that focuses on similar aspects of "resistance to persuasion" and "cognitive dissonance". Therefore, while the paper presents a good starting point, it requires more substantial validation and refinement to demonstrate its true value. It would be more significant if the models demonstrated success in situations containing instances of disagreement, misunderstanding, and attempts at deception.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Self-Adapting Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10943v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Zweiger, Jyothish Pari, Han Guo, Ekin Akyürek, Yoon Kim, Pulkit Agrawal
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are powerful but static; they lack mechanisms to adapt their weights in response to new tasks, knowledge, or examples. We introduce Self-Adapting LLMs (SEAL), a framework that enables LLMs to self-adapt by generating their own finetuning data and update directives. Given a new input, the model produces a self-edit-a generation that may restructure the information in different ways, specify optimization hyperparameters, or invoke tools for data augmentation and gradient-based updates. Through supervised finetuning (SFT), these self-edits result in persistent weight updates, enabling lasting adaptation. To train the model to produce effective self-edits, we use a reinforcement learning loop with the downstream performance of the updated model as the reward signal. Unlike prior approaches that rely on separate adaptation modules or auxiliary networks, SEAL directly uses the model's own generation to control its adaptation process. Experiments on knowledge incorporation and few-shot generalization show that SEAL is a promising step toward language models capable of self-directed adaptation. Our website and code is available at https://jyopari.github.io/posts/seal.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Self-Adapting Language Models" (SEAL), a framework that enables LLMs to adapt their weights in response to new data by generating their own finetuning data and update directives, termed "self-edits". Given new input, the LLM produces a self-edit specifying data transformations, optimization hyperparameters, or tool invocations. These edits are then used to finetune the model, leading to persistent adaptation. A reinforcement learning loop is used to train the model to produce effective self-edits, where the reward signal is based on the downstream performance of the updated model. The framework is evaluated on knowledge incorporation and few-shot generalization tasks. The results demonstrate that SEAL can improve performance by generating and learning from its own synthetic data, outperforming methods that finetune directly on raw data or use static synthetic data generation strategies. The paper emphasizes that SEAL directly leverages the model's generative capabilities to control its adaptation, unlike methods relying on external adaptation modules.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of having an LLM generate its own finetuning data and update parameters is relatively novel. While synthetic data generation and meta-learning for LLMs are established areas, the specific combination of using RL to train an LLM to generate *self-edits* that control its own adaptation pipeline is a distinct contribution. The key novelty lies in the *integration* of synthetic data generation, hyperparameter optimization, and weight updating all controlled through the model's own generation.

**Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential for more efficient and scalable adaptation of LLMs. Current approaches to adapting LLMs often require significant human effort in curating datasets or tuning hyperparameters. If SEAL can effectively automate this process, it could democratize access to specialized LLMs and enable continual learning. The results, while not demonstrating state-of-the-art performance *overall*, show promising improvements in specific settings like knowledge incorporation and a simplified ARC-AGI task. The fact that SEAL outperforms GPT-4.1 in self-generating knowledge incorporation examples is noteworthy, showing the model can evolve past its current understanding.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Concept:** The paper clearly articulates the SEAL framework and its underlying motivation.
*   **Well-Defined Approach:** The methods are well-defined, explaining the use of RL, self-edit generation, and supervised finetuning.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes experiments across two distinct tasks, demonstrating the framework's versatility.
*   **Interesting Results:** The results show that SEAL can improve performance over baselines in knowledge incorporation and few-shot generalization.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scale:**  Experiments are primarily conducted on a smaller Llama-3.2-1B model.  The computational overhead section also highlights this limitation. It's uncertain how well SEAL will scale to larger, state-of-the-art models (e.g., 70B+ parameter models). The continual learning section states "it can perform multiple updates without complete collapse, indicating possibility for improvement," which suggests there is still work to be done in this area to improve upon SEAL's performance.
*   **Performance Gap to Oracle:** The few-shot learning results show a significant gap in performance between SEAL and the "Oracle TTT" baseline, suggesting that the learned self-edit policy is still suboptimal. It also suggests that further work needs to be done in RL exploration/policy generation.
*   **Catastrophic Forgetting:** The experiments reveal issues with catastrophic forgetting during continual learning. While acknowledged, this highlights a major limitation for real-world applications that require continuous adaptation.
*   **Computational Cost:** The TTT reward loop is computationally intensive and significantly slows down training. This may limit the applicability of the method.
*   **Limited Generalizability of Synthetic Data:** The question of whether SEAL’s learned synthetic data generation policies can generalize across completely different domains remains unclear.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **6.5**. The idea has merit, but the results aren't revolutionary and there are potential scalability and generalizability problems. The key contribution of controlling the entire adaptation pipeline through model generation is interesting and useful. It also has limited generalizability, as seen in both the continual self-edits and computational expense sections.

**Potential Influence:**

If the scalability and forgetting issues are addressed, SEAL (or its derivatives) could become a valuable tool for adapting LLMs to specific tasks and enabling continual learning. It also represents a significant step towards automating the processes required for LLM adaptation.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 0/10

### GUARD: Guided Unlearning and Retention via Data Attribution for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10946v1)
- **Authors**: Evelyn Ma, Duo Zhou, Peizhi Niu, Huiting Zhou, Huan Zhang, Olgica Milenkovic, S. Rasoul Etesami
- **Abstract**: Unlearning in large language models (LLMs) is becoming increasingly important due to regulatory compliance, copyright protection, and privacy concerns. However, a key challenge in LLM unlearning is unintended forgetting, where the removal of specific data inadvertently impairs the utility of the model and its retention of valuable, desired information. While prior work has primarily focused on architectural innovations, the influence of data-level factors on unlearning performance remains underexplored. As a result, existing methods often suffer from degraded retention when forgetting high-impact data. To address this, we propose GUARD-a novel framework for Guided Unlearning And Retention via Data attribution. At its core, GUARD introduces a lightweight proxy data attribution metric tailored for LLM unlearning, which quantifies the "alignment" between the forget and retain sets while remaining computationally efficient. Building on this, we design a novel unlearning objective that assigns adaptive, nonuniform unlearning weights to samples, inversely proportional to their proxy attribution scores. Through such a reallocation of unlearning power, GUARD mitigates unintended losses in retention. We provide rigorous theoretical guarantees that GUARD significantly enhances retention while maintaining forgetting metrics comparable to prior methods. Extensive experiments on the TOFU benchmark across multiple LLM architectures demonstrate that GUARD substantially improves utility preservation while ensuring effective unlearning. Notably, GUARD reduces utility sacrifice on the Retain Set by up to 194.92% in terms of Truth Ratio when forgetting 10% of the training data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GUARD, a novel framework for machine unlearning in large language models (LLMs). GUARD aims to mitigate the problem of unintended forgetting, where removing specific data degrades the LLM's ability to retain valuable, unrelated information. The core idea of GUARD is to use a lightweight data attribution metric tailored for LLMs, which quantifies the "alignment" between the data to be forgotten and the data to be retained.  GUARD then adaptively adjusts unlearning weights, giving less weight to samples that have a higher impact on retention, thereby minimizing unintended losses. The authors provide theoretical guarantees and extensive empirical results on the TOFU benchmark, demonstrating that GUARD improves utility preservation while maintaining effective unlearning across various LLM architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components. The *data attribution metric tailored for LLMs* is a significant contribution, as it addresses the computational inefficiency of traditional methods. The *retention-aware objective reweighting* scheme, which dynamically adjusts unlearning weights based on the attribution scores, is also a novel approach that effectively mitigates unintended forgetting.  The combination of these elements into a comprehensive framework is a notable contribution.

* **Significance:** LLM unlearning is a growing area of importance due to privacy concerns and regulatory compliance. The paper directly addresses a critical challenge in this field: the tradeoff between forgetting unwanted information and retaining valuable knowledge. By introducing a method that demonstrably improves retention without sacrificing unlearning effectiveness, the paper makes a significant step towards more practical and reliable LLM unlearning techniques. The theoretical guarantees offer a strong foundation for the empirical results. The improvements demonstrated on the TOFU benchmark, which is becoming a standard evaluation suite, further enhance the paper's significance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem of unintended forgetting in LLM unlearning.
    * **Novel Approach:**  The proposed data attribution and reweighting scheme represent a fresh perspective on the problem.
    * **Theoretical Guarantees:**  The theoretical analysis provides valuable insights into the behavior of GUARD and offers a rigorous foundation for its effectiveness.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  Extensive experiments on the TOFU benchmark across multiple LLM architectures demonstrate the practical benefits of GUARD.
    * **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is well-written, organized, and easy to follow, making it accessible to a wide audience.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of the Data Attribution Metric:** While the gradient-based attribution method is computationally efficient, it might not capture all aspects of data influence. Other more complex attribution methods (e.g., those that approximate the Hessian) could potentially provide more accurate estimates, although at a higher cost. This could potentially be a subject of future research that tries to maintain computational efficiency while further improving accuracy.
    * **Dependence on Temperature Parameter:**  The performance of GUARD is sensitive to the temperature parameter τ, which requires careful tuning.  Automating the tuning process or developing methods that are less sensitive to this parameter would improve the practicality of the framework. More research into methods to automate this tuning process could reduce the reliance on manual intervention.
    * **Theoretical Assumptions:** The theoretical guarantees rely on several assumptions, such as small updates and isotropic gradients. While these assumptions are common in data attribution literature, their validity in real-world LLM unlearning scenarios might be limited.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM unlearning by shifting the focus towards data-level factors and the importance of retention awareness. The proposed data attribution method and reweighting scheme could serve as a basis for developing more sophisticated unlearning techniques. The insights from the theoretical analysis could also inform the design of new unlearning objectives and algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and the overall novelty and significance of the work, a score of **7.5** is justified. The paper introduces a clearly motivated novel framework with compelling empirical results and solid theoretical foundations.  The significance of the work is clearly demonstrated by its ability to enhance retention without comprising forgetting. The weaknesses mentioned regarding the limited scope of the data attribution, the dependence on temperature parameter and theoretical assumption, are also important factors that prevent it from reaching a high score. Overall, the approach addresses an important problem, the solution is clever and effective, and the paper is well-written and contributes meaningfully to the field.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Execution Guided Line-by-Line Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10948v1)
- **Authors**: Boaz Lavon, Shahar Katz, Lior Wolf
- **Abstract**: We present a novel approach to neural code generation that incorporates real-time execution signals into the language model generation process. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive code generation capabilities, they typically do not utilize execution feedback during inference, a critical signal that human programmers regularly leverage. Our method, Execution-Guided Classifier-Free Guidance (EG-CFG), dynamically incorporates execution signals as the model generates code, providing line-by-line feedback that guides the generation process toward executable solutions. EG-CFG employs a multi-stage process: first, we conduct beam search to sample candidate program completions for each line; second, we extract execution signals by executing these candidates against test cases; and finally, we incorporate these signals into the prompt during generation. By maintaining consistent signals across tokens within the same line and refreshing signals at line boundaries, our approach provides coherent guidance while preserving syntactic structure. Moreover, the method naturally supports native parallelism at the task level in which multiple agents operate in parallel, exploring diverse reasoning paths and collectively generating a broad set of candidate solutions. Our experiments across diverse coding tasks demonstrate that EG-CFG significantly improves code generation performance compared to standard approaches, achieving state-of-the-art results across various levels of complexity, from foundational problems to challenging competitive programming tasks. Our code is available at: https://github.com/boazlavon/eg_cfg
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Execution Guided Line-by-Line Code Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called Execution-Guided Classifier-Free Guidance (EG-CFG) for neural code generation.  EG-CFG leverages real-time execution feedback during the code generation process, mimicking how human programmers iteratively test and refine their code. The method involves a multi-stage process: (1) beam search to generate candidate program completions for each line, (2) execution of these candidates against test cases to extract execution signals, and (3) incorporation of these signals into the prompt using Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) to guide subsequent token generation. The key here is line-by-line feedback and maintaining consistent signals within a line. The method also supports task-level parallelism.  The authors demonstrate that EG-CFG achieves state-of-the-art results on MBPP, MBPP-ET, HumanEval-ET, and CodeContests benchmarks, even when using open-source language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating real-time execution feedback into code generation isn't entirely new (prior work exists in iterative refinement and self-debugging). However, EG-CFG's *dynamic* and *line-by-line* integration of execution traces using CFG is a significant departure from existing techniques.  Previous approaches often work in discrete cycles (generate full code, execute, then refine), while EG-CFG continuously integrates feedback at the token level. The use of execution traces as a soft guidance signal, rather than just pass/fail or verbal critiques, is also innovative. The approach also leverages native parallelism by allowing multiple agents to work concurrently on the same task, something iterative refinement methods lack.
*   **Significance:** The reported results demonstrate a clear and substantial improvement over existing methods on several established benchmarks.  The fact that EG-CFG achieves state-of-the-art performance *using open-source models* and *outperforming approaches relying on larger, closed-source models* is a significant contribution. This potentially lowers the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners working in this area.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dynamic and fine-grained integration of execution feedback is a key strength.
    *   The method is demonstrated to be effective across a range of complexity levels, from foundational problems to challenging competitive programming tasks.
    *   The use of CFG allows for a principled way to balance the prior (language model knowledge) with the execution-based guidance.
    *   Native task-level parallelism is an added bonus, enabling more efficient exploration of the solution space.
    *   The authors release their code, improving reproducibility and enabling future research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method introduces computational overhead due to beam search, execution, and CFG. While the parallel execution strategy helps, this overhead could be a limitation for certain applications.
    *   The effectiveness is dependent on the availability of adequate test cases.
    *   The approach is bottom-up, and it does not incorporate task decomposition.

**Justification for Score:**

EG-CFG represents a significant advance in neural code generation due to its dynamic feedback mechanism, fine-grained control with CFG, and ability to leverage parallelism. It also outperforms other methods, even when using open-source models. The weaknesses are primarily related to computational cost and reliance on test cases, but these are common challenges in this field.
