- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Build the web for agents, not agents for the web
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10953v1)
- **Authors**: Xing Han Lù, Gaurav Kamath, Marius Mosbach, Siva Reddy
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and multimodal counterparts have spurred significant interest in developing web agents -- AI systems capable of autonomously navigating and completing tasks within web environments. While holding tremendous promise for automating complex web interactions, current approaches face substantial challenges due to the fundamental mismatch between human-designed interfaces and LLM capabilities. Current methods struggle with the inherent complexity of web inputs, whether processing massive DOM trees, relying on screenshots augmented with additional information, or bypassing the user interface entirely through API interactions. This position paper advocates for a paradigm shift in web agent research: rather than forcing web agents to adapt to interfaces designed for humans, we should develop a new interaction paradigm specifically optimized for agentic capabilities. To this end, we introduce the concept of an Agentic Web Interface (AWI), an interface specifically designed for agents to navigate a website. We establish six guiding principles for AWI design, emphasizing safety, efficiency, and standardization, to account for the interests of all primary stakeholders. This reframing aims to overcome fundamental limitations of existing interfaces, paving the way for more efficient, reliable, and transparent web agent design, which will be a collaborative effort involving the broader ML community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper advocates for a paradigm shift in web agent research. Instead of forcing AI agents to navigate existing web interfaces designed for humans, the authors propose developing *Agentic Web Interfaces* (AWIs) – interfaces specifically optimized for agentic capabilities. They argue that current approaches are fundamentally limited by the mismatch between human-designed interfaces and LLM capabilities, leading to inefficiencies, safety concerns, and representational limitations. The paper outlines six guiding principles for AWI design: standardization, human-centricity, safety, optimal representations, efficient hosting, and developer-friendliness. The authors also suggest concrete implementation ideas, such as unified higher-level actions, compatibility with user interfaces, access control for agents, progressive information transfer, and agentic task queues. The paper highlights the relevance of AWI development to various ML subfields, including HCAI, AI safety, NLP, multimodality, reinforcement learning, planning, and generalization. Finally, they differentiate AWIs from existing tool use protocols for LLMs such as MCP.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of *Agentic Web Interfaces* is relatively novel. While the limitations of existing web interfaces for AI agents have been discussed before, the explicit call for designing agent-specific interfaces and the framing of this problem as a fundamental research direction is a valuable contribution. The articulated guiding principles, while not entirely surprising, provide a useful framework for thinking about AWI design.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this idea is high. If successfully implemented, AWIs could significantly improve the efficiency, reliability, and safety of web agents, paving the way for more widespread adoption. The connection to different ML subfields is crucial. By highlighting how advancements in these areas can contribute to AWI design and benefit from it, the paper encourages interdisciplinary collaboration.

    However, the paper has limitations:

*   **Lack of Concrete Implementation:** The paper is primarily conceptual. It proposes an idea and outlines guiding principles, but it doesn't offer a prototype, detailed design, or experimental evaluation. This lack of practical demonstration weakens the argument and makes it difficult to assess the feasibility of the proposed approach. The intentional absence of a specific implementation roadmap also means that it is more akin to a position paper intended to promote the idea.
*   **Oversimplification of Stakeholder Alignment:** The authors acknowledge the need to align the interests of various stakeholders (ML researchers, web developers, end users), but the paper doesn't fully address the potential conflicts and trade-offs. For example, designing for agent efficiency might come at the cost of human usability, even with the "human-centric" principle. How these conflicting needs will be reconciled requires more detailed analysis.
*   **Feasibility Concerns:** The paper assumes widespread adoption of AWIs by website developers. This might be difficult to achieve in practice, especially if it requires significant changes to existing websites. The incentive structures (such as avoiding resource strain from web agents) for developers would need to be substantial and convincing.

Despite these limitations, the paper effectively identifies an important problem and proposes a promising, albeit high-level, solution. Its value lies in reframing the web agent research landscape, encouraging a shift in focus towards designing interfaces that are specifically tailored for AI agents. The impact of the paper will largely depend on whether the research community embraces this vision and actively contributes to the development and adoption of AWIs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's novel and potentially significant idea, balanced against its conceptual nature and the lack of concrete implementation details. The call for a paradigm shift in web agent research is compelling, and the guiding principles provide a useful framework. However, the lack of practical validation and the potential challenges in stakeholder alignment and adoption limit the current impact of the paper. The paper's significance hinges on its ability to inspire and guide future research in this area, so the assigned score is based on potential impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SWE-Factory: Your Automated Factory for Issue Resolution Training Data and Evaluation Benchmarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10954v1)
- **Authors**: Lianghong Guo, Yanlin Wang, Caihua Li, Pengyu Yang, Jiachi Chen, Wei Tao, Yingtian Zou, Duyu Tang, Zibin Zheng
- **Abstract**: Constructing large-scale datasets for the GitHub issue resolution task is crucial for both training and evaluating the software engineering capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the traditional process for creating such benchmarks is notoriously challenging and labor-intensive, particularly in the stages of setting up evaluation environments, grading test outcomes, and validating task instances. In this paper, we propose SWE-Factory, an automated pipeline designed to address these challenges. To tackle these issues, our pipeline integrates three core automated components. First, we introduce SWE-Builder, a multi-agent system that automates evaluation environment construction, which employs four specialized agents that work in a collaborative, iterative loop and leverages an environment memory pool to enhance efficiency. Second, we introduce a standardized, exit-code-based grading method that eliminates the need for manually writing custom parsers. Finally, we automate the fail2pass validation process using these reliable exit code signals. Experiments on 671 issues across four programming languages show that our pipeline can effectively construct valid task instances; for example, with GPT-4.1-mini, our SWE-Builder constructs 269 valid instances at $0.045 per instance, while with Gemini-2.5-flash, it achieves comparable performance at the lowest cost of $0.024 per instance. We also demonstrate that our exit-code-based grading achieves 100% accuracy compared to manual inspection, and our automated fail2pass validation reaches a precision of 0.92 and a recall of 1.00. We hope our automated pipeline will accelerate the collection of large-scale, high-quality GitHub issue resolution datasets for both training and evaluation. Our code and datasets are released at https://github.com/DeepSoftwareAnalytics/swe-factory.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SWE-Factory paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SWE-Factory, an automated pipeline designed to create datasets for training and evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) in GitHub issue resolution. It addresses the traditionally labor-intensive aspects of benchmark creation, particularly setting up evaluation environments, grading test outcomes, and validating task instances. SWE-Factory incorporates three core automated components: SWE-Builder, a multi-agent system for automating environment construction; a standardized, exit-code-based grading method; and automated fail2pass validation.  Experiments on a diverse set of issues across multiple programming languages demonstrate the pipeline's effectiveness in constructing valid task instances at a reasonable cost, while also highlighting specific advantages of different LLMs used within the framework. The paper also identifies and characterizes the "error2pass" phenomenon which can lead to underestimation of model capabilities and proposes methods to filter it.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a practical solution to a significant problem in the field of LLMs for software engineering: the costly and time-consuming creation of high-quality benchmark datasets.  The automation of the environment setup (SWE-Builder) is a valuable contribution.  The multi-agent approach for this task seems well-designed, and the use of an environment memory pool is a reasonable optimization. The adoption of exit codes for grading is a clever simplification that eliminates the need for complex log parsers.  Automated fail2pass validation, combined with the identification and analysis of the error2pass phenomenon, significantly improves the quality control of the generated datasets.

**Novelty:**

The novelty lies in the *integrated* approach to automating the entire dataset creation pipeline.  While individual components like multi-agent systems or exit-code-based grading are not entirely new, the combination of these techniques, specifically tailored to the GitHub issue resolution task, is novel.  The error2pass analysis is also a noteworthy contribution that raises awareness about potential pitfalls in benchmark creation.  Compared to existing works such as SWE-bench, which focuses on providing the dataset itself, SWE-Factory contributes a methodology for *creating* such datasets automatically and efficiently, which makes it relevant for a broader range of tasks and languages. Existing approaches still had manual elements involved in grading and validation.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the potential to accelerate the development and evaluation of LLMs for software engineering. By automating the benchmark creation process, SWE-Factory enables the construction of larger and more diverse datasets, leading to more robust and reliable LLMs.  The cost reduction associated with automation is also a key factor, making it accessible to a wider range of researchers. The insights into the error2pass phenomenon are valuable for improving benchmark quality control in general.  The fact that it's open-source ensures reproducibility and encourages future research in this direction.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The evaluation primarily focuses on the technical aspects of the pipeline (success rate, valid rate, cost).  A more in-depth analysis of the *quality* of the generated benchmarks in terms of their challenge and relevance would further strengthen the paper. How well do the generated datasets translate to real-world performance of the models?
*   While the paper mentions the use of multiple LLMs, a more comprehensive comparison of different LLMs *within* SWE-Builder would be beneficial.  What LLM properties make it a good repository explorer or test manager?
*   While the paper proposes a solution for a significant problem, it would be nice to consider an extensive assessment of other methods to improve the creation of Github Issue resolution data.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a practical and well-engineered solution to a major bottleneck in the development of LLMs for software engineering. The integration of different automated components and the error2pass analysis are significant contributions that improve the efficiency and quality of benchmark creation. The limitations mentioned above hold it back from a higher score, but the potential for impact on the field is substantial.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### ReGuidance: A Simple Diffusion Wrapper for Boosting Sample Quality on Hard Inverse Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10955v1)
- **Authors**: Aayush Karan, Kulin Shah, Sitan Chen
- **Abstract**: There has been a flurry of activity around using pretrained diffusion models as informed data priors for solving inverse problems, and more generally around steering these models using reward models. Training-free methods like diffusion posterior sampling (DPS) and its many variants have offered flexible heuristic algorithms for these tasks, but when the reward is not informative enough, e.g., in hard inverse problems with low signal-to-noise ratio, these techniques veer off the data manifold, failing to produce realistic outputs. In this work, we devise a simple wrapper, ReGuidance, for boosting both the sample realism and reward achieved by these methods. Given a candidate solution $\hat{x}$ produced by an algorithm of the user's choice, we propose inverting the solution by running the unconditional probability flow ODE in reverse starting from $\hat{x}$, and then using the resulting latent as an initialization for DPS. We evaluate our wrapper on hard inverse problems like large box in-painting and super-resolution with high upscaling. Whereas state-of-the-art baselines visibly fail, we find that applying our wrapper on top of these baselines significantly boosts sample quality and measurement consistency. We complement these findings with theory proving that on certain multimodal data distributions, ReGuidance simultaneously boosts the reward and brings the candidate solution closer to the data manifold. To our knowledge, this constitutes the first rigorous algorithmic guarantee for DPS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces REGUIDANCE, a simple two-step diffusion model wrapper designed to improve the sample quality of existing diffusion-based inverse problem solvers, especially in challenging scenarios with low signal-to-noise ratios. The approach first extracts a latent representation from a candidate solution produced by any inverse problem solver using the reverse probability flow ODE.  Then, it refines this latent using the Diffusion Posterior Sampling (DPS) algorithm. The authors demonstrate empirically on image inpainting and super-resolution tasks that REGUIDANCE enhances realism and measurement consistency compared to state-of-the-art baselines.  They also provide theoretical guarantees, proving in a Gaussian mixture model setting that REGUIDANCE improves reward and brings the solution closer to the data manifold, offering the first algorithmic guarantees for DPS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a practical and effective method for boosting the performance of diffusion models in inverse problems. The core idea of using reverse diffusion to find a "good" latent and then applying DPS is intuitively appealing and yields demonstrable improvements. The theoretical analysis, while limited to a simplified model, provides a valuable initial step towards understanding the behavior of DPS and the role of latent initialization. While not revolutionary, the combination of a readily applicable technique with a supporting theoretical argument is a valuable contribution. The novelty comes from the specific way to leverage existing methods to get better samples, along with the first theoretical results about it.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its ability to address a key limitation of existing diffusion-based inverse problem solvers: their tendency to produce unrealistic outputs when the reward signal is weak. REGUIDANCE provides a relatively simple and computationally inexpensive way to mitigate this issue, making it a practical tool for researchers and practitioners. The theoretical guarantees, although limited in scope, offer valuable insights into the workings of DPS and suggest avenues for further research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** REGUIDANCE is straightforward to implement and can be readily integrated with existing diffusion-based solvers.
    *   **Empirical Results:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of REGUIDANCE across a range of challenging inverse problems. The qualitative examples are particularly compelling.
    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** The theoretical analysis provides a rigorous justification for the algorithm's behavior in a simplified setting.
    *   **Strong Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods and motivates the need for REGUIDANCE.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Scope:** The theoretical analysis is restricted to a simple Gaussian mixture model, which may not fully capture the complexities of real-world data distributions. The assumptions are strong and don't fully translate to the more complex image datasets used.
    *   **Dependency on DPS:** The method is built on top of DPS, so it relies on DPS's quality and any inherent biases in DPS will influence REGUIDANCE's outcome.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions that the performance of REGUIDANCE can be sensitive to factors such as the choice of the ODE solver and the discretization error. This could limit its applicability in certain scenarios. Some description of parameter tuning could strengthen the paper.
    *  **Ablation studies**: The paper lacks experiments where the contribution of the method comes only from the reverse process step. The importance of each step in the method are not tested and proved in isolation.

*   **Potential Influence:** REGUIDANCE has the potential to influence future research in diffusion-based inverse problem solving by highlighting the importance of latent initialization and providing a concrete approach for improving sample quality. The theoretical analysis could also spur further investigations into the behavior of DPS and other diffusion-based algorithms.

*   **Score:** 6

*   **Rationale:** REGUIDANCE makes a solid contribution to the field of diffusion models for inverse problems. It offers a practical and demonstrably effective technique, accompanied by a initial theoretical analysis. However, the limitations of the theoretical analysis and the dependency on specific implementations of DPS somewhat temper the overall impact. I am also factoring in some lack of important experimental results on different modules, like ablation studies and sensitivity analysis. The method definitely has practical utility, and for that the paper deserves a good score. The theoretical analysis is an important step forward, and for that the paper deserves to be highly recognized as well.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Understanding In-Context Learning on Structured Manifolds: Bridging Attention to Kernel Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10959v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaiming Shen, Alexander Hsu, Rongjie Lai, Wenjing Liao
- **Abstract**: While in-context learning (ICL) has achieved remarkable success in natural language and vision domains, its theoretical understanding--particularly in the context of structured geometric data--remains unexplored. In this work, we initiate a theoretical study of ICL for regression of H\"older functions on manifolds. By establishing a novel connection between the attention mechanism and classical kernel methods, we derive generalization error bounds in terms of the prompt length and the number of training tasks. When a sufficient number of training tasks are observed, transformers give rise to the minimax regression rate of H\"older functions on manifolds, which scales exponentially with the intrinsic dimension of the manifold, rather than the ambient space dimension. Our result also characterizes how the generalization error scales with the number of training tasks, shedding light on the complexity of transformers as in-context algorithm learners. Our findings provide foundational insights into the role of geometry in ICL and novels tools to study ICL of nonlinear models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper initiates a theoretical study of in-context learning (ICL) by Transformers for regression of Hölder functions on manifolds. It establishes a novel connection between the attention mechanism in Transformers and classical kernel methods.  The paper derives generalization error bounds for transformer-based ICL, expressed in terms of prompt length and the number of training tasks. A key finding is that, with sufficient training tasks, Transformers can achieve the minimax regression rate for Hölder functions on manifolds, scaling exponentially with the intrinsic dimension of the manifold rather than the ambient space dimension.  The paper also characterizes how the generalization error scales with the number of training tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:
    *   **Theoretical framework for ICL on manifolds:** It's one of the first works to provide a theoretical understanding of ICL when the data possesses a manifold structure, a common assumption in many machine learning applications.
    *   **Connection between attention and kernel methods:** The paper convincingly bridges the gap between attention mechanisms and kernel methods, offering a valuable perspective on how Transformers perform ICL.
    *   **Dimension dependence:** The result showing that the error depends on the intrinsic dimension of the manifold rather than the ambient dimension is a significant contribution. This directly addresses a key advantage of using geometric priors.
    *   **Tightness result**: Establishes Transformer achieve near-optimal regression rate when provided with sufficient number of tasks.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Theoretical understanding of ICL:** ICL is a crucial capability of modern LLMs, but its theoretical foundations are still lacking. This work offers valuable insights into the mechanisms behind ICL, particularly in the context of structured data.
    *   **Practical implications:** The theoretical results could inform the design and optimization of Transformers for tasks where data lies on a manifold, potentially leading to more efficient and accurate models.
    *   **New tool to study ICL**: The kernel framework allows ICL to be analyzed using classic tools from non-parametric statistics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of ICL for manifold regression and sets up appropriate assumptions.
    *   **Rigorous analysis:** The proofs and theoretical arguments appear to be sound and well-structured.
    *   **Relevant connections:** The paper makes strong connections to existing work in kernel methods and manifold learning, placing the results in a broader context.
    *   **Clear writing**: The paper is clearly written and organized, making it easy to follow the arguments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assumptions:** The paper relies on several simplifying assumptions, such as the Hölder smoothness of functions, a uniform distribution on the manifold, and exact kernel implementation via attention. While these assumptions are necessary for the theoretical analysis, they limit the applicability of the results to real-world scenarios.
    *   **Complexity:** The analysis is quite involved, which might make it less accessible to researchers without a strong theoretical background.
    *   **Practical validation:** The paper lacks empirical validation of the theoretical findings. While the theoretical results are significant, it would be valuable to see how well they align with experimental results on real datasets.
    *   **Scope**: Focus on regression.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could serve as a foundation for future theoretical research on ICL, encouraging further investigations into the role of geometry, kernel methods, and manifold learning.
    *   The insights gained could inspire new architectural designs for Transformers that better leverage geometric priors.
    *   The work could contribute to a deeper understanding of the generalization properties of large language models, leading to more reliable and predictable performance.

**Justification for the score:**

Given the novelty and significance, the solid theoretical foundation, and the potential for future research, the paper warrants a high score. However, the simplifying assumptions and lack of empirical validation temper the overall impact. The link to classical kernel methods is significant. The dependence on the intrinsic dimension is important. However, the assumptions are strong. Also this paper is a bit incremental since recent work are also trying to establish tight rate for ICL (see https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.15360).

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ChineseHarm-Bench: A Chinese Harmful Content Detection Benchmark
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10960v1)
- **Authors**: Kangwei Liu, Siyuan Cheng, Bozhong Tian, Xiaozhuan Liang, Yuyang Yin, Meng Han, Ningyu Zhang, Bryan Hooi, Xi Chen, Shumin Deng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have been increasingly applied to automated harmful content detection tasks, assisting moderators in identifying policy violations and improving the overall efficiency and accuracy of content review. However, existing resources for harmful content detection are predominantly focused on English, with Chinese datasets remaining scarce and often limited in scope. We present a comprehensive, professionally annotated benchmark for Chinese content harm detection, which covers six representative categories and is constructed entirely from real-world data. Our annotation process further yields a knowledge rule base that provides explicit expert knowledge to assist LLMs in Chinese harmful content detection. In addition, we propose a knowledge-augmented baseline that integrates both human-annotated knowledge rules and implicit knowledge from large language models, enabling smaller models to achieve performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLMs. Code and data are available at https://github.com/zjunlp/ChineseHarm-bench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChineseHarm-Bench, a new benchmark dataset for Chinese harmful content detection. Unlike existing resources, which are often focused on English or limited in scope, ChineseHarm-Bench aims to be comprehensive, covering six representative categories of harmful content (gambling, pornography, abuse, fraud, illicit ads, and non-violation) and constructed entirely from real-world data. A key feature is the development of a knowledge rule base during the annotation process, providing explicit expert knowledge to assist LLMs.  The paper also proposes a knowledge-augmented baseline that combines human-annotated rules with implicit knowledge from large language models, enabling smaller models to achieve performance comparable to state-of-the-art LLMs. The authors conduct experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Positive:** The creation of a multi-category, professionally annotated benchmark specifically for Chinese harmful content detection is a significant contribution. The incorporation of real-world data is also a strength. The knowledge rule base generated during annotation is a valuable resource for both guiding future annotation efforts and augmenting model performance.
*   **Negative:** While the concept of using external knowledge to improve harmful content detection is not entirely new, the specific application to Chinese and the detailed process of knowledge rule base construction adds to the existing knowledge. While a notable step, similar studies are coming out more frequently. The novelty may be perceived as somewhat incremental rather than groundbreaking.

**Significance:**

*   **Positive:** Harmful content detection in Chinese is a crucial problem given the scale of the Chinese internet and the unique linguistic challenges it presents. The benchmark addresses a clear gap in existing resources and will likely stimulate further research in this area. The knowledge rule base can potentially serve as a valuable resource for improving the robustness and explainability of harmful content detection systems. Furthermore, the authors make the dataset publicly available, allowing for replicability.
*   **Negative:** While the experiments show that the knowledge-augmented baseline improves performance, the gain seems modest compared to the complexity involved. Also, while the scale is decent, there may be questions in how diverse the dataset is, potentially not encapsulating all cases. The comparison with state-of-the-art LLMs is limited to direct prompting and a single-model-derived synthetic dataset; more robust comparison could exist. The long-term impact of the work depends on the extent to which other researchers adopt and build upon the benchmark and rule base.

**Strengths:**

*   The multi-category benchmark is a valuable resource.
*   The knowledge rule base is a novel and useful addition.
*   Experiments demonstrate improved performance with the proposed baseline.
*   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology.
*   Focuses on a specific linguistic challenge.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Novelty, although there, is not ground breaking in the field.
*   Performance gains of the baseline are somewhat modest given the complexity.
*   Experimentation could include more diverse comparisons to the current literature.

**Potential Influence:**

The ChineseHarm-Bench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for Chinese harmful content detection. The knowledge rule base could also be used to improve the performance of existing systems. However, the actual influence will depend on the level of adoption by the research community and its influence on the evolution of new models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of harmful content detection by introducing a new, multi-category benchmark dataset for Chinese and a knowledge-augmented baseline. However, the novelty is incremental, and the observed performance gains of the baseline are somewhat modest. More experimentation could've pushed the score even further. The significance is clear given the need for such resources, and the paper has the potential to stimulate further research. It is also a very important ethical direction to tackle and provides helpful guidance for future work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SpectralAR: Spectral Autoregressive Visual Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10962v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanhui Huang, Weiliang Chen, Wenzhao Zheng, Yueqi Duan, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive visual generation has garnered increasing attention due to its scalability and compatibility with other modalities compared with diffusion models. Most existing methods construct visual sequences as spatial patches for autoregressive generation. However, image patches are inherently parallel, contradicting the causal nature of autoregressive modeling. To address this, we propose a Spectral AutoRegressive (SpectralAR) visual generation framework, which realizes causality for visual sequences from the spectral perspective. Specifically, we first transform an image into ordered spectral tokens with Nested Spectral Tokenization, representing lower to higher frequency components. We then perform autoregressive generation in a coarse-to-fine manner with the sequences of spectral tokens. By considering different levels of detail in images, our SpectralAR achieves both sequence causality and token efficiency without bells and whistles. We conduct extensive experiments on ImageNet-1K for image reconstruction and autoregressive generation, and SpectralAR achieves 3.02 gFID with only 64 tokens and 310M parameters. Project page: https://huang-yh.github.io/spectralar/.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper "SpectralAR: Spectral Autoregressive Visual Generation." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel autoregressive visual generation framework called SpectralAR. It addresses the limitations of existing autoregressive methods that rely on spatial patches, which inherently contradict the causal nature of autoregressive modeling. SpectralAR tackles this by operating in the spectral domain.  It converts images into 1D causal sequences of spectral tokens using a "Nested Spectral Tokenization" (NST) method. This NST represents images in a hierarchical coarse-to-fine manner based on frequency components. Lower frequencies representing the overall image structure are generated first, followed by higher frequencies adding finer details. The framework also employs a non-uniform token-frequency mapping, allocating more tokens to low-frequency components for better detail representation and fewer tokens to high-frequency components for efficiency. Experiments on ImageNet-1K demonstrate competitive performance compared to other methods in terms of FID score and token efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its application of spectral analysis to autoregressive visual generation. Existing autoregressive methods predominantly operate in the spatial domain. Moving to the spectral domain and tokenizing based on frequency components is a fresh perspective. The "Nested Spectral Tokenization" and the non-uniform token-frequency mapping are also specific contributions that improve token efficiency and maintain visual quality.

*   **Significance:** The significance comes from addressing a key limitation of spatial-based autoregressive methods – the violation of causality due to the inherent equality among image pixels/patches. By operating in the spectral domain, the paper introduces a more natural causal structure, where low-frequency (overall structure) components precede high-frequency (details) components. Furthermore, the method achieves competitive results with fewer tokens, which is crucial for scalability and efficiency, particularly in multimodal applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Approach:**  The approach is well-motivated by the properties of images in the spectral domain (power-law distribution) and human visual perception.
    *   **Token Efficiency:** The non-uniform token mapping allows for a significant reduction in the number of tokens without severely impacting image quality.
    *   **Causality:**  The spectral perspective and causal masking improves the causal relationships and efficiency in autoregressive generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:**  The method involves multiple steps: DCT, nested tokenization, vector quantization, and transformer-based generation. This added complexity could be seen as a barrier to adoption.
    *   **Tokenizer's Reconstruction Performance:**  The paper admits that the tokenizer's reconstruction performance (rFID) is inferior to TiTok.  While the autoregressive generation results are competitive, a stronger tokenizer would likely further improve the overall performance.
    *   **Limited Applications:** While the paper briefly mentions applications like super-resolution and truncated generation, a more in-depth exploration of these and other applications would strengthen the paper.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a new direction for autoregressive visual generation. The spectral approach could inspire further research on leveraging frequency domain properties for other vision tasks. The token efficiency and causality advantages are also valuable contributions that could be adopted in future models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to autoregressive visual generation with clear benefits in causality and token efficiency. While the tokenizer's reconstruction performance could be improved, the competitive generation results and the potential for future applications justify a solid score.  It's not a groundbreaking revolution, but a significant and well-reasoned advancement in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MMMG: A Massive, Multidisciplinary, Multi-Tier Generation Benchmark for Text-to-Image Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10963v2)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Luo, Yuhui Yuan, Junwen Chen, Haonan Cai, Ziyi Yue, Yuwei Yang, Fatima Zohra Daha, Ji Li, Zhouhui Lian
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we introduce knowledge image generation as a new task, alongside the Massive Multi-Discipline Multi-Tier Knowledge-Image Generation Benchmark (MMMG) to probe the reasoning capability of image generation models. Knowledge images have been central to human civilization and to the mechanisms of human learning -- a fact underscored by dual-coding theory and the picture-superiority effect. Generating such images is challenging, demanding multimodal reasoning that fuses world knowledge with pixel-level grounding into clear explanatory visuals. To enable comprehensive evaluation, MMMG offers 4,456 expert-validated (knowledge) image-prompt pairs spanning 10 disciplines, 6 educational levels, and diverse knowledge formats such as charts, diagrams, and mind maps. To eliminate confounding complexity during evaluation, we adopt a unified Knowledge Graph (KG) representation. Each KG explicitly delineates a target image's core entities and their dependencies. We further introduce MMMG-Score to evaluate generated knowledge images. This metric combines factual fidelity, measured by graph-edit distance between KGs, with visual clarity assessment. Comprehensive evaluations of 16 state-of-the-art text-to-image generation models expose serious reasoning deficits -- low entity fidelity, weak relations, and clutter -- with GPT-4o achieving an MMMG-Score of only 50.20, underscoring the benchmark's difficulty. To spur further progress, we release FLUX-Reason (MMMG-Score of 34.45), an effective and open baseline that combines a reasoning LLM with diffusion models and is trained on 16,000 curated knowledge image-prompt pairs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MMMG: A Massive, Multidisciplinary, Multi-Tier Generation Benchmark for Text-to-Image Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task called knowledge image generation, where models are given a concise text prompt and must generate an informative image (diagram, chart, etc.) that reflects the knowledge domain implied by the prompt. To facilitate research in this area, the authors present the Massive Multi-Discipline Multi-Tier Knowledge-Image Generation Benchmark (MMMG). This benchmark contains 4,456 expert-validated image-prompt pairs spanning 10 disciplines and 6 educational levels. Each sample includes a high-quality knowledge graph representation of the core entities and dependencies in the target image. The paper also introduces a new metric, MMMG-Score, that combines factual fidelity (measured by graph-edit distance) with visual clarity assessment (using segmentation models). The paper presents comprehensive evaluations of 16 existing text-to-image models, demonstrating that current models struggle with the reasoning demands of this task. Finally, the authors release FLUX-Reason, a reasoning-enhanced text-to-image model, trained on curated data, as a new baseline for the task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is highly novel. Defining knowledge image generation as a specific research task, creating a benchmark with diverse domains and educational levels, developing a specialized evaluation metric that goes beyond standard image quality metrics, and releasing a new baseline model all represent substantial contributions. The focus on reasoning, which is currently lacking in most text-to-image evaluations, is a welcome addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the text-to-image generation field by focusing on reasoning. Knowledge images are crucial in communication and understanding, and creating models that can generate such images has wide-ranging applications (education, scientific communication, etc.).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Task:** The paper clearly defines the knowledge image generation task.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The MMMG benchmark has excellent properties: it is large-scale, multidisciplinary, multi-tiered, and features high-quality knowledge graph annotations.  The dataset's focus on reasoning, rather than just instruction following, is a significant strength.
    *   **Appropriate Evaluation Metric:** The MMMG-Score appropriately combines factual fidelity with visual clarity, addressing the shortcomings of standard metrics. The graph-edit distance provides a robust way to measure knowledge alignment.
    *   **Strong Baseline:** The released FLUX-Reason model provides a good starting point for future research.  The insights gleaned from training this model are also valuable.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of existing models, highlighting their limitations and offering valuable insights.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the motivations, methodology, and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Knowledge Graph Extraction Challenges:** The reliance on LLMs (OpenAI-03 in particular) for knowledge graph extraction introduces potential biases and inaccuracies. While expert validation mitigates this, it is still a source of potential error.  More robust knowledge extraction methods could improve future versions of the benchmark.
    *   **Visual Clarity metric simplicity**: The visual clarity metric focuses on segmentation numbers and region detection. While helpful, it might miss more subtle aspects of visual clarity like layout, color scheme, and visual hierarchy.
    *   **Computational Cost**: Training and evaluating models on this benchmark (particularly knowledge graph generation) may incur a substantial computational cost for researchers.
*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to significantly influence the text-to-image generation field. It redirects the community towards focusing on reasoning and knowledge representation, rather than solely on photorealism and compositionality. The benchmark is likely to become a valuable resource for evaluating future models.
*   **Room for Future Work:** The error analysis section points out avenues for further research. More sophisticated knowledge graph representations, improved visual clarity metrics, and new model architectures are all areas that future work can address.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by formally introducing and structuring the task of knowledge image generation, a task with high practical relevance. The MMMG benchmark is exceptionally valuable given its breadth, depth, and annotation quality. The introduction of MMMG-Score is crucial and addresses a need in the T2I space of having quantitative and explainable metrics. The weaknesses are minor compared to the overall contributions and offer clear directions for improvement. Therefore, while not a perfect paper, this is a very good and well-executed paper, thus it deserves the following.

### Beyond Attention or Similarity: Maximizing Conditional Diversity for Token Pruning in MLLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10967v1)
- **Authors**: Qizhe Zhang, Mengzhen Liu, Lichen Li, Ming Lu, Yuan Zhang, Junwen Pan, Qi She, Shanghang Zhang
- **Abstract**: In multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the length of input visual tokens is often significantly greater than that of their textual counterparts, leading to a high inference cost. Many works aim to address this issue by removing redundant visual tokens. However, current approaches either rely on attention-based pruning, which retains numerous duplicate tokens, or use similarity-based pruning, overlooking the instruction relevance, consequently causing suboptimal performance. In this paper, we go beyond attention or similarity by proposing a novel visual token pruning method named CDPruner, which maximizes the conditional diversity of retained tokens. We first define the conditional similarity between visual tokens conditioned on the instruction, and then reformulate the token pruning problem with determinantal point process (DPP) to maximize the conditional diversity of the selected subset. The proposed CDPruner is training-free and model-agnostic, allowing easy application to various MLLMs. Extensive experiments across diverse MLLMs show that CDPruner establishes new state-of-the-art on various vision-language benchmarks. By maximizing conditional diversity through DPP, the selected subset better represents the input images while closely adhering to user instructions, thereby preserving strong performance even with high reduction ratios. When applied to LLaVA, CDPruner reduces FLOPs by 95\% and CUDA latency by 78\%, while maintaining 94\% of the original accuracy. Our code is available at https://github.com/Theia-4869/CDPruner.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Attention or Similarity: Maximizing Conditional Diversity for Token Pruning in MLLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of high computational cost associated with the large number of visual tokens used in multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  It proposes a novel visual token pruning method called CDPruner, which aims to maximize the "conditional diversity" of retained tokens.  CDPruner first defines conditional similarity between visual tokens based on their relevance to the user's instruction (query). Then, it formulates token pruning as a determinantal point process (DPP) optimization problem to maximize the diversity of the selected token subset, taking into account the instruction relevance. The method is training-free and model-agnostic.  The authors demonstrate, through experiments on various MLLMs and vision-language benchmarks, that CDPruner achieves state-of-the-art performance, reducing FLOPs and latency while preserving accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper introduces a conceptually novel approach to visual token pruning.  While attention-based and similarity-based methods exist, this paper's key innovation lies in the "conditional diversity" concept. Combining feature similarity with user instruction relevance within a DPP framework for visual token pruning is a fresh perspective. Reformulating the pruning problem with determinantal point process, which facilitates dynamic pruning by jointly considering feature similarity and instruction relevance is another key aspect to its novelty.
*   **Significance:**  Reducing the computational cost of MLLMs is a significant problem hindering their wider adoption, particularly in resource-constrained environments. The paper makes a valuable contribution by offering a training-free and model-agnostic pruning solution. The reported performance gains (FLOPs reduction, latency reduction, and maintained accuracy) are considerable and demonstrate the practical potential of CDPruner.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel approach:**  The "conditional diversity" concept and its implementation via DPP are well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   **Training-free and model-agnostic:**  The method's simplicity and broad applicability across different MLLMs are significant advantages.
    *   **Strong experimental results:**  Extensive experiments across various benchmarks and MLLMs demonstrate state-of-the-art performance. The results show a marked improvement over existing token pruning methods, especially at high reduction ratios.
    *   **Efficiency gains:** Detailed analysis of FLOPS reduction, CUDA latency, and GPU memory usage are provided, highlighting practical benefits.
    * The paper is well-written and easy to understand.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on text embeddings and visual features:** The performance of CDPruner depends on the quality of the text embeddings and visual features.
    *   **Limited explanation on the balance factor:** The paper did not provide an in-depth explanation about the balance factor, how to derive it, and which criteria is good for which benchmark.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in MLLM efficiency and token pruning. The CDPruner approach can be adopted and extended by others, potentially leading to even more efficient and accurate MLLMs. It can also serve as a benchmark for future methods. Also, appropriate pruning may help mitigate hallucination in MLLMs, which we believe is a valuable direction for future research.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a strong, well-motivated approach with significant practical implications and thoroughly supported by experimental results. While there's room for further exploration of the balance factor and a more theoretical understanding of how different embeddings affect results, the paper's novelty, clarity, and impact on the field of MLLM efficiency warrant a high score. The CDPruner contributes a robust and easily deployable technique which reduces the computational load while preserving performance.

### What Exactly Does Guidance Do in Masked Discrete Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10971v1)
- **Authors**: He Ye, Rojas Kevin, Tao Molei
- **Abstract**: We study masked discrete diffusion models with classifier-free guidance (CFG). Assuming no score error nor discretization error, we derive an explicit solution to the guided reverse dynamics, so that how guidance influences the sampling behavior can be precisely characterized. When the full data distribution is a mixture over classes and the goal is to sample from a specific class, guidance amplifies class-specific regions while suppresses regions shared with other classes. This effect depends on the guidance strength $w$ and induces distinct covariance structures in the sampled distribution. Notably, we observe quantitatively different behaviors in $1$D and $2$D. We also show that for large $w$, the decay rate of the total variation ($\mathrm{TV}$) along the reverse dynamics is double-exponential in $w$ for both $1$D and $2$D. These findings highlight the role of guidance, not just in shaping the output distribution, but also in controlling the dynamics of the sampling trajectory. Our theoretical analysis is supported by experiments that illustrate the geometric effects of guidance and its impact on convergence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper theoretically analyzes classifier-free guidance (CFG) in masked discrete diffusion models, focusing on low-dimensional settings (1D and 2D). Assuming no score approximation or discretization errors, the authors derive explicit formulas for the guided reverse dynamics and analyze how guidance affects the sampled distributions and the convergence rates.  Key findings include: guidance amplifies class-specific regions and suppresses overlaps between classes, the strength of this effect depending on the guidance weight; the covariance structure of the sampled distribution differs between 1D and 2D; and the total variation (TV) distance decays double-exponentially with the guidance strength for large guidance weights. The theoretical analysis is supported by experimental results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While CFG is widely used in continuous diffusion models, the theoretical analysis of its effects in *discrete* diffusion models, specifically the masked variant, represents a valuable contribution. Deriving explicit formulas for the reverse dynamics and analyzing their properties is novel, especially given the distinct characteristics of discrete spaces (compared to continuous ones, such as the absence of gradients). The double-exponential decay rate result is also a novel and potentially useful finding for the field. The nuanced differences in the behavior of CFG in 1D vs 2D also adds a level of novelty.
* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a significant gap in the theoretical understanding of guidance in discrete diffusion. By characterizing how guidance reshapes the output distribution and affects convergence, the paper can help to design better discrete diffusion models, potentially improving sample quality and controllability in applications like text generation, molecule design, and controlled molecule design.  The explicit formulas can be useful for practitioners to reason about the effect of guidance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous mathematical analysis:** The authors provide detailed proofs and explicit formulas.
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly poses questions about the effect of guidance.
    * **Comprehensive treatment:** The analysis addresses both the distributional effects and the convergence dynamics.
    * **Support from experiments:** The experiments are consistent with the theoretical findings.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Idealized assumptions:** The assumption of perfect score approximation and absence of numerical errors is a strong one.  In practice, score approximation errors and numerical integration inaccuracies are often significant. The derived results may not directly translate to real-world scenarios where these errors are present.  The low-dimensional focus limits the generalizability of some findings.
    * **Limited Scope:**  The paper focuses on *masked* discrete diffusion models.  The results may not directly apply to other discrete diffusion formulations. The transformer model and Tau-leaping scheme may also introduce some bias in the results.
* **Potential Impact:** The paper can potentially influence future research by:
    * Providing a theoretical foundation for understanding CFG in discrete diffusion.
    * Guiding the development of new guidance techniques tailored to discrete spaces.
    * Inspiring further investigation into the effects of score approximation and numerical errors.
    * Serving as a starting point for analyzing CFG in higher-dimensional discrete spaces.
* **Justification:** The paper does make non-trivial contributions to understanding the impact of CFG on masked discrete diffusion models. The derivation of explicit formulas allows for deeper insight than is currently available in existing literature. The paper is limited by its assumptions of perfect score approximation and perfect numerical integration, as well as by the focus on a relatively simple setting in one and two dimensions. Nevertheless, the paper introduces solid mathematical analysis of practical relevance and adds theoretical insights. The experimental results partially validate the theoretical conclusions despite these limitations.

### Farseer: A Refined Scaling Law in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10972v1)
- **Authors**: Houyi Li, Wenzhen Zheng, Qiufeng Wang, Zhenyu Ding, Haoying Wang, Zili Wang, Shijie Xuyang, Ning Ding, Shuigeng Zhou, Xiangyu Zhang, Daxin Jiang
- **Abstract**: Training Large Language Models (LLMs) is prohibitively expensive, creating a critical scaling gap where insights from small-scale experiments often fail to transfer to resource-intensive production systems, thereby hindering efficient innovation. To bridge this, we introduce Farseer, a novel and refined scaling law offering enhanced predictive accuracy across scales. By systematically constructing a model loss surface $L(N,D)$, Farseer achieves a significantly better fit to empirical data than prior laws (e.g., Chinchilla's law). Our methodology yields accurate, robust, and highly generalizable predictions, demonstrating excellent extrapolation capabilities, improving upon Chinchilla's law by reducing extrapolation error by 433\%. This allows for the reliable evaluation of competing training strategies across all $(N,D)$ settings, enabling conclusions from small-scale ablation studies to be confidently extrapolated to predict large-scale performance. Furthermore, Farseer provides new insights into optimal compute allocation, better reflecting the nuanced demands of modern LLM training. To validate our approach, we trained an extensive suite of approximately 1,000 LLMs across diverse scales and configurations, consuming roughly 3 million NVIDIA H100 GPU hours. We are comprehensively open-sourcing all models, data, results, and logs at https://github.com/Farseer-Scaling-Law/Farseer to foster further research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Farseer," a refined scaling law for large language models (LLMs) designed to improve predictive accuracy across different scales of model size (N) and training data (D). The authors argue that existing scaling laws, like Chinchilla's, have limitations in capturing the complex interaction between model size and data scaling, particularly when extrapolating to larger scales. Farseer addresses this by systematically constructing a model loss surface, *L(N, D)*, that achieves a better fit to empirical data, demonstrated through an extensive suite of experiments involving approximately 1,000 LLMs. The paper claims superior extrapolation capabilities, better insights into optimal compute allocation, and open-sources all models, data, and results to foster further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a significant improvement over existing scaling laws, most notably by reducing extrapolation error by a substantial margin.  The core novelty lies in the differential piecewise fitting approach, allowing for explicit N-dependent data scaling.  This goes beyond simply fitting a new curve; it represents a more nuanced understanding of how model size and data volume interact. The high accuracy and robustness compared to Chinchilla are clearly demonstrated and are compelling.

* **Significance:** If the claims hold up under further scrutiny (particularly by the wider research community that now has access to the data), Farseer could significantly impact the way LLMs are trained and evaluated. It enables more reliable prediction of large-scale performance from small-scale experiments, effectively bridging the critical scaling gap that hinders innovation.  The improved compute guidance has the potential to make LLM training more efficient.  The comprehensive open-sourcing provides valuable resources for the community.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Validation:** The extensive experimentation (1,000 LLMs, 3 million GPU hours) provides solid support for the claims.
    * **Quantifiable Improvements:** The paper provides clear metrics (reduction in extrapolation error) to demonstrate the superiority of Farseer.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The differential piecewise fitting approach is explained in detail, allowing for reproducibility and further development.
    * **Open-Sourcing:** Releasing the data and models will enable the community to validate the claims and build upon the work.
    * **Detailed Ablation Studies:** Rigorous exploration of model properties (robustness, data distribution generalization, monotonicity, and the impact of embedding layers).

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on a Specific Architecture:** The study primarily focuses on LLaMA-style models. The extent to which Farseer generalizes to other architectures (e.g., different attention mechanisms, MoE models as mentioned, or alternative decoder structures) needs further investigation. The scaling laws may require re-fitting and could change.
    * **Limited Theoretical Explanation:** While the paper provides a robust empirical framework, it lacks a deep theoretical explanation for *why* Farseer works better. Understanding the underlying mechanisms driving the improved performance would add greater value to the work. It is, fundamentally, a curve-fitting approach, albeit a very sophisticated one.
    * **Validation Remains Limited to BPC:** While the detailed quality controls on the BPC metric are appreciated, the long-term effects of applying this law have not been evaluated. Has the BPC metric improved in real-world downstream tasks, when using models trained under this law?

* **Potential Influence:** The impact of the paper is potentially high. It could become a standard tool for predicting LLM performance and guiding compute allocation.  The open-sourced data and models will likely spur further research in scaling laws and LLM training.

* **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a *significant* empirical advancement with potentially high practical impact. The novelty in the methodology and the rigorous validation, coupled with the open-sourcing, strongly justify a high score. However, the limitations regarding architectural generalization and the lack of a deeper theoretical explanation prevent it from reaching the absolute top tier.

### DiffPR: Diffusion-Based Phase Reconstruction via Frequency-Decoupled Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11183v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Zhang
- **Abstract**: Oversmoothing remains a persistent problem when applying deep learning to off-axis quantitative phase imaging (QPI). End-to-end U-Nets favour low-frequency content and under-represent fine, diagnostic detail. We trace this issue to spectral bias and show that the bias is reinforced by high-level skip connections that feed high-frequency features directly into the decoder. Removing those deepest skips thus supervising the network only at a low resolution significantly improves generalisation and fidelity. Building on this insight, we introduce DiffPR, a two-stage frequency-decoupled framework. Stage 1: an asymmetric U-Net with cancelled high-frequency skips predicts a quarter-scale phase map from the interferogram, capturing reliable low-frequency structure while avoiding spectral bias. Stage 2: the upsampled prediction, lightly perturbed with Gaussian noise, is refined by an unconditional diffusion model that iteratively recovers the missing high-frequency residuals through reverse denoising. Experiments on four QPI datasets (B-Cell, WBC, HeLa, 3T3) show that DiffPR outperforms strong U-Net baselines, boosting PSNR by up to 1.1 dB and reducing MAE by 11 percent, while delivering markedly sharper membrane ridges and speckle patterns. The results demonstrate that cancelling high-level skips and delegating detail synthesis to a diffusion prior is an effective remedy for the spectral bias that limits conventional phase-retrieval networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DiffPR: Diffusion-Based Phase Reconstruction via Frequency-Decoupled Learning" addresses the issue of oversmoothing in deep learning models applied to off-axis quantitative phase imaging (QPI).  The authors identify spectral bias, exacerbated by high-level skip connections in U-Nets, as the primary cause. They propose a two-stage framework, DiffPR.  The first stage uses an asymmetric U-Net without high-frequency skip connections to predict a low-resolution phase map.  The second stage leverages an unconditional diffusion model to refine this low-resolution map, generating the missing high-frequency details. Experimental results on four QPI datasets demonstrate improved performance over standard U-Net baselines, evidenced by higher PSNR, lower MAE, and visually sharper reconstructions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of techniques to address a well-known problem (spectral bias) in a specific application domain (QPI). The *frequency decoupling* concept is not entirely novel in image processing (e.g., multiresolution analysis), but its application and justification within the context of deep learning for QPI are new. Critically, the insightful observation that deep *skip connections* can hurt results (by directly feeding error-prone high-frequency information to the decoder) is a key contribution that goes against standard U-Net wisdom.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the quality of phase reconstructions in QPI, which has applications in live cell imaging, morphology analysis, and cellular mechanism studies. Better reconstructions could lead to more accurate diagnoses and a deeper understanding of biological processes. The paper also adds to the growing body of literature applying diffusion models to inverse problems. The improvement in performance, while measurable (up to 1.1 dB PSNR and 11% MAE reduction), needs to be considered in the context of the specific needs of the QPI community. Whether this translates to a substantial change in clinical diagnosis capabilities is not explored.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    *   The identification of spectral bias and the detrimental effect of high-level skip connections is a valuable contribution.
    *   The DiffPR framework is well-described and relatively easy to understand.
    *   The experimental results are convincing, demonstrating consistent improvements over baseline methods across multiple datasets.
    *   The paper includes qualitative results that visually highlight the benefits of DiffPR, showing sharper details and reduced artifacts.
    *   The connection to recent literature regarding the spectral bias of diffusion models is well noted, and the link to reverse autoregression is well drawn and presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the authors discuss inference time in the conclusion, this is not mentioned elsewhere. It is likely that the addition of a second denoising stage (even if it is unconditional) will impact the time taken for inference. A discussion of this impact is important.
    *   The gains while quantifiable, and improved, may be limited or less important for other medical imaging modalities or applications, so the impact on other forms of image reconstruction are less certain.
    *   The paper lacks a thorough analysis of the computational cost associated with the diffusion model, which can be significant.
    *   The specific architecture choices for the U-Nets (number of layers, filter sizes, etc.) are not justified in detail. A sensitivity analysis exploring the impact of these choices would strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper assumes that the optimal approach is to down-sample the input data - i.e. reducing the feature size by four. There are other options for retaining the feature resolution and detail, and an argument for choosing the 4-times down-sampling would be more robust.
    *   A more detailed comparison with other methods that address spectral bias (e.g., using Fourier features) would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on deep learning for QPI by encouraging the exploration of frequency-decoupled architectures and the use of diffusion models for detail enhancement. The finding regarding the negative impact of high-level skip connections may also prompt a re-evaluation of U-Net architectures in other image reconstruction tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the paper's novelty in combining frequency decoupling with diffusion priors for QPI, its potential impact on improving phase reconstruction quality, and the insightful finding regarding skip connections, but also acknowledging its limitations regarding computational cost analysis, detailed architectural justification, and more complete comparisons to existing spectral bias mitigation methods, a score of **7** is appropriate. The paper presents a solid contribution with the potential to advance the field, but further analysis and broader applicability need to be demonstrated.

### LLM-as-a-Fuzzy-Judge: Fine-Tuning Large Language Models as a Clinical Evaluation Judge with Fuzzy Logic
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11221v1)
- **Authors**: Weibing Zheng, Laurah Turner, Jess Kropczynski, Murat Ozer, Tri Nguyen, Shane Halse
- **Abstract**: Clinical communication skills are critical in medical education, and practicing and assessing clinical communication skills on a scale is challenging. Although LLM-powered clinical scenario simulations have shown promise in enhancing medical students' clinical practice, providing automated and scalable clinical evaluation that follows nuanced physician judgment is difficult. This paper combines fuzzy logic and Large Language Model (LLM) and proposes LLM-as-a-Fuzzy-Judge to address the challenge of aligning the automated evaluation of medical students' clinical skills with subjective physicians' preferences. LLM-as-a-Fuzzy-Judge is an approach that LLM is fine-tuned to evaluate medical students' utterances within student-AI patient conversation scripts based on human annotations from four fuzzy sets, including Professionalism, Medical Relevance, Ethical Behavior, and Contextual Distraction. The methodology of this paper started from data collection from the LLM-powered medical education system, data annotation based on multidimensional fuzzy sets, followed by prompt engineering and the supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of the pre-trained LLMs using these human annotations. The results show that the LLM-as-a-Fuzzy-Judge achieves over 80\% accuracy, with major criteria items over 90\%, effectively leveraging fuzzy logic and LLM as a solution to deliver interpretable, human-aligned assessment. This work suggests the viability of leveraging fuzzy logic and LLM to align with human preferences, advances automated evaluation in medical education, and supports more robust assessment and judgment practices. The GitHub repository of this work is available at https://github.com/2sigmaEdTech/LLMAsAJudge
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "LLM-as-a-Fuzzy-Judge," a novel framework that uses fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) and fuzzy logic to evaluate medical students' clinical communication skills. The framework aims to provide automated clinical skill assessment that aligns with the nuanced judgment of human physicians. It works by fine-tuning an LLM on a dataset of medical student-AI patient conversations annotated with fuzzy sets representing "Professionalism," "Medical Relevance," "Ethical Behavior," and "Contextual Distraction." The results show that the fine-tuned LLM achieves a high accuracy in evaluating student utterances and aligns well with human judgments. The approach combines supervised fine-tuning and prompt engineering to improve model performance and interpretability, ultimately suggesting a scalable and human-aligned method for automated evaluation in medical education.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining LLMs with fuzzy logic for nuanced clinical skill assessment is relatively novel. Traditional methods for evaluation are often binary (Pass/Fail), while LLMs are often fine-tuned with single labels. This work attempts to address this limitation by using fuzzy logic which allows the model to better capture the spectrum of physician judgement. The hybrid approach of incorporating both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and prompt engineering to improve the model's performance is a valid approach.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a real-world problem in medical education - the difficulty of scaling and standardizing clinical skill evaluation while maintaining alignment with human expert judgment. If the approach is robust, it could significantly reduce the burden on human evaluators and provide more consistent and scalable feedback to medical students. Automated evaluation of medical education is essential for the integration of virtual education systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined methodology. The paper explains the process of data collection, annotation, model fine-tuning, and evaluation in detail.
    *   Quantitative results are provided with a thorough analysis of metrics across the four fuzzy criteria.
    *   Qualitative analysis provides some case studies which help to illustrate how the model is able to capture nuanced judgments.
    *   Addresses an important practical problem in medical education.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited scope of the fuzzy sets. Professionalism, Medical Relevance, Ethical Behavior, and Contextual Distraction are limited fuzzy sets and do not cover all possible criteria in an evaluation of medical students' clinical skills.
    *   The approach relies heavily on the quality of the annotated data. The expert annotation is time-consuming and can be subjective and difficult. The paper mentions that some criteria set levels are raw and data points are small, with some differing opinions by judges.
    *   The improvement over baseline is good but not excellent. While hybrid models perform well, achieving accuracy over 80% is expected.
    *   The long-term impact on learner outcomes and faculty assessment workflows is not evaluated in this study.
    *   Limited description of LLM infrastructure. The paper uses multiple LLMs but only provides a brief description of the LLMs, the hardware, and training process.

*   **Impact:**
    The paper proposes an interesting approach to improve automated medical education. If proven robust, the system may be a useful solution for the evaluation of student clinical communication skills.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of LLMs in education, with some limitations to the approach. While the idea of combining fuzzy logic with LLMs for nuanced evaluation is novel, the lack of improvement over baselines and need for expert annotation suggest that a lot of improvement is still needed.
