### No Universal Prompt: Unifying Reasoning through Adaptive Prompting for Temporal Table Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11246v1)
- **Authors**: Kushagra Dixit, Abhishek Rajgaria, Harshavardhan Kalalbandi, Dan Roth, Vivek Gupta
- **Abstract**: Temporal Table Reasoning is a critical challenge for Large Language Models (LLMs), requiring effective prompting techniques to extract relevant insights. Despite existence of multiple prompting methods, their impact on table reasoning remains largely unexplored. Furthermore, the performance of these models varies drastically across different table and context structures, making it difficult to determine an optimal approach. This work investigates multiple prompting technique across diverse table types to determine optimal approaches for different scenarios. We find that performance varies based on entity type, table structure, requirement of additional context and question complexity, with NO single method consistently outperforming others. To mitigate these challenges, we introduce SEAR, an adaptive prompting framework inspired by human reasoning that dynamically adjusts based on context characteristics and integrates a structured reasoning. Our results demonstrate that SEAR achieves superior performance across all table types compared to other baseline prompting techniques. Additionally, we explore the impact of table structure refactoring, finding that a unified representation enhances model's reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of temporal table reasoning for Large Language Models (LLMs), highlighting the difficulty of effectively prompting LLMs to extract relevant insights from tables that evolve over time. It demonstrates that no single prompting method consistently outperforms others across diverse table types, which vary based on entity type, table structure, context requirements, and question complexity. To mitigate these challenges, the authors introduce SEAR (Select-Elaborate-Answer & Reasoning), an adaptive prompting framework inspired by human reasoning. SEAR dynamically adjusts prompts based on context and integrates structured reasoning. Results demonstrate that SEAR achieves superior performance across various table types compared to baseline prompting techniques. The paper also explores the impact of table structure refactoring, finding that a unified representation enhances model reasoning. The study conducts systematic analyses across various table types, highlighting the impact of different reasoning strategies and structure modifications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its adaptive prompting framework, SEAR, and the investigation into table structure refactoring. While the individual components (e.g., chain-of-thought, program-of-thought) are not novel in themselves, the *dynamic selection and integration* of these components within SEAR is a valuable contribution. The unified refactoring approach, aiming for lossless transformation, is also a beneficial contribution for general use of tabular data with LLMs. The rigorous empirical demonstration of this adaptive approach across multiple datasets is strong. However, it builds primarily upon existing techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:
    *   **Addressing a Practical Problem:** Temporal table reasoning is a crucial capability for LLMs in many real-world applications (financial analysis, etc.).
    *   **Empirical Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive empirical evaluation of multiple prompting techniques on diverse tabular datasets. This helps understand the strengths and weaknesses of each approach and informs future research.
    *   **Practical Framework:** SEAR provides a practical framework for improving LLM performance on temporal table reasoning tasks. Its adaptability is valuable, and the consolidation into SEAR\_Unified has promise.
    *   **Table Refactoring Insight:** The insight that a unified representation can enhance model reasoning through refactoring is meaningful for data preprocessing.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is a major strength, covering a wide range of table types, LLMs, and prompting techniques.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** SEAR is well-defined and logically motivated.
    *   **Clear Writing and Presentation:** The paper is written clearly and logically.
    *   The comparison with more sophisticated modular reasoning techniques like Self-Discover is valuable, showing the advantage of an adaptive approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The core prompting techniques are not entirely new. SEAR combines existing methods.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework itself can be complex to implement and deploy, potentially limiting its accessibility to practitioners.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** As noted by the authors, the heavy reliance on In-Context Learning (ICL) raises concerns about the scalability of the approach to very large tables or datasets.
    *  **Generality of Refactoring:** While the results show an increase in performance from refactoring, the generalizability and automatic implementation of table refactoring for unseen table structures could be limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's focus on adaptive prompting and table refactoring will likely influence future research in this area.  The framework SEAR is well grounded, providing a strong approach for reasoning over tabular data for LLMs. The emphasis on understanding the limitations of existing methods and proposing a tailored solution is a good sign. It provides a benchmark to improve against.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of LLM-based table reasoning.  While SEAR builds on existing prompting techniques, the systematic analysis, the adaptive nature of the framework, and the investigation into table refactoring add significant value. The comprehensive empirical results strengthen the claims. However, the reliance on ICL and the incremental nature of the core techniques prevent a higher score. It has good potential influence, and builds on the existing tabular framework very well.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Time-Series Foundation Models Perform Building Energy Management Tasks?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11250v1)
- **Authors**: Ozan Baris Mulayim, Pengrui Quan, Liying Han, Xiaomin Ouyang, Dezhi Hong, Mario Bergés, Mani Srivastava
- **Abstract**: Building energy management (BEM) tasks require processing and learning from a variety of time-series data. Existing solutions rely on bespoke task- and data-specific models to perform these tasks, limiting their broader applicability. Inspired by the transformative success of Large Language Models (LLMs), Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs), trained on diverse datasets, have the potential to change this. Were TSFMs to achieve a level of generalizability across tasks and contexts akin to LLMs, they could fundamentally address the scalability challenges pervasive in BEM. To understand where they stand today, we evaluate TSFMs across four dimensions: (1) generalizability in zero-shot univariate forecasting, (2) forecasting with covariates for thermal behavior modeling, (3) zero-shot representation learning for classification tasks, and (4) robustness to performance metrics and varying operational conditions. Our results reveal that TSFMs exhibit \emph{limited} generalizability, performing only marginally better than statistical models on unseen datasets and modalities for univariate forecasting. Similarly, inclusion of covariates in TSFMs does not yield performance improvements, and their performance remains inferior to conventional models that utilize covariates. While TSFMs generate effective zero-shot representations for downstream classification tasks, they may remain inferior to statistical models in forecasting when statistical models perform test-time fitting. Moreover, TSFMs forecasting performance is sensitive to evaluation metrics, and they struggle in more complex building environments compared to statistical models. These findings underscore the need for targeted advancements in TSFM design, particularly their handling of covariates and incorporating context and temporal dynamics into prediction mechanisms, to develop more adaptable and scalable solutions for BEM.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score and a detailed justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "Can Time-Series Foundation Models Perform Building Energy Management Tasks?" investigates the applicability of Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) to Building Energy Management (BEM) tasks. It comprehensively evaluates TSFMs in four key dimensions: (1) zero-shot univariate forecasting, (2) forecasting with covariates, (3) zero-shot representation learning for classification tasks, and (4) robustness to various performance metrics and operational conditions. The authors compare several TSFMs (MOMENT, Chronos, TimesFM, TimeGPT, LagLlama, and Uni2TS) against statistical and physics-based baselines on datasets related to electricity consumption and indoor air temperature.  The study finds that TSFMs show limited generalizability and often underperform traditional methods, especially when dealing with covariates. They demonstrate potential for zero-shot representation learning in classification tasks but struggle with sensitivity to evaluation metrics and adaptability to diverse operational conditions.  The paper concludes that targeted advancements are needed in TSFM design, particularly in handling covariates and incorporating context, to make them truly effective for BEM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper offers a well-rounded examination of TSFMs, evaluating them across various tasks and conditions relevant to BEM.  It goes beyond simple univariate forecasting to consider the complexities of real-world BEM challenges.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The experimental setup is rigorous, with clear descriptions of datasets, models, evaluation metrics, and experimental procedures. Test-time fitting of traditional statistical models is a clever approach to create a strong baseline.
    *   **Detailed Analysis of Results:** The paper presents and interprets the results carefully, identifying both the strengths and limitations of the TSFMs.  The authors discuss the impact of dataset familiarity, covariate utilization, metric selection, and varying conditions on performance.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides clear directions for future research in TSFM design, highlighting the need for better covariate handling, context awareness, and task-agnostic reasoning capabilities.
    *   **Importance of Baselines:** The choice of strong baselines, including AutoARIMA, S-ARIMA, BestFit, R2C2, Random Forest, and established building thermal modeling approaches, is a key strength of the paper and adds credibility to the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in TSFM Application:**  While the comprehensive evaluation is valuable, the core *application* of existing TSFMs to BEM tasks is not inherently groundbreaking. Many of the TSFMs used are relatively new, and the findings essentially confirm their limitations when applied *out-of-the-box* to a complex domain.
    *   **Lack of Intervention:** The study is primarily observational. The authors do not propose or implement any novel modifications or improvements to the TSFMs themselves. The analysis reveals the shortfalls, but does not offer a solution beyond suggesting areas for future research.
    *   **Over-Emphasis on Zero-Shot:** A significant focus is placed on zero-shot performance, but the authors don't explore how targeted fine-tuning or transfer learning strategies might improve TSFM performance in BEM.  While zero-shot is desirable, it might be unrealistic to expect it to be a practical solution for complex applications.
    *   **Limited Scope of Datasets:** While the datasets used are relevant to BEM, expanding the scope to include more diverse building types, geographical locations, and data modalities could strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Some Subjective Thresholds:** Choosing thresholds for PRR is ad hoc; exploring multiple choices (rather than relying on a single "typical" setting) could improve the robustness of conclusions derived from this measure.
    *   **Closed Source Model:** The black-box nature of TimeGPT and its inclusion in the analysis presents a limited understanding of why it performs in particular ways.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a much-needed empirical assessment of the applicability of TSFMs to a real-world domain (BEM) that could benefit significantly from their success.
    *   The identification of specific shortcomings (covariate handling, context awareness) helps focus future research efforts on improving TSFMs for practical applications.
    *   The comparative analysis of TSFMs and traditional methods highlights the strengths of domain-specific approaches and provides valuable insights for practitioners in BEM.
    *   The study provides a template for evaluating the suitability of foundation models in other complex, data-rich domains.
    * The careful articulation of TSFMs' strengths and limitations across different datasets and sensors has the potential to promote more rigorous and careful model choices in practical use cases.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, I assign a score of **6**. The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of TSFMs for BEM tasks, identifying key limitations and suggesting future research directions. The findings are significant in guiding future development and application of TSFMs. However, the lack of direct intervention or novel methodological contribution and the primarily observational nature, reduce its impact. While the study demonstrates the promise of TSFMs and provides valuable insights, it is more of a thorough assessment than a groundbreaking innovation.
It is also worth noting that other papers applying the same core idea and using TSFMs to a similar setting may not present enough "new" information to merit publication. This paper, thus, provides a valuable service to the community by providing empirical benchmarks for which future BEM efforts may compare themselves against.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Gondola: Grounded Vision Language Planning for Generalizable Robotic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11261v1)
- **Authors**: Shizhe Chen, Ricardo Garcia, Paul Pacaud, Cordelia Schmid
- **Abstract**: Robotic manipulation faces a significant challenge in generalizing across unseen objects, environments and tasks specified by diverse language instructions. To improve generalization capabilities, recent research has incorporated large language models (LLMs) for planning and action execution. While promising, these methods often fall short in generating grounded plans in visual environments. Although efforts have been made to perform visual instructional tuning on LLMs for robotic manipulation, existing methods are typically constrained by single-view image input and struggle with precise object grounding. In this work, we introduce Gondola, a novel grounded vision-language planning model based on LLMs for generalizable robotic manipulation. Gondola takes multi-view images and history plans to produce the next action plan with interleaved texts and segmentation masks of target objects and locations. To support the training of Gondola, we construct three types of datasets using the RLBench simulator, namely robot grounded planning, multi-view referring expression and pseudo long-horizon task datasets. Gondola outperforms the state-of-the-art LLM-based method across all four generalization levels of the GemBench dataset, including novel placements, rigid objects, articulated objects and long-horizon tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Gondola: Grounded Vision Language Planning for Generalizable Robotic Manipulation" paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Gondola, a novel vision-language planning model for robotic manipulation that aims to improve generalization across unseen objects, environments, and tasks specified by diverse language instructions. Gondola takes multi-view images and history plans as input to produce the next action plan, including interleaved texts and segmentation masks of target objects and locations. The authors create three new datasets using the RLBench simulator to train Gondola.  The paper demonstrates Gondola's superior performance across all four generalization levels of the GemBench dataset compared to state-of-the-art LLM-based methods, including novel placements, rigid objects, articulated objects, and long-horizon tasks. The authors claim that the code, models, and datasets will be publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in combining multi-view image input, history-aware planning, and segmentation mask output within a VLM framework specifically fine-tuned for robotic manipulation. While previous works have explored VLMs for robotics, this paper's approach seems to be a comprehensive integration of different aspects of visual grounding. Building datasets with referring expressions and pseudo-long horizon tasks for improved object grounding and long-term planning is also a notable contribution. Gondola using Sa2VA and fine-tuning on robot grounded planning datasets is a contribution.
*   **Significance:** The ability to generalize across unseen objects, environments, and long-horizon tasks is a key challenge in robotic manipulation. If the reported results hold up in real-world experiments, Gondola could significantly advance the field by enabling robots to handle more complex and diverse tasks specified by natural language instructions. The release of the datasets would also contribute significantly to the community by providing resources for future research.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper tackles multiple aspects of the generalization problem (objects, environments, long-horizon tasks) in a unified framework.
    *   **Multi-View Input:** Addresses the limitations of single-view approaches in handling occlusions and limited fields of view.
    *   **Grounding with Segmentation Masks:** Provides more precise and actionable plans compared to coarser representations like bounding boxes or points.
    *   **History-Aware Planning:** Attempts to improve plan consistency by considering previous actions.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on GemBench, a established robotic manipulation benchmark.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulator Bias:** The majority of training data is generated in simulation. The sim-to-real gap remains a significant challenge, and the success of Gondola in real-world environments might be limited. Although the appendix shows that Gondola can handle real-world environments, it performs worse than in the simulator.
    *   **Limited Real-World Evaluation:** The paper reports experiments conducted in real robot settings in the supplementary material, it might not be extensive enough to demonstrate the robustness of the model in varied real-world conditions.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The use of large language models and segmentation models can be computationally expensive, limiting the applicability of the approach in resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Complex Dataset Construction**: It is not explicit whether the RLBench's label construction process had limitations due to regular expressions.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand the proposed method and the experimental results.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors promise to release code, models, and datasets which greatly increases the reproducibility of the work.

**Overall:**

Gondola addresses a significant problem in robotic manipulation with a novel and well-engineered approach. The empirical results are promising, but the dependence on simulation data and computational cost are potential limitations. The significance hinges on how well Gondola transfers to the real world and the impact of the released code and datasets on the community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a significant advancement towards more generalizable robotic manipulation through a comprehensive vision-language planning approach. The combined use of multi-view information, segmentation masks for grounding, and memory of previous history represents a novel way to tackle the generalization problem. However, the reliance on simulated data and limited real-world evaluations temper the enthusiasm. The score reflects the potential impact of the work, while acknowledging the need for further real-world validation.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Invocable APIs derived from NL2SQL datasets for LLM Tool-Calling Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11266v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Elder, Anupama Murthi, Jungkoo Kang, Ankita Rajaram Naik, Kiran Kate, Kinjal Basu, Danish Contractor
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are routinely deployed as agentic systems, with access to tools that interact with live environments to accomplish tasks. In enterprise deployments these systems need to interact with API collections that can be extremely large and complex, often backed by databases. In order to create datasets with such characteristics, we explore how existing NL2SQL (Natural Language to SQL query) datasets can be used to automatically create NL2API datasets. Specifically, this work describes a novel data generation pipeline that exploits the syntax of SQL queries to construct a functionally equivalent sequence of API calls. We apply this pipeline to one of the largest NL2SQL datasets, BIRD-SQL to create a collection of over 2500 APIs that can be served as invocable tools or REST-endpoints. We pair natural language queries from BIRD-SQL to ground-truth API sequences based on this API pool. We use this collection to study the performance of 10 public LLMs and find that all models struggle to determine the right set of tools (consisting of tasks of intent detection, sequencing with nested function calls, and slot-filling). We find that models have extremely low task completion rates (7-47 percent - depending on the dataset) which marginally improves to 50 percent when models are employed as ReACT agents that interact with the live API environment. The best task completion rates are far below what may be required for effective general-use tool-calling agents, suggesting substantial scope for improvement in current state-of-the-art tool-calling LLMs. We also conduct detailed ablation studies, such as assessing the impact of the number of tools available as well as the impact of tool and slot-name obfuscation. We compare the performance of models on the original SQL generation tasks and find that current models are sometimes able to exploit SQL better than APIs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of evaluating tool-calling capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in enterprise settings where interaction with complex API collections backed by databases is crucial. The authors propose a novel data generation pipeline that transforms existing NL2SQL datasets into NL2API datasets. They automatically construct functionally equivalent sequences of API calls from SQL queries, applied to the BIRD-SQL dataset. The resulting dataset consists of over 2500 APIs, which can serve as invocable tools. The paper evaluates the performance of 10 public LLMs on this dataset, finding that models struggle with tasks like intent detection, sequencing with nested function calls, and slot-filling, resulting in low task completion rates. The study also explores the impact of live API interaction through ReACT agents, tool availability, and tool name obfuscation. Finally, the paper compares the performance on the generated APIs with the original SQL generation task. All data and code is released for further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in its automated pipeline for converting NL2SQL datasets into NL2API datasets. While using existing datasets is not entirely new, the specific technique of deriving API call sequences from SQL queries and providing invocable APIs for LLM agents to interact with is a significant contribution. The creation of SLOT, SEL, and REST API versions offers different perspectives on the tool-calling problem.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is several-fold:
    *   It addresses a gap in existing LLM evaluation datasets, which often lack large-scale APIs backed by real-world databases.
    *   The generated dataset offers a standardized benchmark for evaluating tool-calling capabilities, with a focus on enterprise-relevant scenarios.
    *   The detailed ablation studies provide valuable insights into the challenges faced by LLMs in tool selection, sequencing, and the importance of semantic cues.
    *   The analysis comparing API performance with SQL generation highlights potential biases or strengths of LLMs toward different modes of interaction.
    *   The open release of data and code promotes replicability and further research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method provides a reusable strategy for quickly assembling many datasets, and the authors have already tested on several.
    *   The dataset addresses an important domain (database interaction) and a limitation in other datasets (lacking scale of real databases)
    *   The experiments are well designed and provide useful insight on what is and is not working with LLMs
    *   The error analysis and ablation studies are comprehensive and provide detailed insights into the limitations of current LLMs in this domain.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The generated APIs are tied to the structure of SQL queries, potentially limiting the diversity and complexity of tool interactions compared to real-world API collections.
    *   The reliance on a subset of SQL queries from the original dataset (due to limitations in the pipeline) may introduce selection bias.
    *   While the paper explores different API styles (SLOT, SEL, REST), the evaluation primarily focuses on performance metrics, with limited qualitative analysis of the generated API sequences.
    *   The limited scope of supported SQL Queries could have implications on the generalizability of the findings

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM evaluation by providing a novel and scalable method for generating NL2API datasets from existing NL2SQL resources. This approach addresses a significant gap in the availability of benchmarks for assessing tool-calling capabilities in enterprise-relevant settings. The ablation studies and detailed analysis offer valuable insights into the challenges faced by LLMs in tool selection and sequencing. While the generated APIs may be constrained by their SQL origins, the dataset provides a standardized and accessible resource for further research. Because the findings were of more scientific than engineering value, and some limitations on the findings were encountered, a moderately high score is assigned.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Domain-Constrained Diffusion Models to Synthesize Tabular Data: A Case Study in Power Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11281v1)
- **Authors**: Milad Hoseinpour, Vladimir Dvorkin
- **Abstract**: Growing concerns over privacy, security, and legal barriers are driving the rising demand for synthetic data across domains such as healthcare, finance, and energy. While generative models offer a promising solution to overcome these barriers, their utility depends on the incorporation of domain-specific knowledge. We propose to synthesize data using a guided diffusion model that integrates domain constraints directly into the generative process. We develop the model in the context of power systems, with potential applicability to other domains that involve tabular data. Specifically, we synthesize statistically representative and high-fidelity power flow datasets. To satisfy domain constraints, e.g., Kirchhoff laws, we introduce a gradient-based guidance to steer the sampling trajectory in a feasible direction. Numerical results demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a domain-constrained diffusion model for synthesizing tabular data, specifically focusing on power flow datasets.  The key idea is to incorporate domain-specific knowledge (specifically, power flow constraints like Kirchhoff's laws) directly into the diffusion model's sampling process. This is achieved by introducing a gradient-based guidance mechanism that steers the sampling trajectory towards feasible solutions.  The authors train the model on historical power flow data and demonstrate that the generated synthetic data is both statistically representative of real-world datasets and satisfies the physical constraints of power systems. They apply Riemannian gradient descent to minimize data consistency loss, ensuring feasibility of synthetic samples, resulting in better trained and safer AI models for the power grid.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies in the *integration of domain constraints directly into the diffusion model's sampling process* for tabular data synthesis. While diffusion models have been used for data generation in various domains, and physics-informed machine learning is not entirely new, the authors specifically address a critical challenge in power systems: ensuring that generated data respects physical laws. Existing generative models often struggle with this, leading to potentially unreliable or unsafe AI models. The application of Riemannian gradient descent to enforce consistency is a well-chosen and appropriate methodology. Furthermore, there seems to be no previous work exploring similar types of diffusion constraints for power flow equations.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the critical need for trustworthy AI models in power systems.  Generating synthetic power flow datasets that adhere to physical laws is essential for training robust and reliable machine learning models for tasks like optimal power flow (OPF) solving and grid stability analysis. The paper addresses a crucial gap, potentially leading to safer and more efficient power grid operations. The growing reliance on renewable energy sources increases complexity in these grids, so addressing the issue of data fidelity is highly relevant.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of generating high-quality, physically feasible power flow datasets.
*   **Novel Approach:** The gradient-based guidance mechanism is a well-motivated and novel approach to incorporating domain constraints into the diffusion model.
*   **Sound Methodology:**  The use of Riemannian gradient descent is a suitable technique for maintaining the integrity of the data manifold while enforcing constraints.
*   **Demonstrated Effectiveness:** The numerical results demonstrate that the proposed method generates synthetic data that is both statistically representative and physically feasible, outperforming unguided diffusion models in constraint satisfaction.
*   **Relevant Application:** The power systems domain is a high-impact application area where data quality and reliability are paramount.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to a PJM 5-bus system. While this provides a proof-of-concept, it is crucial to evaluate the model on larger and more complex power grids to assess its scalability and generalizability. It would be beneficial to show the results on larger datasets.
*   **Synthetic Ground Truth:** The real dataset is created synthetically. Comparing the diffusion model to real-world data would increase the paper's credibility. Real-world power flow data might contain complex correlations and anomalies that are not captured in the synthetically generated ground truth.
*   **Limited Comparison:** The comparison is only against an unguided diffusion model. Comparing with other constraint handling methods or alternative generative models (like GANs) that may have been adapted for constraint satisfaction could strengthen the paper.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions a hyperparameter λt, but a more detailed discussion about its tuning and sensitivity would be helpful.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the development of more trustworthy and reliable AI models in power systems. The proposed approach could be extended to other domains where domain constraints are critical for data quality and model reliability. It could also inspire further research into more efficient and scalable methods for incorporating domain knowledge into generative models.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the above points, the paper presents a valuable contribution with a novel approach to a relevant problem. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the synthetic nature of the real dataset somewhat limit the paper's immediate impact. A score of 7 reflects the paper's novelty and potential significance while acknowledging its weaknesses.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Joint Denoising of Cryo-EM Projection Images using Polar Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11283v1)
- **Authors**: Joakim Andén, Justus Sagemüller
- **Abstract**: Deep neural networks~(DNNs) have proven powerful for denoising, but they are ultimately of limited use in high-noise settings, such as for cryogenic electron microscopy~(cryo-EM) projection images. In this setting, however, datasets contain a large number of projections of the same molecule, each taken from a different viewing direction. This redundancy of information is useful in traditional denoising techniques known as class averaging methods, where images are clustered, aligned, and then averaged to reduce the noise level. We present a neural network architecture based on transformers that extends these class averaging methods by simultaneously clustering, aligning, and denoising cryo-EM images. Results on synthetic data show accurate denoising performance using this architecture, reducing the relative mean squared error (MSE) single-image DNNs by $45\%$ at a signal-to-noise (SNR) of $0.03$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Joint Denoising of Cryo-EM Projection Images using Polar Transformers":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel neural network architecture, the "Polar Transformer," for jointly denoising Cryo-EM projection images. The architecture builds upon traditional class averaging methods by simultaneously clustering, aligning, and denoising the images.  It leverages a polar representation of the images and a convolutional attention mechanism that ensures rotational equivariance. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of the Polar Transformer on synthetic data, showing a significant reduction in mean squared error compared to single-image denoising DNNs, especially at low signal-to-noise ratios (SNRs).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the Polar Transformer architecture itself. The combination of a polar representation with an attention mechanism specifically designed for rotational alignment in Cryo-EM images is a fresh approach. The paper's strength is in explicitly addressing the rotational symmetry inherent in the data distribution, which distinguishes it from more general-purpose denoising DNNs. The rotational equivariance property, achieved through the angular convolution, is a notable design feature.

*   **Significance:** Cryo-EM image denoising is a crucial step in the 3D structure determination pipeline, especially with the ever-increasing volume of data being generated. Improving denoising performance directly impacts the resolution and accuracy of the final 3D models. The authors rightly identify the limitations of traditional methods and generic DNNs in extremely noisy settings. If the proposed method scales to real-world data, it could have a meaningful impact on the field by enabling better use of noisy or incomplete datasets and accelerating the structure determination process.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly defined problem and approach:** The paper clearly outlines the challenges of Cryo-EM denoising and explains how the Polar Transformer addresses them.
    *   **Rigorous architecture:** The details of the Polar Transformer architecture, including the polar representation, angular convolutions, and attention mechanism, are well-described.
    *   **Performance on simulated data:** The results on synthetic datasets are compelling, showing significant improvements in denoising performance compared to baseline methods.
    *   **Rotational equivariance:** The paper explicitly addresses the rotational symmetry in Cryo-EM images, a key strength compared to generic denoising approaches.
    *  **Connection to Existing Methodologies:** The paper connects its work to both previous computational imaging methodologies and existing cryo-EM techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Evaluation on Synthetic Data Only:** The most significant weakness is the exclusive reliance on synthetic data. While the simulated data incorporates important aspects of the Cryo-EM imaging process, it cannot fully capture the complexities and artifacts present in real experimental datasets. The gap between simulated and real data in Cryo-EM can be substantial.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Scalability:** The paper mentions the challenge of scaling the model to larger datasets but provides limited details on how this can be addressed. Training complex transformers on large datasets is computationally intensive, and the authors need to provide insights into efficient training strategies.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** While the paper shows that the Polar Transformer outperforms other single image denoising methods, it would benefit from ablation studies to demonstrate the importance of individual components, like polar representation and angular attention.
    *   **Limited discussion of generalizability:** The authors do not explore the degree to which the model can generalize to experimental data without being retrained. This is an important factor in evaluating the efficacy of the proposed approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper introduces a promising new architecture for Cryo-EM image denoising.  If the Polar Transformer can be successfully scaled and adapted to real-world datasets, it could become a valuable tool for structural biologists, enabling them to obtain higher-resolution structures from noisy data. The explicit incorporation of rotational symmetry and joint denoising approach sets a good example for future research in this area.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a novel and potentially significant contribution to the field of Cryo-EM image processing.  However, the lack of validation on real experimental datasets is a major limitation.  The promising results on simulated data warrant further investigation, but the true impact of the Polar Transformer remains to be seen.
