- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### RollingQ: Reviving the Cooperation Dynamics in Multimodal Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11465v1)
- **Authors**: Haotian Ni, Yake Wei, Hang Liu, Gong Chen, Chong Peng, Hao Lin, Di Hu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal learning faces challenges in effectively fusing information from diverse modalities, especially when modality quality varies across samples. Dynamic fusion strategies, such as attention mechanism in Transformers, aim to address such challenge by adaptively emphasizing modalities based on the characteristics of input data. However, through amounts of carefully designed experiments, we surprisingly observed that the dynamic adaptability of widely-used self-attention models diminishes. Model tends to prefer one modality regardless of data characteristics. This bias triggers a self-reinforcing cycle that progressively overemphasizes the favored modality, widening the distribution gap in attention keys across modalities and deactivating attention mechanism's dynamic properties. To revive adaptability, we propose a simple yet effective method Rolling Query (RollingQ), which balances attention allocation by rotating the query to break the self-reinforcing cycle and mitigate the key distribution gap. Extensive experiments on various multimodal scenarios validate the effectiveness of RollingQ and the restoration of cooperation dynamics is pivotal for enhancing the broader capabilities of widely deployed multimodal Transformers. The source code is available at https://github.com/GeWu-Lab/RollingQ_ICML2025.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "RollingQ: Reviving the Cooperation Dynamics in Multimodal Transformer" addresses a key challenge in multimodal learning: the tendency of dynamic fusion mechanisms in Transformers to become overly reliant on a single modality, even when that modality is noisy or uninformative. The authors empirically observe this issue and attribute it to a self-reinforcing cycle, where an initial bias toward one modality leads to better feature extraction for that modality, which in turn reinforces its dominance in the attention mechanism. To counter this, they propose Rolling Query (RollingQ), a method that rotates the query vector towards an anchor point designed to promote attention towards the less-favored modality. By balancing attention scores, RollingQ aims to revive the intended dynamic adaptability of multimodal Transformers. Extensive experiments on several multimodal datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of RollingQ in improving performance and restoring cooperation dynamics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the identification of the "self-reinforcing cycle" phenomenon in the *dynamic fusion* of multimodal Transformers. While the issue of modality imbalance has been previously recognized in static fusion approaches, the paper makes a good case that the more flexible dynamic fusion can also become imbalanced. The *RollingQ* solution, while relatively simple, is a novel attempt to counteract this identified problem. The combination of identifying the specific "self-reinforcing" nature of imbalance within Transformers and proposing an attention-based method to mitigate the situation offers significant value.

*   **Significance:** Multimodal learning is becoming ever more important, particularly with the increase in availability of complex datasets. The finding that Transformers, a very popular technique for multimodal information processing, can fall prey to unintended imbalances has the potential to affect a significant area of research. If the findings are generally applicable, this approach could benefit all future work incorporating multimodal data. If RollingQ (or related methods) becomes the *de facto* standard for training, the paper will have had very high impact on the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper articulates the problem of diminishing dynamic adaptability with strong empirical evidence, and provides a very good overview of the relevant literature.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The authors present a well-reasoned theoretical explanation of the self-reinforcing cycle, which helps to understand the underlying mechanism.
    *   **Effective Solution:** RollingQ is a relatively simple and computationally efficient solution, making it potentially attractive for practical applications.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experiments across various datasets and fusion scenarios, demonstrating the effectiveness and robustness of RollingQ.  The ablations and visualizations add further support.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Theoretical Analysis:** While the theoretical analysis is good, it focuses primarily on a single self-attention layer. More complex models with multiple layers and different fusion architectures might exhibit different dynamics. Further expansion could improve the robustness of the claim.
    *   **Lack of Generalization Beyond Transformer Architectures:** The method is designed primarily for Transformer models. Its applicability to other types of multimodal fusion architectures might be limited, although RollingQ might prompt the invention of similar fixes for other architectures.
    *   **Dependence on Hyperparameters:** Like most learning systems, RollingQ has introduced at least one hyperparameter that needs to be appropriately set. It has the potential to be very sensitive to those settings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has strong potential to influence the field. It raises important questions about the behavior of attention mechanisms in multimodal Transformers and provides a practical solution to address the identified issue. The findings are likely to stimulate further research on improving the dynamic adaptability and robustness of multimodal learning models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and addressing a practical problem in multimodal Transformer learning. The novelty lies in the clear articulation of the "self-reinforcing cycle," supported by both empirical evidence and theoretical analysis, with a simple yet effective method provided. While further research will likely refine the approach, the paper's insights have strong potential to influence the field.

### A Gamified Evaluation and Recruitment Platform for Low Resource Language Machine Translation Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11467v1)
- **Authors**: Carlos Rafael Catalan
- **Abstract**: Human evaluators provide necessary contributions in evaluating large language models. In the context of Machine Translation (MT) systems for low-resource languages (LRLs), this is made even more apparent since popular automated metrics tend to be string-based, and therefore do not provide a full picture of the nuances of the behavior of the system. Human evaluators, when equipped with the necessary expertise of the language, will be able to test for adequacy, fluency, and other important metrics. However, the low resource nature of the language means that both datasets and evaluators are in short supply. This presents the following conundrum: How can developers of MT systems for these LRLs find adequate human evaluators and datasets? This paper first presents a comprehensive review of existing evaluation procedures, with the objective of producing a design proposal for a platform that addresses the resource gap in terms of datasets and evaluators in developing MT systems. The result is a design for a recruitment and gamified evaluation platform for developers of MT systems. Challenges are also discussed in terms of evaluating this platform, as well as its possible applications in the wider scope of Natural Language Processing (NLP) research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating machine translation (MT) systems for low-resource languages (LRLs), focusing on the scarcity of both datasets and human evaluators. It proposes a gamified evaluation and recruitment platform designed to connect developers of MT systems with native speakers of LRLs. The platform aims to facilitate the annotation and evaluation of MT outputs, using gamification techniques to enhance user engagement. A key feature is allowing evaluators to set their compensation (monetary, authorship etc.). The platform also aims to opensource the evaluated data. The paper details the platform's features, including a world map visualization, recruitment module, and evaluation module incorporating adequacy and fluency assessments. It also discusses the potential challenges, such as verifying annotator expertise, and suggests future work, including bias mitigation in text generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper identifies a genuine problem: the resource gap (both data and human expertise) in MT development for LRLs. While automated metrics are convenient, they often fail to capture the nuances of language, making human evaluation crucial. The idea of a platform specifically targeting this issue, and using gamification to incentivize participation, is a sound one.

However, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited.  Crowdsourcing platforms exist (e.g., Amazon Mechanical Turk, Clickworker) that *could* be used for similar tasks, even if they aren't specifically designed for it. The paper argues that its platform goes beyond simple outsourcing by fostering long-term collaboration and giving evaluators more control over compensation. While the *intention* to do this is clearly outlined in the description of the recruitment module, the implementation and features guaranteeing this are not explicitly laid out in the paper's design - meaning that it is still mainly aspirational rather than present in the platform's design.

A key strength of the paper is its detailed description of the proposed platform's components: the world map visualization, recruitment features, and evaluation module. The inclusion of specific UML diagrams (use case) and wireframes is helpful in understanding the envisioned functionality. However, the challenge of verifying annotator expertise is only briefly mentioned, and the proposed solution (relying on formal education certificates) is not particularly robust, especially given the informal nature of many LRLs. Another question would be to consider the viability of annotator certification and test (rather than just diploma) for such languages.

The claim of promoting "long term opportunities between the developer and native speaker for collaboration on other research projects" sounds optimistic but is never elaborated further. The main features, such as the ability to chat and share contact details, do not necessarily guarantee that such a connection will result in actual collaborations.

The future work section proposes sensible directions for development, including a pilot study in a smaller region and the integration of bias mitigation tasks. The suggestion of integrating GPTZero's API to detect AI-generated text in postediting is a good deterrent, but it isn't failsafe as there are workarounds.

The paper is clearly written and well-organized, but lacks empirical evidence. There's no user study or prototype implementation to demonstrate the feasibility and effectiveness of the proposed platform. The success of the platform hinges on many variables such as user adoption, data quality, which are not addressed. Therefore, the paper remains primarily a design proposal.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**. The paper identifies a relevant problem and proposes a solution with some interesting features, particularly the gamified evaluation module. However, the novelty is limited by the existence of other crowdsourcing platforms. The lack of empirical evidence (user study, prototype implementation) and the simplistic approach to expertise verification are weaknesses. The description of the platform and its components is detailed, but the actual implementation of features that guarantee developer/evaluator collaboration and long-term data quality are missing.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multi-Loco: Unifying Multi-Embodiment Legged Locomotion via Reinforcement Learning Augmented Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11470v1)
- **Authors**: Shunpeng Yang, Zhen Fu, Zhefeng Cao, Guo Junde, Patrick Wensing, Wei Zhang, Hua Chen
- **Abstract**: Generalizing locomotion policies across diverse legged robots with varying morphologies is a key challenge due to differences in observation/action dimensions and system dynamics. In this work, we propose Multi-Loco, a novel unified framework combining a morphology-agnostic generative diffusion model with a lightweight residual policy optimized via reinforcement learning (RL). The diffusion model captures morphology-invariant locomotion patterns from diverse cross-embodiment datasets, improving generalization and robustness. The residual policy is shared across all embodiments and refines the actions generated by the diffusion model, enhancing task-aware performance and robustness for real-world deployment. We evaluated our method with a rich library of four legged robots in both simulation and real-world experiments. Compared to a standard RL framework with PPO, our approach -- replacing the Gaussian policy with a diffusion model and residual term -- achieves a 10.35% average return improvement, with gains up to 13.57% in wheeled-biped locomotion tasks. These results highlight the benefits of cross-embodiment data and composite generative architectures in learning robust, generalized locomotion skills.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Multi-Loco: Unifying Multi-Embodiment Legged Locomotion via Reinforcement Learning Augmented Diffusion" introduces a novel framework (Multi-Loco) for training locomotion policies that can be generalized across diverse legged robots with different morphologies. It combines a morphology-agnostic generative diffusion model, which captures common locomotion patterns from a mixed dataset of different robots, with a lightweight residual policy learned via reinforcement learning (RL). The residual policy refines the actions generated by the diffusion model, making the policy more task-aware and robust for real-world deployment. Experiments across four different legged robots in both simulation and real-world scenarios demonstrate that the Multi-Loco framework achieves superior performance compared to a standard RL baseline (PPO), and highlights the effectiveness of cross-embodiment data and composite generative architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and challenging problem in robotics: how to create control policies that can be easily adapted and reused across different robot morphologies. The idea of using a diffusion model to capture general locomotion patterns and then refining these patterns with RL is interesting and potentially effective. The experimental results, showing improvements over a standard PPO baseline, are promising. The zero-shot transfer to a Unitree Go2 robot is particularly compelling and highlights the generalization capabilities of the approach. The ablation studies are also valuable for understanding the contribution of each component in the proposed framework.

**However, the paper also has some weaknesses:**

*   **Novelty is incremental:** While the combination of diffusion models and RL is novel in this specific context of multi-embodiment *locomotion*, both techniques are well-established.  The paper primarily combines existing techniques in a new way, rather than introducing fundamentally new algorithms or theory.  The novelty lies in the application and engineering of the solution, rather than a breakthrough methodological advancement. Other works also use diffusion models for legged locomotion control, although not in the context of cross-embodiment learning. Also the multi-critic approach to RL is not new either.

*   **Limited real-world evaluation:** While the zero-shot transfer to the Unitree Go2 is good, the overall real-world validation appears limited and could benefit from a more thorough analysis of the challenges encountered.

*   **Complexity and implementation details:** The framework is fairly complex, involving diffusion models, RL, and dimension alignment techniques. The paper provides some details, but more extensive information would aid reproducibility and wider adoption. Crucially, it is unclear how computationally expensive it is to train the model. The real-world performance (50Hz control rate) is relatively slow.

*   **Limited comparison to SOTA baselines in other cross-embodiment settings:** The paper primarily compares to a PPO baseline where separate policies for separate robots are trained. The paper is compared favorably to SR-DP but the advantage is less clear compared to other relevant cross-embodiment algorithms in the literature, which means the approach needs more rigorous benchmarks.

*   **Limited explanation for specific data selection strategies:** Why some knowledge is better from some robots more than others need further elucidation. The justification for improved humanoid-> wheeled Biped performance has not been explained deeply enough.

**Significance:**

Despite the limitations, the paper contributes to the growing body of research on cross-embodiment learning, demonstrating a practical approach for sharing locomotion knowledge across different robot platforms.  The use of diffusion models in this context is potentially beneficial, as they can represent multimodal action distributions, which are likely present in diverse locomotion tasks.  The work could inspire future research on more efficient and scalable methods for robot learning. In particular, the framework could inform the development of more modular and reusable control software for robotics.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, the paper is scored as 6/10. The novelty is good, but incremental as it mainly combines existing techniques. The experimental results are promising, but the real-world validation could be strengthened. However, it provides a clear, implementable framework with potentially significant value in the expanding field of robotics.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Med-PRM: Medical Reasoning Models with Stepwise, Guideline-verified Process Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11474v1)
- **Authors**: Jaehoon Yun, Jiwoong Sohn, Jungwoo Park, Hyunjae Kim, Xiangru Tang, Yanjun Shao, Yonghoe Koo, Minhyeok Ko, Qingyu Chen, Mark Gerstein, Michael Moor, Jaewoo Kang
- **Abstract**: Large language models have shown promise in clinical decision making, but current approaches struggle to localize and correct errors at specific steps of the reasoning process. This limitation is critical in medicine, where identifying and addressing reasoning errors is essential for accurate diagnosis and effective patient care. We introduce Med-PRM, a process reward modeling framework that leverages retrieval-augmented generation to verify each reasoning step against established medical knowledge bases. By verifying intermediate reasoning steps with evidence retrieved from clinical guidelines and literature, our model can precisely assess the reasoning quality in a fine-grained manner. Evaluations on five medical QA benchmarks and two open-ended diagnostic tasks demonstrate that Med-PRM achieves state-of-the-art performance, with improving the performance of base models by up to 13.50% using Med-PRM. Moreover, we demonstrate the generality of Med-PRM by integrating it in a plug-and-play fashion with strong policy models such as Meerkat, achieving over 80\% accuracy on MedQA for the first time using small-scale models of 8 billion parameters. Our code and data are available at: https://med-prm.github.io/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Med-PRM: Medical Reasoning Models with Stepwise, Guideline-verified Process Rewards":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Med-PRM, a novel process reward modeling framework tailored for medical reasoning. It leverages retrieval-augmented generation to verify each reasoning step against established medical knowledge bases (clinical guidelines and literature). Med-PRM acts as a verifier, evaluating the quality of intermediate reasoning steps in a fine-grained manner. The authors demonstrate that Med-PRM achieves state-of-the-art performance on several medical QA benchmarks, improving upon existing language models and process reward models, and showing good plug-and-play generality. A key aspect of the method is its ability to incorporate clinical knowledge into both the training and inference stages, leading to more accurate assessment of intermediate reasoning and cost-effective performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the **RAG-AS-A-JUDGE** approach for step-wise reward modeling.  While RAG and LLM-as-a-Judge frameworks are not entirely novel concepts individually, their synergistic combination for *process reward* in medical reasoning, and subsequent use in training a robust reward model *specifically for intermediate step validation*, seems to be a valuable contribution. This is more nuanced than typical outcome-based reward modeling or using LLMs to evaluate final answers. The specific tailoring to the medical domain with medical guidelines is also an important element of the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The significance is multifaceted:
    *   **Improved Performance:** The paper shows compelling empirical evidence of Med-PRM's effectiveness across multiple medical QA and diagnostic benchmarks.  The consistent outperformance of strong baselines (including MedS3) demonstrates the practical benefit of the approach. Especially noteworthy is the performance increase on the more realistic AgentClinic benchmark, hinting at its potential for real-world application.
    *   **Explainability/Correctability:** By focusing on process rewards, Med-PRM offers the potential for increased transparency and error correction. The ability to pinpoint which steps of a reasoning process are flawed is crucial for building trustworthy medical AI systems.  However, the paper primarily demonstrates performance improvements rather than deep dives into the explainability aspects.
    *   **Cost-Efficiency:** The paper emphasizes the cost-effectiveness of their approach by achieving better results with a smaller reward model trained on less data. This is an important consideration for practical deployment in resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Generalizability:** The plug-and-play nature and improvements over strong underlying models underscores practical applicability of this framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results across diverse benchmarks.
    *   Clear problem formulation and well-motivated approach.
    *   Addresses a critical need for explainability and trustworthiness in medical AI.
    *   Demonstrates cost-efficiency and scalability.
    *   Sound medical domain background and incorporation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited exploration of the *explainability* benefits. While it identifies incorrect steps, the paper does not show how this information might be used to improve the underlying reasoning of the agent.
    *   Limited Scaling: Only tested with small scale models. While the cost-efficient scaling demonstrated is interesting, how this will hold at larger scales is undetermined.
    *   Simplified AgentClinic environment: The authors acknowledge simplifying the AgentClinic benchmark. It's important to consider how the results translate to more realistic multi-turn interactive clinical environments, although initial results are promising.
    *   The human evaluation sample is small. A larger scale human evaluation of the RAG-AS-A-JUDGE is required to ensure a strong link with expert physician opinion.

*   **Potential Influence:** Med-PRM has the potential to influence the direction of research in medical AI by:
    *   Encouraging more focus on process-oriented reward modeling.
    *   Promoting the integration of medical knowledge bases into reasoning frameworks.
    *   Highlighting the importance of transparency and error correction.
    *   Providing a practical approach for building more reliable and trustworthy medical AI systems.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

I assign a score of 8 because the paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to an important problem in medical AI. Med-PRM shows compelling empirical results, demonstrating its ability to improve the accuracy and potentially the trustworthiness of medical reasoning systems. The RAG-AS-A-JUDGE framework for step-wise reward modeling is a valuable contribution that is particularly well-suited for this domain. While the paper has some limitations (particularly regarding a lack of in-depth explainability analysis and small human studies), its strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a significant contribution to the field with potential for future impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LiLAC: A Lightweight Latent ControlNet for Musical Audio Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11476v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Baker, Javier Nistal
- **Abstract**: Text-to-audio diffusion models produce high-quality and diverse music but many, if not most, of the SOTA models lack the fine-grained, time-varying controls essential for music production. ControlNet enables attaching external controls to a pre-trained generative model by cloning and fine-tuning its encoder on new conditionings. However, this approach incurs a large memory footprint and restricts users to a fixed set of controls. We propose a lightweight, modular architecture that considerably reduces parameter count while matching ControlNet in audio quality and condition adherence. Our method offers greater flexibility and significantly lower memory usage, enabling more efficient training and deployment of independent controls. We conduct extensive objective and subjective evaluations and provide numerous audio examples on the accompanying website at https://lightlatentcontrol.github.io
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LILAC, a lightweight and modular architecture based on ControlNet for generating musical audio. It aims to address the limitations of existing ControlNet implementations, specifically their high memory footprint and inflexibility. LILAC replaces the cloned encoder blocks with smaller convolutional layers, significantly reducing the parameter count while maintaining comparable audio quality and adherence to conditioning signals.  The authors evaluate LILAC using objective metrics (FAD, APA, cMSE) and subjective listening tests, demonstrating its effectiveness and efficiency in controlling music generation. The authors explore various aspects of LILAC's architecture, including ablations of its components (head, tail, residual connections) and its robustness in the presence of conflicting conditioning signals (CLAP embeddings and chroma).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel architecture for adapting ControlNet to musical audio generation. The key innovation lies in replacing the resource-intensive cloned encoder blocks with lightweight convolutional layers (head, tail, residual), creating a parameter-efficient yet performant alternative.  While parameter-efficient variants of ControlNet have been explored in computer vision, this paper is the first to demonstrate this for music, and carefully analyze the resulting trade-offs between performance and footprint. The exploration of conflicting conditioning signals and strategies for mitigating their impact is also valuable. However, the underlying concept of using lightweight convolutions as adaptor modules is not entirely new, as similar techniques exist in other domains.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its practical benefits. The reduced memory footprint of LILAC makes it more accessible for researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources. The modular architecture allows for more flexible training and deployment of independent control signals, enabling a wider range of user-defined conditions to guide music generation. The comparative results showing comparable audio quality and condition adherence to ControlNet, while significantly reducing parameters, strengthens the value of this proposed architecture. This work could stimulate further research into parameter-efficient architectures for controllable music generation.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Parameter efficiency:**  A major strength is the significant reduction in parameter count compared to traditional ControlNet, which can drastically reduce computational cost and memory requirements.
    * **Modularity and Flexibility:** The architecture enables independent training and deployment of control signals, offering greater flexibility for users.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a thorough evaluation using both objective and subjective metrics, supporting the claims of comparable audio quality and condition adherence. The analysis of conflicting conditioning signals is a notable strength, addressing a potential limitation of conditional generative models.
    * **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed architecture, experimental setup, and results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Baselines:** While the comparison to the original ControlNet is important, comparing to other recent lightweight control methods specifically adapted for audio could strengthen the results. The use of Diff-a-Riff as the backbone also somewhat limits the generality of the findings. Testing LILAC with a different backbone architecture would further demonstrate its robustness.
    * **Lack of Ablation detail:** The full ablation of the different connection topologies is not entirely thorough. For instance, a deeper ablation investigating the importance of each conv layer in the different head, tail and residual blocks could enhance the architectural contribution.
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the application to music audio and the comprehensive evaluation are valuable, the core architectural innovation (lightweight convolutional adaptors) is incremental.
    * **Reliance on objective Metrics:** It is also important to note that objective metrics only provide an approximation of quality and condition adherence, and might be misleading when used in complex audio tasks such as music generation.

* **Potential Influence:**  This work has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and flexible music generation systems. The LILAC architecture provides a practical solution for incorporating control signals into generative models without incurring a significant memory overhead. The analysis of conflicting conditioning signals could inform the design of more robust and user-friendly interfaces for music creation.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of music generation by proposing a parameter-efficient and flexible architecture for controllable audio generation. The novelty lies in the adaptation of ControlNet to the music domain with a focus on minimizing computational cost, something not fully explored previously in this context. The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation demonstrating the effectiveness of LILAC, which makes it more practical and accessible for real-world applications. However, the core architectural innovation is somewhat incremental, building upon existing ideas in computer vision. Moreover, some design choices (e.g., fixed kernel sizes) aren't sufficiently justified or ablated, diminishing the originality of the proposed architecture. The limitations in baselines and lack of ablation detail detract from the impact of the paper. Overall, LILAC is a solid piece of work that provides a practical and efficient solution for controllable music generation, warranting a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### ImmunoFOMO: Are Language Models missing what oncologists see?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11478v1)
- **Authors**: Aman Sinha, Bogdan-Valentin Popescu, Xavier Coubez, Marianne Clausel, Mathieu Constant
- **Abstract**: Language models (LMs) capabilities have grown with a fast pace over the past decade leading researchers in various disciplines, such as biomedical research, to increasingly explore the utility of LMs in their day-to-day applications. Domain specific language models have already been in use for biomedical natural language processing (NLP) applications. Recently however, the interest has grown towards medical language models and their understanding capabilities. In this paper, we investigate the medical conceptual grounding of various language models against expert clinicians for identification of hallmarks of immunotherapy in breast cancer abstracts. Our results show that pre-trained language models have potential to outperform large language models in identifying very specific (low-level) concepts.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll summarize the paper, critically evaluate its novelty and significance, and provide a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "ImmunoFOMO: Are Language Models missing what oncologists see?" investigates the ability of language models (LMs), both small (SLMs) and large (LLMs), to identify hallmarks of immunotherapy in breast cancer research abstracts. The authors introduce a novel evaluation study where LMs are assessed on their conceptual grounding against expert clinician annotations of multi-level (Tier-I to Tier-III) biomedical concepts related to immunotherapy hallmarks. The study reveals that domain-specific SLMs, such as PubMedBERT and BioBERT, outperform LLMs in identifying specific (low-level) concepts (Tier-III), while LLMs are better at grasping high-level (Tier-I) concepts. The paper also highlights the challenges LLMs face with hallucinations and overall low agreement between LMs and expert annotations. The authors argue that conceptual grounding is crucial for medical language understanding and that current LMs have limitations in capturing the complex biomedical concepts understood by clinicians.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of biomedical natural language processing (NLP), but also has certain limitations:

*   **Novelty:** The study's novelty lies in its specific focus and evaluation methodology. Most previous work in biomedical NLP has concentrated on tasks like question answering, named entity recognition, or relation extraction. This paper takes a different approach by examining the *conceptual* understanding of LMs in a specialized clinical domain (immunotherapy in breast cancer) against expert-level annotations. It also introduces a tiered system to evaluate at different levels of detail. Prior works haven't directly compared SLMs versus LLMs in identifying low-level *vs* high-level concepts from cancer abstracts. Furthermore, the study explicitly investigates model "hallucinations" within this context, which is a crucial aspect of evaluating the reliability of LMs in healthcare.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is in its practical implications for the application of LMs in oncology research. It underscores the importance of *conceptual grounding* for LMs in biomedicine, demonstrating that while LLMs offer certain advantages in broader understanding, they might not be as effective as SLMs in identifying specific, lower-level concepts critical for clinical decision-making. This insight is important for researchers and clinicians who are considering using LMs for tasks like literature review, knowledge discovery, and treatment planning.
*   **Limitations & Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Size and Scope:** The dataset size of 239 abstracts is relatively small. A larger, more diverse dataset might yield more robust and generalizable results. The focus on ESMO abstracts, while providing a specific context, may also limit the generalizability to other clinical or research domains.
    *   **Lack of Tier-II and Tier-III Expert Annotation**: While the annotation from Tier-I level is comprehensive, it would have been more valuable to provide expert annotation for Tier-II and Tier-III. The models' reasoning cannot be appropriately accessed without expert level Tier-II and Tier-III annotations.
    *   **Task Simplicity**: The selected task, hallmark identification, although complex, is still limited in its complexity and might not fully capture the full spectrum of reasoning skills required by oncologists.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The success of LLMs in zero-shot learning relies heavily on prompt engineering. While the prompt is shown (figure 4), the paper does not discuss the extensive prompting engineering it took to arrive on that prompt or alternatives tried.
*   **Potential Influence:** The study's findings could influence the development and application of LMs in biomedical NLP by encouraging researchers to prioritize conceptual grounding, explore the benefits of both SLMs and LLMs, and develop more robust evaluation methodologies that go beyond standard NLP benchmarks. The focus on hallucination detection is particularly important for ensuring the trustworthiness of LMs in healthcare.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above points, the paper represents a **moderate** contribution to the field. It identifies a crucial gap in the conceptual understanding of LMs in a specific clinical context and presents a novel evaluation methodology. While the dataset size and some limitations detract from the overall impact, the findings have valuable practical implications for the use of LMs in oncology research and highlight the need for more robust conceptual grounding and hallucination detection in biomedical NLP. The paper provides critical insights that could inform the development and application of LMs in healthcare in a more responsible and effective manner.
Its contribution is that it points to a gap, the importance of conceptual understanding, and the need to evaluate models in nuanced tasks and the relative strengths of SLMs and LLMs. It sets a direction for future studies.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LearnAlign: Reasoning Data Selection for Reinforcement Learning in Large Language Models Based on Improved Gradient Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11480v1)
- **Authors**: Shikun Li, Shipeng Li, Zhiqin Yang, Xinghua Zhang, Gaode Chen, Xiaobo Xia, Hengyu Liu, Zhe Peng
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a key technique for enhancing LLMs' reasoning abilities, yet its data inefficiency remains a major bottleneck. To address this critical yet challenging issue, we present a novel gradient-alignment-based method, named LearnAlign, which intelligently selects the learnable and representative training reasoning data for RL post-training. To overcome the well-known issue of response-length bias in gradient norms, we introduce the data learnability based on the success rate, which can indicate the learning potential of each data point. Experiments across three mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our method significantly reduces training data requirements while achieving minor performance degradation or even improving performance compared to full-data training. For example, it reduces data requirements by up to 1,000 data points with better performance (77.53%) than that on the full dataset on GSM8K benchmark (77.04%). Furthermore, we show its effectiveness in the staged RL setting. This work provides valuable insights into data-efficient RL post-training and establishes a foundation for future research in optimizing reasoning data selection.To facilitate future work, we will release code.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "LearnAlign," a novel data selection method designed to improve the data efficiency of reinforcement learning (RL) applied to large language models (LLMs) for reasoning tasks. The core idea is to select training data based on improved gradient alignment, considering both the directional alignment of gradients and the "learnability" of each data point.  To overcome response length bias in gradient norms, the paper introduces a data learnability metric based on success rate to represent learnable potential. The method is tested on mathematical reasoning benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH, AMC2023), demonstrating that LearnAlign can achieve comparable or superior performance to full-data training with significantly reduced data requirements. The paper also explores integrating LearnAlign into a staged RL framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its combination of gradient alignment with a data learnability metric specifically tailored to the RL post-training of LLMs for reasoning.  While gradient alignment techniques have been used for data selection in supervised learning, the adaptation to RL and the introduction of learnability as a factor is a significant contribution. The use of the success rate as a measure of learnability, while not entirely new in the broader ML literature, is a clever application to the RL-LLM context. It specifically addresses the shortcomings of cosine similarity in the context of LLM training. The proposed staged RL setting is also a sensible addition.

*   **Significance:** Data efficiency is a crucial concern in LLM training, particularly for computationally expensive RL-based methods. LearnAlign directly addresses this challenge.  The experimental results demonstrate a substantial reduction in data requirements, which translates to lower computational costs. This makes RL post-training more accessible. The observed performance improvements over full-data training in some cases are also significant, suggesting that LearnAlign can help LLMs learn more effectively. The paper is well-written and the experiments are thorough. The inclusion of ablation studies further strengthens the validity of their claims.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of data inefficiency in RL post-training of LLMs.
    *   **Sound Methodology:**  The LearnAlign method is well-motivated and technically sound. The combination of gradient alignment and learnability is a logical and effective approach.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experiments across multiple benchmarks provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of LearnAlign. The comparisons against strong baselines highlight the advantages of the proposed method.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablations demonstrate the importance of each component in LearnAlign (warmup, learnability metric, and gradient similarity).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scale:** The experiments are conducted on relatively small models (1.5B parameters). It is important to see how well LearnAlign scales to significantly larger models (e.g., 100B+ parameters) which are now common. LLM landscape is rapidly evolving. Will it perform similarly well with newer models that adopt newer architectures, training paradigms, and techniques? The computational cost of evaluating this on bigger models is understandable, but it creates a question mark on the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Task Focus:** The paper's focus is primarily on mathematical reasoning datasets. It would be helpful to see experiments on other types of reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical reasoning) or code generation, to demonstrate the broader applicability of LearnAlign.

*   **Impact:** If the method holds for bigger LLMs and a variety of reasoning and code generation scenarios, it can be very impactful.

*   **Clarity of writing:** The writing is relatively good but can be improved further.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5. While the paper presents a novel and significant approach to data selection for RL-based LLM training, the limitations regarding model scale and task diversity prevent a higher score. The novelty of combining gradient alignment with learnability metric in the RL-LLM context and the impressive experimental results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks are strong indicators of potential impact.  However, demonstrating the effectiveness and scalability of LearnAlign on larger models and a broader range of tasks is crucial for establishing its widespread utility and broader acceptance within the research community. Furthermore, the impact of LLM is rapidly shifting, the current models adopted (Qwen2.5-1.5B and Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B) while solid, may not be state-of-the-art for very long.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Relational Schemata in BERT Are Inducible, Not Emergent: A Study of Performance vs. Competence in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11485v1)
- **Authors**: Cole Gawin
- **Abstract**: While large language models like BERT demonstrate strong empirical performance on semantic tasks, whether this reflects true conceptual competence or surface-level statistical association remains unclear. I investigate whether BERT encodes abstract relational schemata by examining internal representations of concept pairs across taxonomic, mereological, and functional relations. I compare BERT's relational classification performance with representational structure in [CLS] token embeddings. Results reveal that pretrained BERT enables high classification accuracy, indicating latent relational signals. However, concept pairs organize by relation type in high-dimensional embedding space only after fine-tuning on supervised relation classification tasks. This indicates relational schemata are not emergent from pretraining alone but can be induced via task scaffolding. These findings demonstrate that behavioral performance does not necessarily imply structured conceptual understanding, though models can acquire inductive biases for grounded relational abstraction through appropriate training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates whether large language models (LLMs) like BERT acquire abstract relational schemata (mental templates for relationship types) during pretraining or if this requires explicit task-specific training (fine-tuning). The author compares BERT's relational classification performance with the structural organization of its internal representations ([CLS] token embeddings) before and after fine-tuning across taxonomic, mereological, and functional relations. The results indicate that pre-trained BERT can achieve high classification accuracy, but its internal representations only exhibit meaningful relational structure (organization by relation type) after fine-tuning. This suggests relational schemata are inducible through task scaffolding, not emergent from pretraining alone, highlighting a dissociation between behavioral performance and structured conceptual understanding. The study employs Representational Similarity Analysis (RSA) and dimensionality reduction techniques (UMAP) to analyze the embedding space and compare it with a ground-truth similarity matrix based on relational labels.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a fundamental question about LLMs: Are they truly understanding the relationships they classify, or merely exploiting statistical correlations?  The study's methodology is sound, using well-established techniques (RSA, UMAP, control conditions) to probe internal representations.  The clear distinction between "performance" (classification accuracy) and "competence" (structured internal representations) is valuable.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Research Question:** The paper directly tackles a significant question concerning the nature of knowledge representation in LLMs.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of RSA and UMAP provides a robust approach for analyzing internal representations. Control experiments using randomized labels add further validity to the findings.
*   **Well-Defined Metrics:** The study clearly defines and measures both performance (classification accuracy) and competence (representational structure), allowing for a nuanced interpretation of the results.
*   **Empirical Evidence:** The experimental results offer compelling evidence that fine-tuning significantly improves the structural organization of relational knowledge within BERT's embeddings.
*   **Connection to Cognitive Science:** The paper effectively draws parallels to concepts from cognitive science, such as the distinction between performance and competence, and the role of scaffolding in learning.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making the complex analysis accessible.
*   **Focus:** The paper provides a good focus on its main task (relation extraction) without going too deeply into many other aspects.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope of Relations:** The study only considers three broad types of relations (taxonomic, mereological, and functional). While cognitively motivated, it may not generalize to all types of relationships found in natural language. Expanding this set may result in different findings.
*   **ConceptNet Dependency:** The dataset is constructed from ConceptNet. The quality and biases inherent in ConceptNet may influence the results. The choice of the relationship types found within ConceptNet limits the study to only a certain type of common-sense knowledge.
*   **BERT as a starting model:** There have been so many iterations of BERT (or derivations of transformers) so the finding may not apply to the more modern models like Llama2 (or derivatives of it) and Gemma, etc. It would be great to run the same types of tasks on a model from the modern era.
*   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** While the paper provides quantitative analysis of the performance and relational structures, the paper lacks the qualitative analysis of what might have triggered BERT to find the relational structures. For example, how does it compare between before and after in the "evolution" of the vectors? It seems like the paper is lacking in what the process looks like.
*   **Limited depth in error analysis:** The paper provides limited error analysis, such as looking into specific instances and seeing if it is an error with the classification or something else.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The finding that relational schemata are inducible but not emergent in BERT has important implications for how we understand the capabilities and limitations of LLMs. It highlights the need for explicit supervision or structural biases when developing models for conceptual reasoning tasks. While probing internal representations of LLMs is a common practice, this paper provides a clear demonstration of the performance-competence gap in the context of relational reasoning. The work contributes to a better understanding of how to bridge this gap through task scaffolding. The results provide a strong argument about the importance of training in generating meaningful relational structures within LLMs.

The paper has a modest impact on the field, in part because of how often there are probing techniques on LLMs. However, the study provides a good step forward as part of the process.

**Score: 6.5**

*Rationale:* The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by rigorously investigating a central question about LLMs and providing clear empirical evidence. The methodology is sound, and the findings are well-supported. However, the limited scope of relations, dataset dependence, and lack of analysis restricts the generalizability and potential impact of the study. There also could be a depth of knowledge on the process as there are a lot of "so what?" moments while reading. A higher score would require a broader scope, more robust datasets, and more generalizable findings. Also including it in more modern era LLMs would have raised the score significantly.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Taming Stable Diffusion for Computed Tomography Blind Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11496v1)
- **Authors**: Chunlei Li, Yilei Shi, Haoxi Hu, Jingliang Hu, Xiao Xiang Zhu, Lichao Mou
- **Abstract**: High-resolution computed tomography (CT) imaging is essential for medical diagnosis but requires increased radiation exposure, creating a critical trade-off between image quality and patient safety. While deep learning methods have shown promise in CT super-resolution, they face challenges with complex degradations and limited medical training data. Meanwhile, large-scale pre-trained diffusion models, particularly Stable Diffusion, have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in synthesizing fine details across various vision tasks. Motivated by this, we propose a novel framework that adapts Stable Diffusion for CT blind super-resolution. We employ a practical degradation model to synthesize realistic low-quality images and leverage a pre-trained vision-language model to generate corresponding descriptions. Subsequently, we perform super-resolution using Stable Diffusion with a specialized controlling strategy, conditioned on both low-resolution inputs and the generated text descriptions. Extensive experiments show that our method outperforms existing approaches, demonstrating its potential for achieving high-quality CT imaging at reduced radiation doses. Our code will be made publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel approach for computed tomography (CT) blind super-resolution by adapting Stable Diffusion, a large-scale pre-trained diffusion model. The method tackles the challenges of complex degradations and limited medical training data typically encountered in CT super-resolution. The proposed framework uses a practical degradation model to synthesize realistic low-quality images and leverages a vision-language model (LLaVA-Med) to generate corresponding text descriptions.  Stable Diffusion is then employed for super-resolution, conditioned on both the low-resolution inputs and the generated text descriptions, using a specialized controlling strategy. The authors demonstrate through experiments that their method outperforms existing approaches in generating high-quality CT images from low-dose acquisitions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its adaptation of Stable Diffusion for CT super-resolution, which is a relatively unexplored area. The use of LLaVA-Med to generate textual descriptions of degraded CT scans to guide the super-resolution process is also a novel contribution. The side-controlling strategy for bridging the domain gap between natural images and medical images is a valuable component of the work. The combination of these elements, particularly the effective fusion of visual and textual information for medical image super-resolution, is where the innovation lies.

* **Significance:** High-resolution CT imaging is essential for medical diagnosis, and reducing radiation exposure remains a critical challenge. By potentially achieving high-quality CT images at reduced radiation doses, this work has potential significance for improving patient safety and diagnostic accuracy.  The demonstrated improvements over existing super-resolution methods suggest practical benefits. Adaption of diffusion models in medical image processing is a promising area and has the potential to yield further improvements. However, the paper’s evaluation primarily focuses on quantitative metrics like PSNR and SSIM.  While these are commonly used, they do not always correlate perfectly with perceived image quality or diagnostic utility by radiologists.  Therefore, the practical significance is partially limited by this. A study involving clinical experts assessing the generated images would have strengthened the paper's claims. The use of pre-trained vision-language model LLaVA-Med is also interesting and can be useful in other medical imaging tasks.

* **Strengths:**
    * Adapts a powerful generative model (Stable Diffusion) to a relevant medical imaging problem.
    * Uses LLaVA-Med to generate text prompts, providing semantic guidance.
    * Side-controlling strategy to address the domain gap is a good idea.
    * Demonstrates quantitative improvements over existing methods.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *  Reliance on PSNR/SSIM as primary evaluation metrics. Lacks a clinical evaluation.
    * The technical details of the side-controlling strategy could be explained with more clarity.
    * Further ablation study is required with different controlling strategies.
    * The real-world application is not demonstrated.

* **Potential Influence:**  The work could influence future research in medical image super-resolution by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion models and vision-language models. It might also encourage the development of more specialized diffusion models tailored to medical imaging data. A follow-up publication that demonstrates clinical utility would be highly influential.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel adaptation of Stable Diffusion for CT super-resolution, addressing a significant problem in medical imaging. The quantitative results and ablation studies provide evidence of the method's effectiveness. However, the lack of clinical evaluation and some limitations in the technical explanation, restrict the true impact. The reliance on PSNR and SSIM, while standard, weakens the argument regarding the clinical applicability of the approach. Given these considerations, a score of 7 is appropriate. It acknowledges the innovation and potential, but also reflects the need for further validation and refinement to demonstrate genuine clinical value.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lag-Relative Sparse Attention In Long Context Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11498v1)
- **Authors**: Manlai Liang, Wanyi Huang, Mandi Liu, Huaijun Li, Jinlong Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have made significant strides in natural language processing and generation, yet their ability to handle long-context input remains constrained by the quadratic complexity of attention computation and linear-increasing key-value memory footprint. To reduce computational costs and memory, key-value cache compression techniques are commonly applied at inference time, but this often leads to severe performance degradation, as models are not trained to handle compressed context. Although there are more sophisticated compression methods, they are typically unsuitable for post-training because of their incompatibility with gradient-based optimization or high computation overhead. To fill this gap with no additional parameter and little computation overhead, we propose Lag-Relative Sparse Attention(LRSA) anchored by the LagKV compression method for long context post-training. Our method performs chunk-by-chunk prefilling, which selects the top K most relevant key-value pairs in a fixed-size lagging window, allowing the model to focus on salient historical context while maintaining efficiency. Experimental results show that our approach significantly enhances the robustness of the LLM with key-value compression and achieves better fine-tuned results in the question-answer tuning task.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty assessment of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Lag-Relative Sparse Attention In Long Context Training" introduces a novel attention mechanism called Lag-Relative Sparse Attention (LRSA) specifically designed for training Large Language Models (LLMs) on long-context inputs under memory constraints. LRSA is anchored by the LagKV compression method and operates by performing chunk-wise prefilling. It selectively attends to the top-K most relevant key-value pairs within a fixed-size lagging window, allowing the model to focus on salient historical context while maintaining computational efficiency.  The authors demonstrate that LRSA enhances the robustness of LLMs with key-value compression and achieves better fine-tuning results in question-answering tasks.  The approach is implemented within the Megatron and MindSpeedLLM frameworks.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper addresses a vital problem in the field of LLMs: the quadratic complexity of attention and the linear increase in memory footprint with long context lengths. The key challenge is to compress the key-value (KV) cache without significantly degrading performance, especially when models are not trained to handle compressed contexts.

*Strengths:*

*   **Addressing the Train-Test Mismatch:** A core strength is the focus on training the model to handle compressed contexts, unlike many existing KV cache compression techniques primarily applied during inference. This addresses the critical train-test mismatch that often leads to performance degradation.
*   **Differentiability and Efficiency:**  The LRSA design emphasizes differentiability and efficiency during training. This makes it suitable for gradient-based optimization and reduces computational overhead, a common limitation of other compression methods.
*   **Practical Implementation:** Implementing LRSA within established frameworks like Megatron and MindSpeedLLM demonstrates its practical applicability and potential for integration into existing LLM training pipelines.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results showcase improved robustness with key-value compression and better fine-tuning results on question-answering tasks. The use of RULER benchmark provides a standardized evaluation for long-context modeling.
*   **Query-Independent Scoring:**  The masking strategy being query-independent means that LRSA can condense the KV cache even shorter as a static mask is employed. This is a notable design choice for efficient memory management.

*Weaknesses and Limitations:*

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The method is *anchored* by the LagKV compression method. Therefore, the level of pure novelty is somewhat bounded by the parent method. The primary contribution is adapting this method to be viable during the training process, not only during the inference stage.
*   **Performance Gain Magnitude:** The improvement in performance (especially as reflected in the training loss graph), while positive, might not be dramatically significant. A more extensive set of experiments and a deeper analysis of the performance trade-offs would strengthen the paper.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper could benefit from more ablation studies.  For example, analyzing the impact of different lag sizes (L) and retention ratios (r) would provide valuable insights into the sensitivity of LRSA to these parameters. Also, it would be beneficial to compare with other sparse attention methods.
*   **Dataset Specificity:**  The reliance on datasets curated from LongAlign and LongLora may limit the generalizability of the findings. It would be beneficial to test LRSA on a wider range of long-context datasets.
*   **Reliance on Existing Methods:** The paper relies on Rotatory Positional Embedding (RoPE). There are multiple improvements on RoPE, for instance YaRN [23] and LongRoPE [5], which could be tested.

*Significance:*

The paper makes a valuable contribution by proposing a practical and trainable sparse attention mechanism for long-context LLMs. The focus on addressing the train-test mismatch in KV cache compression is particularly relevant.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses discussed, I would rate this paper a **Score: 7**.

*Rationale:*

The paper addresses a relevant problem with a practical solution. The focus on trainability and integration into established frameworks is a notable strength. However, the incremental nature of the novelty (building upon LagKV) and the need for more extensive ablation studies and performance analysis prevent it from receiving a higher score. While the experimental results are promising, a more substantial demonstration of performance gains and broader dataset evaluation are needed to establish its significance more firmly. The limitations in the dataset specificity and reliance on RoPE are also factors that contribute to the score.

I hope this is thorough enough. Let me know if you have any other questions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prioritizing Alignment Paradigms over Task-Specific Model Customization in Time-Series LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11512v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Li, Yunyao Cheng, Xinli Hao, Chaohong Ma, Yuxuan Liang, Bin Yang, Christian S. Jensen, Xiaofeng Meng
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have enabled unprecedented capabilities for time-series reasoning in diverse real-world applications, including medical, financial, and spatio-temporal domains. However, existing approaches typically focus on task-specific model customization, such as forecasting and anomaly detection, while overlooking the data itself, referred to as time-series primitives, which are essential for in-depth reasoning. This position paper advocates a fundamental shift in approaching time-series reasoning with LLMs: prioritizing alignment paradigms grounded in the intrinsic primitives of time series data over task-specific model customization. This realignment addresses the core limitations of current time-series reasoning approaches, which are often costly, inflexible, and inefficient, by systematically accounting for intrinsic structure of data before task engineering. To this end, we propose three alignment paradigms: Injective Alignment, Bridging Alignment, and Internal Alignment, which are emphasized by prioritizing different aspects of time-series primitives: domain, characteristic, and representation, respectively, to activate time-series reasoning capabilities of LLMs to enable economical, flexible, and efficient reasoning. We further recommend that practitioners adopt an alignment-oriented method to avail this instruction to select an appropriate alignment paradigm. Additionally, we categorize relevant literature into these alignment paradigms and outline promising research directions.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Prioritizing Alignment Paradigms over Task-Specific Model Customization in Time-Series LLMs."

**Summary:**

This position paper argues for a shift in how Large Language Models (LLMs) are applied to time-series data analysis. Instead of focusing on task-specific model customization (e.g., building separate models for forecasting and anomaly detection), the authors advocate for prioritizing *alignment paradigms* that focus on the intrinsic primitives of time-series data. They identify three key primitives: Domain, Characteristic, and Representation.  Based on these primitives, they propose a taxonomy of three alignment paradigms: 1) Injective Alignment, which injects numerical data into textual representations; 2) Bridging Alignment, which maps numerical data into semantically similar textual forms; and 3) Internal Alignment, which enhances both numerical and textual representations internally within the LLM.  The paper categorizes existing literature within these paradigms and outlines potential research directions. The overall argument is that focusing on these alignment paradigms will lead to more economical, flexible, and efficient time-series reasoning with LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its *conceptual framework* for approaching time-series LLMs. While the individual techniques used within the proposed alignment paradigms (e.g., prompt engineering, adapters, fine-tuning) are not necessarily new, the *organization* and *justification* for prioritizing alignment with fundamental time-series primitives are valuable contributions. It provides a higher-level structure for reasoning about how to best utilize LLMs for time-series analysis, which is a rapidly developing area. The specific taxonomy of alignment paradigms (Injective, Bridging, Internal) based on the identified primitives (Domain, Characteristic, Representation) is a unique and potentially useful contribution.

*Significance:*

The paper's significance stems from its potential to guide future research and development in this area. By shifting the focus from task-specific model building to a more data-centric alignment strategy, the authors argue that LLMs can be more effectively leveraged for a wider range of time-series tasks.  The proposed framework promotes a more systematic and principled approach, which could lead to more robust, generalizable, and interpretable solutions.  The categorization of existing work and the identification of research directions provide a concrete starting point for researchers and practitioners. The examples, especially with clear differentiation among different models in both medical and financial fields, were very helpful to illustrate the idea.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Conceptual Framework:** The paper offers a well-defined framework for understanding and organizing different approaches to time-series LLMs.
*   **Focus on Data Primitives:**  The emphasis on the intrinsic properties of time-series data is a valuable contribution, as it moves beyond simply applying LLMs to existing tasks.
*   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The taxonomy of alignment paradigms is well-structured and provides a useful way to categorize existing literature.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers practical guidance for practitioners in selecting appropriate alignment strategies.
*   **Identified Research Directions:** The authors outline clear directions for future research.
* **Good use of illustrative examples to explain different approaches**
* **Open Github repository**

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** As a position paper, it lacks extensive empirical validation of the proposed framework. While the authors categorize existing work, they do not provide quantitative comparisons to demonstrate the superiority of alignment-focused approaches over task-specific ones.
*   **Somewhat High-Level:** The paper remains at a relatively high conceptual level. More detailed examples and concrete implementation guidance within each alignment paradigm would be beneficial.
*   **Potential Overlap:** The boundaries between the alignment paradigms may be somewhat blurry in practice, and some approaches may fall into multiple categories. There is likely not a crisp, distinct classification in every case.

*Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Encouraging researchers to focus on data alignment strategies rather than solely on task-specific model customization.
*   Providing a common vocabulary and framework for discussing time-series LLMs.
*   Guiding the development of new and more effective alignment techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**. This reflects the paper's valuable conceptual contribution and potential to influence the field, balanced against the lack of empirical validation and the somewhat high-level nature of the discussion. The paper is well-written and provides a clear and compelling argument, but the lack of experimental results demonstrating the benefits of the proposed framework limits its immediate impact. It lays a strong foundation for future work, but more concrete examples and validation are needed to fully realize its potential. The novelty is significant within the specific context of time-series LLMs, even if the underlying techniques are not entirely new.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Brewing Knowledge in Context: Distillation Perspectives on In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11516v1)
- **Authors**: Chengye Li, Haiyun Liu, Yuanxi Li
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) allows large language models (LLMs) to solve novel tasks without weight updates. Despite its empirical success, the mechanism behind ICL remains poorly understood, limiting our ability to interpret, improve, and reliably apply it. In this paper, we propose a new theoretical perspective that interprets ICL as an implicit form of knowledge distillation (KD), where prompt demonstrations guide the model to form a task-specific reference model during inference. Under this view, we derive a Rademacher complexity-based generalization bound and prove that the bias of the distilled weights grows linearly with the Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) between the prompt and target distributions. This theoretical framework explains several empirical phenomena and unifies prior gradient-based and distributional analyses. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first to formalize inference-time attention as a distillation process, which provides theoretical insights for future prompt engineering and automated demonstration selection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Brewing Knowledge in Context: Distillation Perspectives on In-Context Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new theoretical framework for understanding In-Context Learning (ICL) in Large Language Models (LLMs) through the lens of Knowledge Distillation (KD). It argues that ICL can be viewed as an implicit KD process where prompt demonstrations guide the LLM to form a task-specific reference model during inference. The authors derive a Rademacher complexity-based generalization bound and prove that the bias of the distilled weights grows linearly with the Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) between the prompt and target distributions. This framework explains several empirical phenomena and connects previous gradient-based and distributional analyses of ICL. The work formalizes inference-time attention as a distillation process and offers theoretical insights for prompt engineering and demonstration selection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of interpreting ICL as implicit KD is novel and potentially insightful. While prior works have explored connections between ICL and gradient descent or Bayesian inference, framing it directly as a distillation process offers a fresh perspective. The derivation of a generalization bound based on this framework and the connection to MMD further strengthens the novelty. The formalization of inference-time attention as a distillation process is likely the most novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in providing a more unified theoretical framework for understanding ICL. The KD perspective allows for a deeper understanding of how prompts influence ICL performance and offers guidance for prompt design and automated demonstration selection. The generalization bound provides insights into factors that affect ICL performance and could inform strategies for improving it.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Rigor:** The paper presents mathematically sound arguments and rigorous derivations, including the generalization bound and the MMD-based bias analysis.
    *   **Explanatory Power:** The KD framework provides a plausible explanation for several observed empirical phenomena in ICL, such as the importance of prompt quality and demonstration selection.
    *   **Unification:** The paper connects and unifies previously disjointed analyses of ICL (gradient-based and distributional) within a single framework.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The insights derived from the theoretical analysis offer practical guidance for prompt engineering and demonstration selection, potentially leading to improved ICL performance.
    *   **Clear Problem Framing:** The paper addresses a fundamental gap in understanding the mechanism behind ICL which has been a major area of research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplifying Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on certain simplifying assumptions (e.g., linearity, softmax attention, specific loss functions) that might not fully capture the complexity of real-world LLMs and tasks. The authors address some of these assumptions through corollaries and discussions, but the impact of these assumptions on the broader applicability of the results should be discussed further.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** While the theoretical framework explains some existing empirical phenomena, the paper lacks novel empirical validation of the theoretical predictions. It would be more convincing to showcase experiments illustrating the effect of changing MMD and show results in-line with the theory.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The framework primarily focuses on the attention mechanism within the Transformer. While attention is crucial for ICL, the role of other components (e.g., feed-forward networks) could be explored further within the KD perspective.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research on ICL by providing a novel theoretical lens and practical insights for prompt engineering. It could also stimulate further research on the connections between ICL and KD and the development of more sophisticated theoretical models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a novel and potentially significant contribution by interpreting ICL as implicit KD. The theoretical framework is well-developed and provides valuable insights. However, the simplifying assumptions and lack of novel empirical validation limit the immediate impact of the work. Considering these factors, a score of 7 is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Investigating Vulnerabilities and Defenses Against Audio-Visual Attacks: A Comprehensive Survey Emphasizing Multimodal Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11521v1)
- **Authors**: Jinming Wen, Xinyi Wu, Shuai Zhao, Yanhao Jia, Yuwen Li
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs), which bridge the gap between audio-visual and natural language processing, achieve state-of-the-art performance on several audio-visual tasks. Despite the superior performance of MLLMs, the scarcity of high-quality audio-visual training data and computational resources necessitates the utilization of third-party data and open-source MLLMs, a trend that is increasingly observed in contemporary research. This prosperity masks significant security risks. Empirical studies demonstrate that the latest MLLMs can be manipulated to produce malicious or harmful content.
