### GPLQ: A General, Practical, and Lightning QAT Method for Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11784v1)
- **Authors**: Guang Liang, Xinyao Liu, Jianxin Wu
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) are essential in computer vision but are computationally intensive, too. Model quantization, particularly to low bit-widths like 4-bit, aims to alleviate this difficulty, yet existing Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) and Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) methods exhibit significant limitations. PTQ often incurs substantial accuracy drop, while QAT achieves high accuracy but suffers from prohibitive computational costs, limited generalization to downstream tasks, training instability, and lacking of open-source codebase. To address these challenges, this paper introduces General, Practical, and Lightning Quantization (GPLQ), a novel framework designed for efficient and effective ViT quantization. GPLQ is founded on two key empirical insights: the paramount importance of activation quantization and the necessity of preserving the model's original optimization ``basin'' to maintain generalization. Consequently, GPLQ employs a sequential ``activation-first, weights-later'' strategy. Stage 1 keeps weights in FP32 while quantizing activations with a feature mimicking loss in only 1 epoch to keep it stay in the same ``basin'', thereby preserving generalization. Stage 2 quantizes weights using a PTQ method. As a result, GPLQ is 100x faster than existing QAT methods, lowers memory footprint to levels even below FP32 training, and achieves 4-bit model performance that is highly competitive with FP32 models in terms of both accuracy on ImageNet and generalization to diverse downstream tasks, including fine-grained visual classification and object detection. We will release an easy-to-use open-source toolkit supporting multiple vision tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GPLQ (General, Practical, and Lightning Quantization), a novel framework for quantizing Vision Transformers (ViTs) to low bit-widths (e.g., 4-bit).  GPLQ addresses the limitations of existing Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) and Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) methods. PTQ often leads to accuracy drops, while QAT is computationally expensive and can suffer from limited generalization and training instability. GPLQ's core idea is a sequential "activation-first, weights-later" approach.  First, it quantizes activations with weights kept at FP32, using a feature mimicking loss to maintain generalization ability within one epoch.  Second, after the activations are quantized, it applies PTQ to the weights.  This reduces training time significantly, lowers memory footprint, and achieves performance competitive with FP32 models on ImageNet and downstream tasks. The authors also provide an open-source toolkit. The two key insights are (1) activation quantization is the main bottleneck and (2) it's important to stay in the original optimization basin of the FP32 model to maintain generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a Real Problem:** The paper directly addresses the practical challenges of quantizing large ViT models. The trade-off between accuracy, computational cost, and generalization is a critical bottleneck in deploying these models.
    * **Novel Approach:** The "activation-first, weights-later" strategy is a clear departure from standard QAT.  The use of PCA-based feature mimicking to preserve generalization during activation quantization is also a novel and insightful component.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims. The experiments on ImageNet and downstream tasks clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of GPLQ in terms of accuracy, generalization, and training efficiency.  The ablation studies further illuminate the contributions of each component.
    * **Practicality:** The emphasis on practicality is a major strength. The method's speed (100x faster than existing QAT), lower memory footprint (even lower than FP32) and open source toolkit contribute to the significance of the work.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the motivation, methodology, and results. The diagrams are helpful in understanding the overall framework.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependence on PTQ:**  The second stage relies on existing PTQ techniques (RepQ-ViT and QwT). The performance of GPLQ is thus somewhat tied to the advancement of these PTQ methods. While the authors clearly state this as a future research direction, it's a potential limitation.
    * **Limited Scope of QAT Comparison:** The comparison with other QAT methods is limited by the availability of open-source code and the scalability of existing QAT approaches to large models. It would have been useful to see comparisons against more advanced QAT techniques, even if only on smaller models or a subset of tasks.
    * **Justification for choice of PCA dimension:** While the authors conduct a study on the effect of PCA dimension, the guidelines on the specific choice of cumulative explained variance is not robustly justified.

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The idea of sequential quantization, focusing on activations first, is novel and directly addresses the computational bottleneck of QAT.
    * The insight about preserving the optimization basin for better generalization is important and empirically validated.
    * GPLQ provides a practical and efficient way to quantize ViTs, making it a valuable contribution to the field.  The open-source toolkit will likely facilitate further research and adoption.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * GPLQ is likely to become a popular method for quantizing ViTs due to its efficiency and effectiveness.
    * The "activation-first" strategy may inspire new directions in quantization research.
    * The insights about generalization and optimization basins could have broader implications for other areas of deep learning.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper's reliance on existing PTQ techniques is a minor limitation, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses.  GPLQ offers a significant advancement in the practical quantization of ViTs, providing a faster, more efficient, and more generalizable solution than existing methods. The novel insights, clear methodology, strong empirical results, and practical toolkit make it a valuable contribution. The "activation-first" strategy presents an alternative to vanilla QAT that can be more computationally tractable with comparable or better results.

### Conversational AI as a Catalyst for Informal Learning: An Empirical Large-Scale Study on LLM Use in Everyday Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11789v1)
- **Authors**: Nađa Terzimehić, Babette Bühler, Enkelejda Kasneci
- **Abstract**: Large language models have not only captivated the public imagination but have also sparked a profound rethinking of how we learn. In the third year following the breakthrough launch of ChatGPT, everyday informal learning has been transformed as diverse user groups explore these novel tools. Who is embracing LLMs for self-directed learning, and who remains hesitant? What are their reasons for adoption or avoidance? What learning patterns emerge with this novel technological landscape? We present an in-depth analysis from a large-scale survey of 776 participants, showcasing that 88% of our respondents already incorporate LLMs into their everyday learning routines for a wide variety of (learning) tasks. Young adults are at the forefront of adopting LLMs, primarily to enhance their learning experiences independently of time and space. Four types of learners emerge across learning contexts, depending on the tasks they perform with LLMs and the devices they use to access them. Interestingly, our respondents exhibit paradoxical behaviours regarding their trust in LLMs' accuracy and privacy protection measures. Our implications emphasize the importance of including different media types for learning, enabling collaborative learning, providing sources and meeting the needs of different types of learners and learning by design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a large-scale (N=776) survey investigating the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in everyday informal learning. It identifies demographic trends of LLM learners, the motivations and challenges they face, and categorizes users into four distinct learning profiles based on their usage patterns: Structured Knowledge Builders, Self-Guided Explorers, Analytical Problem Solvers, and Adaptive Power Users. The study also explores learner perceptions of LLM effectiveness, over-reliance, and privacy concerns. The results highlight a widespread adoption of LLMs for learning, driven by curiosity and the promise of personalized, on-demand support, but tempered by concerns about accuracy and potential over-reliance. The paper concludes with design implications for LLM-based learning tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Large-Scale Empirical Data:** The study leverages a substantial sample size, providing a broad and potentially representative view of LLM usage in everyday learning, at the moment of study.
    *   **Mixed-Methods Approach:**  Combining quantitative survey data with qualitative insights from open-ended responses offers a richer understanding of the complex factors influencing LLM adoption and usage.
    *   **Identification of Learner Profiles:**  The use of Latent Class Analysis (LCA) to identify distinct learner profiles is a valuable contribution. It helps to move beyond generic descriptions of LLM users and provides a more nuanced understanding of how different individuals integrate these tools into their learning routines.
    *   **Practical Design Implications:** The paper concludes with actionable design implications, making it potentially useful for developers of LLM-based learning tools.
    *   **Addresses a timely and important topic:**  The study addresses the rapidly evolving landscape of AI and education, providing crucial empirical insights into how LLMs are being used in real-world learning contexts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:**  The sample is drawn exclusively from Germany via Prolific, which limits generalizability to other cultural contexts or populations with different levels of access to technology or familiarity with research studies.
    *   **Self-Reported Data:** The reliance on self-reported data may be subject to biases, such as social desirability bias or inaccurate recall. Participants may overstate their usage or underestimate their privacy concerns.
    *   **Modest Explanatory Power of Regression Model:** The regression model examining predictors of class membership only explains a modest amount of variance, suggesting that other factors not included in the survey may be at play.
    *   **Focus on Perceptions rather than Outcomes:** While the study provides valuable insights into user perceptions, it does not directly assess the impact of LLM usage on actual learning outcomes (e.g., knowledge gain, skill development).
    *   **Lack of a Strong Theoretical Framework:** While the study acknowledges the importance of self-directed learning and other relevant concepts, it lacks a strong theoretical framework to guide the analysis and interpretation of the results. This makes it difficult to understand the underlying mechanisms driving LLM adoption and usage.
    *   **The world is already "past" the moment of study:** The pace of development in the field of LLMs is so rapid, that what was current in February of 2025 may have shifted by the time of peer-review.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper is a significant contribution, because the field is still developing.
    *   The large-scale analysis of informal learning with LLMs and the discovery of distinct learner profiles are contributions to knowledge.
    *   This provides empirical grounding for future research, potentially leading to more targeted interventions and personalized learning experiences.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7/10**.

*   The study is well-executed and provides valuable empirical insights into a timely and important topic. The strengths of the paper lie in its large-scale data collection, mixed-methods approach, identification of learner profiles, and practical design implications.

*   However, the limitations regarding generalizability, reliance on self-reported data, modest explanatory power of the regression model, and lack of a strong theoretical framework temper the overall impact. While the study is descriptive and provides valuable insights, it does not offer a deep theoretical understanding of the phenomena under investigation.

*   Although the field is rapidly evolving, these kinds of studies can capture a snapshot of user behavior.

The study is a solid contribution, but it could be strengthened by addressing the identified limitations and developing a more robust theoretical framework.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 0/10

### Persona-driven Simulation of Voting Behavior in the European Parliament with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11798v1)
- **Authors**: Maximilian Kreutner, Marlene Lutz, Markus Strohmaier
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) display remarkable capabilities to understand or even produce political discourse, but have been found to consistently display a progressive left-leaning bias. At the same time, so-called persona or identity prompts have been shown to produce LLM behavior that aligns with socioeconomic groups that the base model is not aligned with. In this work, we analyze whether zero-shot persona prompting with limited information can accurately predict individual voting decisions and, by aggregation, accurately predict positions of European groups on a diverse set of policies. We evaluate if predictions are stable towards counterfactual arguments, different persona prompts and generation methods. Finally, we find that we can simulate voting behavior of Members of the European Parliament reasonably well with a weighted F1 score of approximately 0.793. Our persona dataset of politicians in the 2024 European Parliament and our code are available at https://github.com/dess-mannheim/european_parliament_simulation.
- **Summary**: The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically Llama3 and Qwen2.5, to simulate the voting behavior of Members of the European Parliament (MEPs). The approach employs persona prompting, using MEPs' demographics and political affiliations or Wikipedia summaries, combined with information about policy proposals extracted from group representatives' speeches. The study compares different models, persona prompts (attribute-based vs. Wikipedia summaries), and generation methods (with vs. without reasoning), assessing their accuracy in predicting individual voting decisions and group positions. The research finds that persona-driven LLMs can reasonably predict individual votes, with Llama3-70B achieving the best performance with a weighted F1 score of 0.793. The paper also explores the impact of counterfactual debates and ablates attributes to understand their influence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by extending LLM-based political simulation to the more complex, multi-party European Parliament, contrasting it with prior work focused primarily on the U.S. two-party system. The application of persona prompting to this specific context is innovative. However, using LLMs to model political stances is not entirely new (already proposed in 2023 and 2024, the authors mention it in the related work section). While prior work already focused on bias investigation of LLMs, this paper systematically analyzes this aspect by simulating counterfactual speeches which is a valuable addition. The most relevant novelty comes from the analysis of persona prompting in the context of the EU parliament and showing that this technique mitigates the "inherent biases" of the model.

**Significance:** The paper provides a valuable baseline for simulating voting behavior in a complex political setting. The findings regarding the effectiveness of different models, prompting strategies, and attribute information offer insights into LLM behavior and bias in the context of political simulation. This has relevance for understanding and potentially mitigating biases in LLM applications related to political discourse and decision-making. The data and code release enhances reproducibility and provides a resource for future research. It also contributes to the broader understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of using LLMs for agent-based modeling.

**Strengths:**

*   Systematic experimentation: The paper thoroughly explores various configurations of models, prompts, and generation methods.
*   Contextual relevance: The study addresses the nuances of the European Parliament, including the influence of both national parties and European groups.
*   Ablation studies: The attribute ablation study and the counterfactual speech analysis provide valuable insights into model behavior.
*   Data release: The dataset and code release promotes reproducibility and future research.
*   Addresses ethical considerations: Discusses the possible misuse cases of the proposed method.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited generalizability: The results might not generalize perfectly to other political systems or contexts due to the specific characteristics of the European Parliament and the limited number of tested votes and model architectures.
*   Prediction of Abstention: The models are not good at predicting abstentions.
*   Simplified Representation of Political Reality: The model might not capture all the complexities of political decision-making, such as strategic considerations, lobbying, and behind-the-scenes negotiations. Also, it is assumed that the positions and speeches are unbiased, which is questionable.
*   Small dataset used in the ablation study.
*   Dependency on external data (Debate speeches), making it harder to compare against other methods.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with some valuable insights and a rigorous experimental approach. While it builds on existing work in LLM-based political simulation and LLM bias analysis, the application to the European Parliament and the systematic evaluation of various prompting techniques and ablations add significant value. The limitations, particularly regarding the prediction of abstentions, generalizability, and simplified representation of political realities, temper the impact. Nevertheless, the thorough experimentation and data release justify a positive assessment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are Multimodal Large Language Models Pragmatically Competent Listeners in Simple Reference Resolution Tasks?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11807v1)
- **Authors**: Simeon Junker, Manar Ali, Larissa Koch, Sina Zarrieß, Hendrik Buschmeier
- **Abstract**: We investigate the linguistic abilities of multimodal large language models in reference resolution tasks featuring simple yet abstract visual stimuli, such as color patches and color grids. Although the task may not seem challenging for today's language models, being straightforward for human dyads, we consider it to be a highly relevant probe of the pragmatic capabilities of MLLMs. Our results and analyses indeed suggest that basic pragmatic capabilities, such as context-dependent interpretation of color descriptions, still constitute major challenges for state-of-the-art MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the ability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to perform reference resolution in tasks involving simple abstract visual stimuli (color patches and grids). The authors use a director-matcher style game, where the model must identify a target referent based on human-generated descriptions. The study explores whether these models can understand and respond to color descriptions and spatial arrangements pragmatically. The results show that while larger models (Qwen) achieve promising results on color patches, even the best models struggle with color grids, indicating limitations in their pragmatic understanding of context-dependent color references and spatial reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant Problem:** Investigating the pragmatic capabilities of MLLMs is highly relevant to understanding their true potential in real-world language use. Reference resolution is a fundamental aspect of communication, and this paper provides a valuable probe of MLLM limitations in this area.
    *   **Methodologically Sound:** The use of existing director-matcher datasets provides a solid foundation for the experiments. The controlled variations in visual complexity and the detailed analysis of model responses offer insights into the specific challenges faced by MLLMs. The paper analyzes results from various models and model sizes, adding robustness to the findings.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The qualitative error analysis (examples and tables) is very useful in highlighting specific semantic and pragmatic difficulties encountered by the models. This makes the paper more than just a benchmark and attempts to pinpoint the cause of the observed difficulties.
    *   **Addressing a Gap:**  The paper contributes to a growing body of research on multimodal language understanding, particularly focusing on low-level language grounding and pragmatics, which are often overlooked in favor of higher-level reasoning abilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Task:** The core task (reference resolution in color stimuli) is well-established in the literature. The novelty lies primarily in applying this paradigm to MLLMs. This isn't necessarily a flaw, as a solid benchmark on a known task is valuable, but it limits the "breakthrough" factor.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Datasets:** While using existing datasets adds validity, it also constrains the experimental design. The study is limited by the available data, which may not fully capture the complexities of human-MLLM interaction. Furthermore, the prompting is not interactive and does not allow models to ask for clarification.
    *   **Limited Negative Results:** While showing which models perform well and which do not is important, there is a lack of suggestions on what further measures to take to improve the performance of the poorer performing models (i.e., better data augmentation).
    *   **Modest improvements to existing methods:** The model improvements are modest and not necessarily groundbreaking. It would be interesting to have better improvements, especially when the authors do not offer measures on how models can be further improved.
    *   **Limited Model Coverage:** The number of open-source models analyzed is limited and does not include proprietary state-of-the-art models, such as GPT-4o, Gemini, etc.
    *   **Task simplification:** The task is simplified to the point that linguistic complexity is reduced by applying restrictions on the data. These restrictions can potentially introduce new biases.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies limitations in the pragmatic capabilities of current MLLMs, despite their impressive overall performance. This is crucial for informing future research directions and development efforts.
    *   The paper highlights the importance of careful architectural and fine-tuning choices in MLLMs for situated language understanding. This suggests that there is still much room for improvement in the underlying mechanisms of these models.
    *   The paper provides a clear, replicable experimental setup for evaluating MLLMs on reference resolution tasks, which can serve as a baseline for future research.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by systematically evaluating the pragmatic abilities of MLLMs in reference resolution tasks. However, its novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on established tasks. The strength of the work lies in its rigorous experimental design, detailed analysis, and the clear identification of challenges faced by MLLMs in situated language understanding. Despite its value, the reliance on pre-existing methodology and constrained scope prevents it from being a truly exceptional or transformative work. As such, it doesn't warrant a score above 7. The lack of model coverage and task simplification also contribute to the rationale for the score assigned.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### On the Performance of LLMs for Real Estate Appraisal
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11812v1)
- **Authors**: Margot Geerts, Manon Reusens, Bart Baesens, Seppe vanden Broucke, Jochen De Weerdt
- **Abstract**: The real estate market is vital to global economies but suffers from significant information asymmetry. This study examines how Large Language Models (LLMs) can democratize access to real estate insights by generating competitive and interpretable house price estimates through optimized In-Context Learning (ICL) strategies. We systematically evaluate leading LLMs on diverse international housing datasets, comparing zero-shot, few-shot, market report-enhanced, and hybrid prompting techniques. Our results show that LLMs effectively leverage hedonic variables, such as property size and amenities, to produce meaningful estimates. While traditional machine learning models remain strong for pure predictive accuracy, LLMs offer a more accessible, interactive and interpretable alternative. Although self-explanations require cautious interpretation, we find that LLMs explain their predictions in agreement with state-of-the-art models, confirming their trustworthiness. Carefully selected in-context examples based on feature similarity and geographic proximity, significantly enhance LLM performance, yet LLMs struggle with overconfidence in price intervals and limited spatial reasoning. We offer practical guidance for structured prediction tasks through prompt optimization. Our findings highlight LLMs' potential to improve transparency in real estate appraisal and provide actionable insights for stakeholders.
- **Summary**: This paper investigates the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) for real estate appraisal, focusing on prompt engineering and comparing LLMs to traditional machine learning (ML) models. The authors systematically evaluate different prompting techniques, including zero-shot, few-shot, market report-enhanced, and hybrid approaches, on diverse international housing datasets. They analyze the accuracy of price predictions, price interval delineation, and explanatory capacity. The results show that LLMs can effectively leverage hedonic variables for meaningful estimates and offer a more accessible and interpretable alternative to traditional ML models, although they struggle with spatial reasoning and overconfidence in price intervals.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

While the application of LLMs to real estate appraisal is interesting, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited. Similar studies have explored using NLP and LLMs for real estate-related tasks, although perhaps not with this exact combination of datasets and prompting techniques. The comparison to traditional ML models is valuable, but the conclusion that LLMs are "competitive" while not surpassing ML in predictive accuracy is not groundbreaking. The investigation into prompt engineering is a key contribution.  The finding that incorporating both geographically similar and hedonic-feature similar examples is beneficial for in-context learning is useful and could potentially be applied to other tabular prediction tasks. The identification of limitations, such as spatial reasoning and interval estimation, is also important for highlighting areas needing further research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a systematic evaluation of different prompting strategies and models across various datasets.
*   **Practical Guidance:** The study offers practical guidance for prompt optimization and highlights the potential of LLMs for improving transparency in real estate appraisal.
*   **Comparison to Baselines:** The comparison with traditional ML models provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating LLM performance.
*   **Identification of Limitations:** The paper clearly identifies limitations of LLMs in this domain, such as spatial reasoning and overconfidence in interval estimation.
*   **Explainability Analysis:** Includes an analysis comparing LLM generated explanations to Shapley values from tree based models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The overall research question and methodology build upon existing work in applying LLMs to structured data. While the specific application to real estate appraisal provides value, the fundamental techniques are not new.
*   **Lack of groundbreaking results:** While providing guidance for the application of LLMs to a specific problem domain, it does not result in a radical advancement in the field.
*   **Data is mostly publicly available:** King County and Barcelona datasets are available from Kaggle and open source, respectively. This limits the novelty of the dataset and reduces the barrier to entry for other researchers.
*   **Incremental Advancements**: The finding that more complex prompting yields better results is not surprising or unexpected, and is an incremental result on already explored avenues of applying LLMs.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on applying LLMs to structured data and domain-specific tasks. It provides practical insights for practitioners interested in using LLMs for real estate appraisal and highlights areas for future research. It also demonstrates the potential of LLMs to democratize access to real estate insights by providing a more accessible and interpretable alternative to traditional ML models. It underscores the crucial aspect of prompting strategy when using LLMs and provides actionable recommendations.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by systematically evaluating the use of LLMs for real estate appraisal and identifying key strengths and limitations. However, the novelty is incremental, and the results are not groundbreaking. It is a valuable contribution for practitioners and researchers interested in this specific application but lacks a significant theoretical breakthrough that would justify a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Revealing Political Bias in LLMs through Structured Multi-Agent Debate
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11825v1)
- **Authors**: Aishwarya Bandaru, Fabian Bindley, Trevor Bluth, Nandini Chavda, Baixu Chen, Ethan Law
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate social behaviour, yet their political biases and interaction dynamics in debates remain underexplored. We investigate how LLM type and agent gender attributes influence political bias using a structured multi-agent debate framework, by engaging Neutral, Republican, and Democrat American LLM agents in debates on politically sensitive topics. We systematically vary the underlying LLMs, agent genders, and debate formats to examine how model provenance and agent personas influence political bias and attitudes throughout debates. We find that Neutral agents consistently align with Democrats, while Republicans shift closer to the Neutral; gender influences agent attitudes, with agents adapting their opinions when aware of other agents' genders; and contrary to prior research, agents with shared political affiliations can form echo chambers, exhibiting the expected intensification of attitudes as debates progress.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates political biases in Large Language Models (LLMs) using a structured multi-agent debate framework. The authors explore how LLM type (Neutral, Republican, Democrat) and agent gender influence political bias and attitude shifts during debates. They systematically vary underlying LLMs, agent genders, and debate formats to analyze the impact of model provenance and agent personas. Key findings include:  Neutral agents aligning with Democrats, Republicans shifting towards neutral, the influence of gender on agent attitudes (especially when aware of others' genders), and the formation of echo chambers, contrary to some prior research, leading to attitude intensification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Structured Methodology:** The paper employs a rigorous, well-defined framework for simulating political debates. The systematic variation of parameters (LLM type, gender, debate format) allows for controlled observation of biases.
*   **Multi-faceted Analysis:** The investigation examines several aspects of bias, including political alignment, gender effects, and echo chamber formation. This provides a more comprehensive understanding of the issue.
*   **Counterintuitive Findings:** The discovery that Republican agents tend to shift towards neutral, and the observation of echo chamber formation (contrary to previous studies), are novel and challenge existing assumptions.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings offer insights into the potential risks of bias in LLM-based simulations, especially in politically sensitive contexts. This is crucial for developing fair and reliable conversational agents.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope of LLMs:** The study uses a relatively small set of LLMs (Llama 3.2, GPT-40-mini, Gemma 7B, DeepSeek R1, Qwen 2.5). While the authors argue for capturing a range of training sources, expanding this set would improve the generalizability of the findings. More specifically, GPT-4 and Llama3 70B aren't assessed.
*   **Simplified Agent Personas:** While the personas are designed to minimize bias, they are still somewhat simplified representations of political identities. More complex personas, incorporating additional demographic and psychographic factors, could yield richer insights.
*   **Evaluation reliance on LLM-as-a-judge:** While LLM-as-a-judge is a methodology used here, the work depends on the quality of this methodology. Any biases here would skew results.
*   **Computational Constraints:** The limitations in the number of agents per debate and the lack of exploration of race and age further restricts the scope of the analysis.
*   **Generalizability to real-world debates:** It is very unclear how the structured debate environment will translate to less structured debate environments.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's novelty lies in its structured approach to revealing biases in LLMs through multi-agent debates and its discovery of some counterintuitive behaviors. The observation of Republican agents shifting to neutral, the impact of gender awareness, and the formation of echo chambers, challenge previous assumptions and add significant nuance to our understanding of how LLMs behave in social simulations. The potential impact of this research is substantial, as it provides actionable insights for developing more robust and unbiased LLM-based systems, with a focus on social simulation and fairness.

*Justification of Score:*

The paper presents a solid, well-executed study with interesting findings and implications. However, the limitations in the scope of LLMs studied and the simplified agent personas slightly diminish the paper's overall impact. The reliance on Mistral-7B as an LLM-as-a-judge potentially skews findings. However, due to challenging existing assumptions and practical insights for mitigation is worthy of praise.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### TrustGLM: Evaluating the Robustness of GraphLLMs Against Prompt, Text, and Structure Attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11844v1)
- **Authors**: Qihai Zhang, Xinyue Sheng, Yuanfu Sun, Qiaoyu Tan
- **Abstract**: Inspired by the success of large language models (LLMs), there is a significant research shift from traditional graph learning methods to LLM-based graph frameworks, formally known as GraphLLMs. GraphLLMs leverage the reasoning power of LLMs by integrating three key components: the textual attributes of input nodes, the structural information of node neighborhoods, and task-specific prompts that guide decision-making. Despite their promise, the robustness of GraphLLMs against adversarial perturbations remains largely unexplored-a critical concern for deploying these models in high-stakes scenarios. To bridge the gap, we introduce TrustGLM, a comprehensive study evaluating the vulnerability of GraphLLMs to adversarial attacks across three dimensions: text, graph structure, and prompt manipulations. We implement state-of-the-art attack algorithms from each perspective to rigorously assess model resilience. Through extensive experiments on six benchmark datasets from diverse domains, our findings reveal that GraphLLMs are highly susceptible to text attacks that merely replace a few semantically similar words in a node's textual attribute. We also find that standard graph structure attack methods can significantly degrade model performance, while random shuffling of the candidate label set in prompt templates leads to substantial performance drops. Beyond characterizing these vulnerabilities, we investigate defense techniques tailored to each attack vector through data-augmented training and adversarial training, which show promising potential to enhance the robustness of GraphLLMs. We hope that our open-sourced library will facilitate rapid, equitable evaluation and inspire further innovative research in this field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TrustGLM: Evaluating the Robustness of GraphLLMs Against Prompt, Text, and Structure Attacks":

**Summary:**

The paper "TrustGLM" presents a benchmark for evaluating the robustness of Graph Large Language Models (GraphLLMs) against adversarial attacks. The benchmark, also called TrustGLM, focuses on three main attack vectors: graph structure attacks (modifying the graph topology), raw text attacks (perturbing node text), and prompt manipulation attacks (modifying instruction prompts and label sets). The authors implement several state-of-the-art attack algorithms for each attack vector, extensively testing GraphLLMs' vulnerabilities on various datasets. Additionally, the paper explores defense techniques such as data augmentation and adversarial training to enhance the robustness of GraphLLMs. The code is promised to be publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive, systematic evaluation of GraphLLM robustness. While individual attacks on graphs or text and LLMs exist, the combined and holistic approach to evaluating GraphLLMs across structure, text, and prompt domains is relatively new. Introducing prompt manipulation attacks is also a novel attack paradigm. The open-source benchmark will enable further research.

**Significance:** This paper is significant because GraphLLMs are becoming increasingly important in various applications where graph data and language understanding are combined. Highlighting vulnerabilities and proposing defenses contributes to building more secure and reliable GraphLLM systems. Understanding these vulnerabilities is crucial for deploying GraphLLMs in high-stakes scenarios where adversarial attacks could have serious consequences. The promise of the public benchmark also amplifies the significance, fostering wider research and potentially standardized methods for evaluating future GraphLLM robustness.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper covers a broad range of attack vectors and leverages multiple datasets, providing a thorough evaluation of GraphLLM robustness.
*   **Practical Focus:** The exploration of defense techniques is highly practical and provides valuable insights into mitigating vulnerabilities.
*   **Open-Source Benchmark:** The promised open-source nature of the TrustGLM benchmark will enable other researchers to reproduce the findings, conduct further investigations, and develop new defenses.
*   **Clearly Defined Attacks:** The description and implementation of different attack algorithms are easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Defenses:** While the paper explores data augmentation and adversarial training, it could benefit from investigating other defense strategies, such as robust optimization or certified defenses, to further strengthen GraphLLMs against attacks.
*   **Limited GraphLLM Models Evaluated:** The evaluation only uses three GraphLLM models. The insights might not be transferable to all GraphLLM architectures.
*   **Computational Cost:** While the gray-box approach to structure attacks helps, the computational burden of attacking these models is still high, thus scaling to more attacks/datasets may be difficult.
*   **Limited Detail on Defense Strategies:**  While the paper mentions adversarial training, the specific implementation details (like the exact configuration of FGSM/PGD and the choice of adversarial loss) could be elaborated upon to ensure reproducibility and deeper understanding.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of GraphLLMs by systematically evaluating their robustness against adversarial attacks and by investigating potential defense techniques. The comprehensive evaluation, practical focus, and open-source benchmark make this paper a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working with GraphLLMs.  While there are some limitations regarding the depth of explored defenses and the scope of graph models evaluated, the contribution is substantial. The insights provided will hopefully lead to more robust and reliable GraphLLMs that are less susceptible to malicious manipulations.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Post Persona Alignment for Multi-Session Dialogue Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11857v1)
- **Authors**: Yi-Pei Chen, Noriki Nishida, Hideki Nakayama, Yuji Matsumoto
- **Abstract**: Multi-session persona-based dialogue generation presents challenges in maintaining long-term consistency and generating diverse, personalized responses. While large language models (LLMs) excel in single-session dialogues, they struggle to preserve persona fidelity and conversational coherence across extended interactions. Existing methods typically retrieve persona information before response generation, which can constrain diversity and result in generic outputs. We propose Post Persona Alignment (PPA), a novel two-stage framework that reverses this process. PPA first generates a general response based solely on dialogue context, then retrieves relevant persona memories using the response as a query, and finally refines the response to align with the speaker's persona. This post-hoc alignment strategy promotes naturalness and diversity while preserving consistency and personalization. Experiments on multi-session LLM-generated dialogue data demonstrate that PPA significantly outperforms prior approaches in consistency, diversity, and persona relevance, offering a more flexible and effective paradigm for long-term personalized dialogue generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Post Persona Alignment (PPA), a novel two-stage framework for multi-session persona-based dialogue generation. Unlike traditional methods that integrate persona information before generating a response, PPA first generates a general response based solely on the dialogue context, then retrieves relevant persona memories using the generated response as a query, and finally refines the response to align with the speaker's persona. This post-hoc alignment strategy aims to improve naturalness, diversity, consistency, and personalization in long-term dialogue generation. The authors evaluate PPA against several baselines on LLM-generated multi-session dialogues, demonstrating significant improvements in consistency, diversity, and persona relevance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in reversing the conventional order of persona integration in dialogue generation. Prior work primarily focuses on *pre*-alignment, conditioning the response generation on persona information from the outset. The *post*-alignment approach, where persona information is incorporated after an initial response is generated based on context, is a fresh take. The idea of using the generated response as a retrieval query is also a useful and potentially generalizable technique. However, it's an incremental innovation building on retrieval augmented dialogue.

**Significance:** The paper tackles key challenges in multi-session persona-based dialogue generation, specifically long-term consistency and response diversity. Addressing these challenges is important for creating more engaging and realistic conversational AI. The results show a clear improvement over existing methods in several metrics (consistency, diversity, persona relevance), which suggests that the PPA framework has potential for practical application.  The paper provides evidence that delaying persona grounding can lead to more natural and diverse responses, a valuable insight for the field. The experimental setup and ablations offer a solid foundation for further research in this direction.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in multi-session dialogue generation.
*   **Novel Approach:** PPA offers a distinctive and intuitive solution by reversing the order of response generation and persona integration.
*   **Solid Experimental Results:** The paper presents compelling empirical evidence that PPA outperforms existing methods across multiple metrics.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The analysis of different query strategies and history representations provides valuable insights into the effectiveness of PPA's components.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **LLM Dependency:** The framework relies heavily on the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) for both response generation and refinement. The performance could be sensitive to the choice of LLM and prompting strategies.
*   **Retrieval Quality:** The effectiveness of PPA depends on the quality of the retrieval mechanism.  If the initial response is poor or the embedding space is not well-aligned, the retrieval of relevant persona memories may be suboptimal, and the refinement stage might not be able to recover. The sentence-BERT selection might be limiting and could be improved by fine tuning the retriever on dialogue task.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is conducted on a specific dataset of LLM-generated multi-session dialogues.  It would be beneficial to evaluate PPA on real-world conversational data or with human evaluators to assess its generalizability and user experience.
* **Incremental nature:** Although post-persona alignment is a novel framing, it's still building on retrieval augmented dialogue. The improvement could be attributed to a specific fine tuning of the retrieval process instead of an overarching improvement from the framework.

**Potential Influence:**

PPA has the potential to influence future research in persona-based dialogue generation by shifting the focus from pre-alignment to post-alignment strategies. The idea of using the generated response as a retrieval query could be adopted in other knowledge-grounded tasks. The findings on the importance of structured personal knowledge could also inform the design of memory modules in conversational AI systems.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The PPA framework presents a genuinely novel approach to the problem of persona-based dialogue generation. The experimental results provide strong evidence of its effectiveness. However, the dependency on LLMs, retrieval quality, and the dataset used, the limited evaluation scope and incremental nature of the improvement, slightly limits its overall significance and potential impact on the field. Thus, I assign a score of 6, reflecting a solid contribution with promising results and future directions, but with some caveats regarding its practical applicability and generalizability.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### A Short Survey on Formalising Software Requirements using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11874v1)
- **Authors**: Arshad Beg, Diarmuid O'Donoghue, Rosemary Monahan
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a focused literature survey on the use of large language models (LLM) to assist in writing formal specifications for software. A summary of thirty-five key papers is presented, including examples for specifying programs written in Dafny, C and Java. This paper arose from the project VERIFAI - Traceability and verification of natural language requirements that addresses the challenges in writing formal specifications from requirements that are expressed in natural language. Our methodology employed multiple academic databases to identify relevant research. The AI-assisted tool Elicit facilitated the initial paper selection, which were manually screened for final selection. The survey provides valuable insights and future directions for utilising LLMs while formalising software requirements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of research on using Large Language Models (LLMs) to formalize software requirements expressed in natural language. It categorizes and summarizes 35 key papers, focusing on methodologies used to transform natural language requirements into formal notations like Dafny assertions, LTL formulas, and more general specifications. The survey identifies trends, research questions, and future directions, including the use of prompt engineering, chain-of-thought reasoning, hybrid neuro-symbolic approaches, and the integration of LLMs with formal verification tools (SMT solvers, theorem provers). The work arose from the VERIFAI project which aims to automate the formalization of software requirements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Scope:** The paper focuses on a specific and timely area – the application of LLMs to software requirements formalization. This targeted approach makes the survey more manageable and insightful.
    *   **Structured Methodology:** The literature review methodology is clearly outlined, including search terms, databases used, and inclusion/exclusion criteria. The use of Elicit for initial paper selection enhances efficiency.
    *   **Comprehensive Categorization:** The survey provides a useful categorization of the surveyed literature based on different methodologies: prompt engineering, fine-tuning, verifier-in-the-loop, neuro-symbolic approaches, and baseline methods.  This gives readers a good overview of the landscape.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-organized and provides helpful summaries of each included study in an appendix. The overview figures provides a nice high-level structure. The description of the running example of generating Dafny assertions is helpful for understanding the context.
    *   **Future Directions:** The discussion of future research directions, including advanced prompt engineering techniques and hybrid neuro-symbolic systems, is valuable for researchers in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Critical Depth:** While the paper categorizes the existing literature, the depth of *critical* analysis could be improved. The survey presents a largely descriptive overview, rather than a more in-depth analysis of the limitations and challenges associated with each methodology. It would be beneficial to provide a more nuanced assessment of the strengths and weaknesses of the surveyed approaches.
    *   **Missing comparative analysis:** While the work presents a summarization of existing work, a comparative analysis of existing tools would enhance the paper. This comparison could be used to provide insights and gaps in existing tools.
    *   **Missing empirical validation on running case-study:** In future directions, the paper focuses on future directions, however, this can be enhanced by applying existing survey concepts to the running case-study and analyze the empirical results.
    *   **Superficial discussion of challenges.** Challenges such as bias, robustness, and ethical implication could be improved.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The survey provides a good overview of the current state of research in a fast-evolving field.
    *   The analysis of the trends and future directions contributes to a better understanding of the opportunities and challenges in utilizing LLMs for software requirements formalization.
    *   The survey is valuable for researchers and practitioners who are interested in exploring the potential of LLMs for software engineering tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid survey that provides a valuable service to the community by organizing and summarizing a growing body of literature. However, its lack of a deeper critical analysis and limited exploration of open challenges prevents it from being a truly exceptional contribution. While the categorization is helpful, the paper could have benefited from a more nuanced evaluation of the different approaches and their associated trade-offs. Additionally, empirical validation, further comparative analysis of existing tools, and more discussion of challenges could enhance the paper significantly.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Addressing Bias in LLMs: Strategies and Application to Fair AI-based Recruitment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11880v1)
- **Authors**: Alejandro Peña, Julian Fierrez, Aythami Morales, Gonzalo Mancera, Miguel Lopez, Ruben Tolosana
- **Abstract**: The use of language technologies in high-stake settings is increasing in recent years, mostly motivated by the success of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, despite the great performance of LLMs, they are are susceptible to ethical concerns, such as demographic biases, accountability, or privacy. This work seeks to analyze the capacity of Transformers-based systems to learn demographic biases present in the data, using a case study on AI-based automated recruitment. We propose a privacy-enhancing framework to reduce gender information from the learning pipeline as a way to mitigate biased behaviors in the final tools. Our experiments analyze the influence of data biases on systems built on two different LLMs, and how the proposed framework effectively prevents trained systems from reproducing the bias in the data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of demographic bias in Large Language Models (LLMs) and their application in AI-based recruitment tools.  It investigates how LLMs can learn and reproduce gender biases present in training data, leading to unfair outcomes. The authors propose a privacy-enhancing framework to mitigate these biases by reducing gender information in the learning pipeline. They evaluate two methods: one based on model explainability (identifying and removing biased tokens) and another using adversarial learning (preventing the model from learning gender information).  Experiments are conducted using two LLMs (BERT and RoBERTa) and a synthetic resume dataset (FairCVdb) to analyze the effectiveness of these methods in preventing the reproduction of biases. The results show that both methods successfully reduce gender information and mitigate biased behaviors, ultimately improving fairness in resume scoring.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the application and adaptation of existing bias mitigation techniques to the specific domain of LLM-based recruitment. While neither the explainability-based method (Integrated Gradients and token removal) nor the adversarial learning (LNTL) approach is fundamentally new, their combination and evaluation within the context of automated resume screening represent a worthwhile contribution. The emphasis on privacy-enhancing techniques as a means of fairness is also a solid angle, fitting well with current discussions. The work doesn't introduce groundbreaking new methods for bias mitigation in LLMs but presents a practical application and evaluation of existing ones in a relevant high-stakes domain.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the increasing use of LLMs in high-stakes decision-making settings like recruitment.  The paper highlights the risks associated with deploying biased LLMs and provides practical strategies for mitigating these risks.  The experiments demonstrate that biases present in training data can be learned and amplified by LLMs, underscoring the need for careful design and mitigation strategies. The focus on gender bias is highly relevant, given the historical and ongoing disparities in the labor market. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework in reducing gender bias, which can contribute to building fairer and more equitable recruitment systems.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of bias in LLM-based recruitment and its potential consequences.
    * **Practical Application:** The work focuses on a real-world application with significant societal impact.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a detailed experimental setup and evaluates the effectiveness of the proposed methods using appropriate fairness metrics.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and presents the methods and results clearly.
    * **Reproducibility:** Using FairCVdb helps with reproducibility and allows for future research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** The core techniques (IG and LNTL) are not novel. The main contribution is their adaptation and evaluation in the recruitment setting.
    * **Synthetic Data:** While FairCVdb is useful, results obtained on synthetic data may not fully generalize to real-world resume data. The complexity and nuances of real resumes may not be captured by the synthetic profiles.
    * **Limited Scope:** The study focuses exclusively on gender bias. Other types of demographic biases (e.g., race, ethnicity) are not considered.  The intersectionality of biases is also not addressed.
    * **Token Removal:** The token removal strategy might inadvertently remove legitimate information or useful phrases, decreasing performance.
    * **Performance Loss:** The authors note "mitigating the effect of gender bias" but no analysis is made on what is lost by doing this, ie less-qualified candidates will be recommended

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can contribute to raising awareness about the risks of bias in LLM-based recruitment systems.  The proposed framework provides a practical starting point for practitioners seeking to mitigate bias in their systems. The use of synthetic data facilitates research and comparison across different approaches.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a solid contribution, but its primary weakness is incremental novelty. It effectively applies and evaluates existing bias mitigation techniques to the important domain of LLM-based recruitment.  The paper does not introduce groundbreaking new ideas but provides valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities of building fairer AI systems in this high-stakes setting. The practical nature of the work and the use of a synthetic dataset enhance its accessibility and potential for future research, but synthetic data limits a generalizability of the paper and the results. The limitation to gender biases, makes it difficult to scale to other demographic types. For these reasons the score is in this space.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Beyond Homogeneous Attention: Memory-Efficient LLMs via Fourier-Approximated KV Cache
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11886v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoran Liu, Siyang He, Qiqi Wang, Ruixiao Li, Yuerong Song, Zhigeng Liu, Linlin Li, Qun Liu, Zengfeng Huang, Qipeng Guo, Ziwei He, Xipeng Qiu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models struggle with memory demands from the growing Key-Value (KV) cache as context lengths increase. Existing compression methods homogenize head dimensions or rely on attention-guided token pruning, often sacrificing accuracy or introducing computational overhead. We propose FourierAttention, a training-free framework that exploits the heterogeneous roles of transformer head dimensions: lower dimensions prioritize local context, while upper ones capture long-range dependencies. By projecting the long-context-insensitive dimensions onto orthogonal Fourier bases, FourierAttention approximates their temporal evolution with fixed-length spectral coefficients. Evaluations on LLaMA models show that FourierAttention achieves the best long-context accuracy on LongBench and Needle-In-A-Haystack (NIAH). Besides, a custom Triton kernel, FlashFourierAttention, is designed to optimize memory via streamlined read-write operations, enabling efficient deployment without performance compromise.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "FourierAttention," a training-free method for compressing the Key-Value (KV) cache in Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve memory efficiency. The core idea is to exploit the heterogeneous roles of different head dimensions in the transformer architecture: lower dimensions tend to focus on local context, while higher dimensions capture long-range dependencies. FourierAttention projects the less important (long-context-insensitive) lower dimensions onto a set of orthogonal Fourier bases, effectively representing their temporal evolution with fixed-length spectral coefficients. The paper also proposes "FlashFourierAttention," a custom Triton kernel to optimize memory access patterns during decoding, aiming to minimize overhead. The approach is evaluated on LLaMA models using LongBench and Needle-In-A-Haystack (NIAH) benchmarks, showing competitive long-context accuracy and memory efficiency compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents a relatively novel approach to KV cache compression. While the idea of exploiting the heterogeneity of head dimensions isn't entirely new (some past research has looked at pruning certain heads or applying different quantization strategies), the specific technique of using a Fourier transform to compress dimensions based on their temporal behavior and contextual sensitivity is a fresh contribution.  The use of HiPPO as inspiration is also a good connection to related research. However, the reliance on translated Fourier transforms to compress the cache could be seen as an application of a known technique to a different context, rather than a groundbreaking innovation in signal processing or deep learning theory.

* **Significance:**  Memory efficiency is a crucial bottleneck in LLM deployment.  Therefore, any method that can effectively reduce memory usage while maintaining or improving accuracy has significant practical value. The paper demonstrates promising results on standard benchmarks, suggesting that FourierAttention could be a useful tool for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments.  The design of a custom Triton kernel (FlashFourierAttention) for efficient implementation is an important step towards practical adoption, but the paper mentions it is still under development.  Without performance numbers for the new kernel, the significance is limited to the algorithmic contribution.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Performance:** The empirical results demonstrate a good balance between memory efficiency and accuracy on challenging long-context benchmarks.  In some cases, the performance is superior to existing training-free methods.
    *   **Training-Free:** The method doesn't require fine-tuning or retraining the model, making it easily applicable to existing pre-trained LLMs.
    *   **Dimension Specialization Analysis:** The analysis of head dimension specialization adds valuable insight into the internal workings of transformer models and provides a rationale for the proposed compression strategy.
    *   **Triton Kernel Optimization:** The inclusion of a custom Triton kernel to optimize memory access is crucial for making the method practical.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **FlashFourierAttention performance not demonstrated:** The custom Triton kernel (FlashFourierAttention) is key for making the method efficient, but performance data is not included. This significantly weakens the practical significance claim.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are somewhat limited. While the paper explores different compression schemas, more detailed analysis of the impact of various Fourier transform parameters (e.g., number of spectral coefficients) would be beneficial.
    *   **Comparison to SOTA unclear:** The paper compares to existing training-free compression techniques. However, it does not fully explore whether the improvements are statistically significant over a wide range of datasets, context lengths, and model sizes.

*   **Potential Influence:** If FlashFourierAttention delivers significant speedups over standard attention, this research has the potential to influence future work on memory-efficient LLM deployment. It inspires the exploration of signal processing techniques and head dimension specialization as a means of optimizing transformer architectures. It also emphasizes the importance of custom kernel design for efficient implementation of compression methods.

*   **Overall:** While the paper presents a novel approach with promising empirical results, the significance is somewhat limited by the fact that performance data with the custom Triton kernel is not yet available, and there are some limitations regarding the comprehensiveness of the evaluation and ablation studies.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and practical approach with promising results. However, without the demonstrated performance benefits of the FlashFourierAttention custom kernel, its practical impact is unclear. A score of 6 reflects its positive contributions and insights while acknowledging these limitations. The lack of performance numbers for FlashFourierAttention, the custom Triton kernel, is a crucial weakness, without these, the method's potential is hard to fully ascertain. Further exploration of other methods is also encouraged. The score might be higher with the performance number for FlashFourierAttention.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Understanding Input Selectivity in Mamba: Impact on Approximation Power, Memorization, and Associative Recall Capacity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11891v1)
- **Authors**: Ningyuan Huang, Miguel Sarabia, Abhinav Moudgil, Pau Rodriguez, Luca Zappella, Federico Danieli
- **Abstract**: State-Space Models (SSMs), and particularly Mamba, have recently emerged as a promising alternative to Transformers. Mamba introduces input selectivity to its SSM layer (S6) and incorporates convolution and gating into its block definition. While these modifications do improve Mamba's performance over its SSM predecessors, it remains largely unclear how Mamba leverages the additional functionalities provided by input selectivity, and how these interact with the other operations in the Mamba architecture. In this work, we demystify the role of input selectivity in Mamba, investigating its impact on function approximation power, long-term memorization, and associative recall capabilities. In particular: (i) we prove that the S6 layer of Mamba can represent projections onto Haar wavelets, providing an edge over its Diagonal SSM (S4D) predecessor in approximating discontinuous functions commonly arising in practice; (ii) we show how the S6 layer can dynamically counteract memory decay; (iii) we provide analytical solutions to the MQAR associative recall task using the Mamba architecture with different mixers -- Mamba, Mamba-2, and S4D. We demonstrate the tightness of our theoretical constructions with empirical results on concrete tasks. Our findings offer a mechanistic understanding of Mamba and reveal opportunities for improvement.
- **Summary**: This paper provides a mechanistic understanding of input selectivity in Mamba, a state-space model that has shown promising results as an alternative to Transformers. The authors investigate Mamba's input selectivity and its impact on function approximation power, long-term memorization, and associative recall capabilities. Their contributions include: (i) proving that Mamba's S6 layer can represent projections onto Haar wavelets, giving it an advantage over Diagonal SSMs (S4D) in approximating discontinuous functions; (ii) demonstrating how the S6 layer can dynamically counteract memory decay; and (iii) providing analytical solutions for the Multiple-Query Associative Recall (MQAR) task using Mamba, Mamba-2, and S4D architectures. The authors also complement their theoretical findings with empirical results on concrete tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in providing a more structural and mechanistic explanation for Mamba's superior performance compared to its SSM predecessors, specifically focusing on the role of input selectivity. While previous work has noted the improved expressivity of Mamba's input-dependent parameters, this paper goes further by linking this expressivity to specific function classes (Haar wavelets) and demonstrating its impact on long-term memory and associative recall. Deriving analytical solutions for MQAR task within Mamba architectures is also a novel contribution. The sensitivity analysis of S6 layer's memory decay, while confirming existing intuitions, is formalized and connected to task performance, providing a deeper understanding of the model's behavior. It is also novel in that it shows a simple version of S6 with certain conditions met can solve the keep inth task.

**Significance:** The paper has potential significance because a deeper understanding of Mamba's architecture can lead to further improvements and applications. By demystifying the role of input selectivity and highlighting its interaction with other components, the authors offer valuable insights for future research in state-space models. Their findings can inform the design of more efficient and effective SSMs, especially for tasks involving discontinuous signals, long-term dependencies, and associative recall. The theoretical results are supported with empirical validation, strengthening the impact of the work. The analytical solutions also provide a tool for researchers to benchmark the performance of new SSM variants.

**Strengths:**

*   Strong theoretical foundation: The paper provides rigorous proofs and analytical solutions.
*   Clear explanations: The authors clearly explain the role of input selectivity and its impact on various capabilities.
*   Empirical validation: The theoretical findings are supported by empirical results on concrete tasks.
*   Actionable insights: The paper offers actionable insights for improving Mamba and other SSMs.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited scope: While the paper provides a detailed analysis of input selectivity, it does not explore other aspects of Mamba's architecture, such as the gate branch.
*   Simplified models: The experiments use simplified Mamba architectures without convolution and gating, which may not fully capture the behavior of the complete model. Though this allows focus on the S6 layer, it could limit generalizability.
*   The results, while significant, could be viewed as incremental since they build upon existing knowledge of SSMs and Transformers.
*   The paper is focused on theoretical analysis and provides less insight regarding optimization and generalization in a more practical sense.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to understanding Mamba's architecture and provides valuable insights into the role of input selectivity. The combination of theoretical analysis, empirical validation, and actionable insights justifies a score above average. However, some limitations, such as the narrow scope and simplified models, hold the score back. The fact that the theoretical contributions are not revolutionary but, rather, incremental yet insightful advancements to the existing understanding of S6-based SSMs also influences the score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Attention-based Adversarial Robust Distillation in Radio Signal Classifications for Low-Power IoT Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11892v1)
- **Authors**: Lu Zhang, Sangarapillai Lambotharan, Gan Zheng, Guisheng Liao, Basil AsSadhan, Fabio Roli
- **Abstract**: Due to great success of transformers in many applications such as natural language processing and computer vision, transformers have been successfully applied in automatic modulation classification. We have shown that transformer-based radio signal classification is vulnerable to imperceptible and carefully crafted attacks called adversarial examples. Therefore, we propose a defense system against adversarial examples in transformer-based modulation classifications. Considering the need for computationally efficient architecture particularly for Internet of Things (IoT)-based applications or operation of devices in environment where power supply is limited, we propose a compact transformer for modulation classification. The advantages of robust training such as adversarial training in transformers may not be attainable in compact transformers. By demonstrating this, we propose a novel compact transformer that can enhance robustness in the presence of adversarial attacks. The new method is aimed at transferring the adversarial attention map from the robustly trained large transformer to a compact transformer. The proposed method outperforms the state-of-the-art techniques for the considered white-box scenarios including fast gradient method and projected gradient descent attacks. We have provided reasoning of the underlying working mechanisms and investigated the transferability of the adversarial examples between different architectures. The proposed method has the potential to protect the transformer from the transferability of adversarial examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of transformer-based automatic modulation classification (AMC) systems to adversarial attacks, particularly in low-power IoT devices. It proposes a novel defense mechanism called ATtention-based Adversarial Robustness Distillation (ATARD). ATARD distills adversarial robustness by transferring the adversarial attention map from a robustly trained large transformer network to a smaller, computationally efficient transformer suitable for resource-constrained IoT devices.  The method aims to protect the AMC system from the transferability of adversarial examples.  Experimental results demonstrate that ATARD outperforms state-of-the-art techniques (AT, ARD, IAD, AKD, and RSLAD) in white-box attack scenarios (FGM and PGD), and that the adversarial examples have robust transferability to other model architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach by focusing on transferring adversarial robustness in transformer-based AMC specifically using attention maps.  While knowledge distillation and adversarial training are established techniques, applying them to transformers for AMC, and particularly distilling *attention maps*, is a relatively fresh perspective. The idea of addressing adversarial attacks specifically in low-power IoT context is also significant.

* **Significance:**  The significance lies in addressing a critical challenge: deploying robust, secure machine learning in resource-constrained IoT environments. AMC is crucial for spectrum management, but its vulnerability to adversarial attacks can be exploited. Creating a computationally efficient defense allows for wider deployment of AMC in IoT, enhancing security and reliability of wireless communication.  It also provides potential counter measure for military applications, as depicted by the paper.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of adversarial vulnerability in AMC for IoT and provides a compelling motivation.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The ATARD methodology is clearly explained, with details on the transformer architecture, adversarial training process, and attention map transfer mechanism.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experimental setup is relatively rigorous, using standard datasets (RML2016.19a, RDL2021.12), and a variety of attacks (FGM, PGD), a range of PNR values, and comparisons to several relevant baseline defenses. Transferability experiments adds robustness to the evaluation.
    * **Performance Improvement:** The experimental results consistently show performance improvements of ATARD over baseline defenses, particularly at higher PNR values.
    * **Smoothness analysis:** Provided smoothness analysis is a crucial element that provides a theoretical view on top of experimental results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **White-Box Assumption:** The focus on white-box attacks is a limitation. While providing a worst-case evaluation of security, real-world adversaries often operate with limited knowledge. Expanding the evaluation to gray-box or black-box attacks would strengthen the paper.
    * **Dataset Limitations:** The results have strong reliance on datasets. Using only two data sets limits generalizabilty of the proposed method.
    * **Lack of ablation experiments:** The ATARD method uses adversarial attention map for robustness enhancement. But no results exist showing the level of impact of this particular attention map element in robustness.
    * **Specific IoT Deployment Details:** The paper focuses on IoT context but lacks detail on how ATARD would be specifically implemented and deployed within practical IoT constraints (e.g., energy consumption of ATARD).
